The control remained a second paper fin whose entire career consisted of not helping me feel special.

I maintained the effort.

No extra push.

No shoulder rise.

No change in posture.

Nothing moved.

Hessa called the stop.

I returned.

She checked target.

Control.

Marks.

Glass.

Then me.

"No observed response. Control unchanged."

"Far."

"Yes."

"Again."

"Yes."

That one hurt less than attempt forty-four.

I did not like what that said about expectation.

Hessa wrote the final line.

Forty-two: intermediate, small observed response away, control unchanged.

Forty-three: far, no observed response, control unchanged.

Forty-four: intermediate, no observed response, control unchanged.

Forty-five: far, no observed response, control unchanged.

Four clean attempts.

No control movement.

No apparatus disturbance.

No body change large enough to stop.

I read the list upside down from my chair.

"That is uglier."

"Good."

"You people need a new word."

"No."

"Last session intermediate was two for two."

"Yes."

"Today one for two."

"Yes."

"Far last time was one clean no-response and one contaminated attempt."

"Yes."

"Today two clean no-responses."

"Yes."

"So the difference is still there."

Hessa did not answer immediately.

That meant I had made the sentence too large.

I tried again.

"Intermediate has produced more clean observed responses than far under this setup."

"Yes."

"Across more than one session now."

"Yes."

"Intermediate also failed cleanly today."

"Yes."

"So intermediate response is not reliable."

"Correct."

"Far still has the earlier clean responses from other sessions."

"Correct."

"Today does not erase them."

"No."

"Today also does not strengthen far."

"Correct."

"No range."

"No range."

"Not generalized."

"Not generalized."

I leaned back.

The chair complained.

"Did reversing the order matter?"

"We do not know."

"Excellent."

"We now have one session beginning far and one beginning intermediate under the same four-condition structure."

"With different results."

"Yes."

"Which means?"

"It means we have those results."

I closed my eyes.

"One day I am going to make you say something satisfying."

"That would be poor supervision."

I opened them.

"Restrictions?"

"Unchanged."

"Independent draw?"

"No."

"Independent shaping?"

"No."

"Paper?"

"No."

"Coins?"

"No."

"Thread?"

"No."

"Larger objects?"

"No."

"External testing?"

"No."

"Practice?"

"No."

"Barrier?"

"No."

"Theatre?"

"No."

"Loopholes?"

"No."

"Good."

Hessa stacked the pages.

"That word finally useful?"

"No."

She checked my hand once more.

Then wrist.

Forearm.

Shoulder.

Right leg.

Residual limb.

No concerning change.

Thirty-three successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-nine supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Forty-five supervised external-effect attempts.

The numbers had grown.

My permissions had not.

This was becoming the most reliable result in magic.

At the door I stopped.

"Do you still have several questions?"

"Yes."

"More or fewer?"

"Different."

That was somehow worse.

"Next?"

"Not decided."

"Again?"

"Yes."

I looked back at the board.

Intermediate had moved once and failed once.

Far had failed twice.

The control had done absolutely nothing all afternoon and therefore had an excellent day.

I left.

Outside, the sky had clouded without rain.

Carrow was entering the part of afternoon where everybody wanted to finish one thing before dark and therefore blocked each other in the street.

I bought nothing.

This was partly discipline and mostly because I had already bought a meat pie.

At home, Lyssa was not there.

The table had acquired cloth again.

Not much.

A narrow folded piece of brown lay near the wall with three pins stuck through one corner. My chair remained free.

Selective occupation.

I approved.

I put Hessa's note beside the basin and cut bread.

My cuff caught on the knife handle.

I looked at the two loose threads.

They had become three.

Evidence accumulated.

I did not repair it.

That was genuine restraint.

Lyssa came in after dark carrying a small bundle of turnips and no garment.

She saw me.

Then the bread.

Then the note.

"Paper?"

"Once."

She put the turnips down.

"Out of how many?"

"Four external attempts."

She took off her outer layer.

"Same distance?"

"Two intermediate. Two far."

"Which moved?"

"One intermediate."

"The other intermediate?"

"No."

"Far?"

"No."

She nodded.

That was it.

I waited.

She started cutting a turnip.

"You have no larger conclusion?"

"I am hungry."

"Hessa would hate your methods."

"Hessa told you to eat."

I looked at the note by the basin.

"She has spread again."

Lyssa pushed the cut turnip toward me.

"Rules?"

"Same."

"Good."

There was that word.

I ate.

The turnip needed salt.

We had salt.

I added too much.

Lyssa ate one bite, looked at me, and moved the bowl farther from my hand.

No one recorded the result.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 234

## THE MAGISTRATE

The brown cloth was still on the table in the morning.

Three pins through one corner. Narrow fold. No note.

I looked at it long enough to confirm it had not become my business overnight, then ate the last turnip cold because I had ruined the hot version yesterday and consequences deserved continuity.

Lyssa had already gone. My shirt cuff had three loose threads, and I did not touch them. This was restraint of a kind Hessa would never properly appreciate because it involved no apparatus and very poor sewing.

I had no note from her anyway. More importantly, Jori had said tomorrow maybe two days ago, which had become today only through the ordinary cruelty of calendars.

I put on the damaged shirt, took my crutches, and went to see whether maybe had survived.

The side door at the theatre was open.

Inside, the magistrate's room had acquired windows.

Not real windows. That would have been reckless.

The painted wall from two days ago stood onstage with Jori's trim fixed around a broad blue rectangle. From the audience it looked like a respectable office window with pale light beyond it. From the wing I could see cream paint along one edge, green on another, and a narrow stripe of older blue at the back where the same timber had lived several previous lives.

The piece I had bumped against the wall carried a small cream mark near the bottom.

Nobody had repaired it.

The audience would never see it.

I found this deeply offensive.

Davin was kneeling beside a low rail with three short boards across his lap. Jori stood over him holding a longer one.

"That one fits," Davin said.

"It fits because I cut it."

"You cut it because I found the gap."

"You made the gap."

"History is written by carpenters."

Jori saw me.

"You're late."

I looked toward the empty house.

"For what?"

He pointed toward the stage.

"Magistrate."

I waited.

Davin looked up.

"Congratulations."

"On what?"

"Corruption."

That was how I learned I had a role.

Nessa found me before Teren did. She came out of the rear passage carrying a black coat and a bundle of pages.

"No flowers," she said.

"I wasn't worried."

"You should be. Marek knows where they are."

She handed me the coat.

It was shorter than the steward's coat from The Second Toast and heavier through the shoulders. The hem stopped high enough that it would not wrap around a crutch tip when I stood. Nessa checked that before I finished putting it on.

"Good."

"Did you choose this for my dignity?"

"For your legs."

"Less romantic."

"Also the other one smells like Pell."

From the stage Pell shouted, "I can hear you."

Nessa raised her voice.

"Then wash it."

Pell said something I could not make out.

Marek laughed.

The company was already busy enough that nobody explained why.

Teren arrived with a pencil tucked behind one ear and pointed to the pages in my hand.

"The Crooked Fence."

I looked down.

The cast list was short.

WIDOW: HARA

NEIGHBOR: MAREK

CLERK: PELL

MAGISTRATE: GREG

Below that, fifteen lines.

"Fifteen."

"Yes."

"I am advancing."

"You're sitting."

I looked at the stage.

A broad desk had been placed beneath the painted window. Behind it, one chair.

"Ah."

"You stand once."

"Demotion."

Teren ignored that.

"The play opens with you already there. Pell enters, then Hara. Marek comes after. You do not need to make the magistrate important."

"I am the magistrate."

"That's the trap."

He took the pages back long enough to tap one line near the middle.

"Listen to the dispute. Don't play the joke before they give it to you."

"Do I know what the dispute is?"

"You read the title."

"Crooked fence."

"Excellent scholarship."

He gave the pages back.

"Stage."

I had played men who delivered things, carried things, served things, borrowed things, guarded doors, stood in rooms, and once represented the entire concept of being a neighbor.

Magistrate felt suspiciously like employment with furniture.

For once, the job description appealed to me.

The set had more pieces than the painted wall suggested.

Desk. Chair. Clerk's stool. A narrow witness rail Davin and Jori were still arguing about. One rolled map. A wooden seal block that did not seal anything. Three folded petitions. A length of rope. A short painted fence panel that looked as though it had lost an argument with weather.

Nessa put the seal block on the desk.

"Do not hit the desk with it."

I looked at the block.

"Why would I?"

"Because every man who plays this part does."

"Tradition."

"Damage."

She placed a small square of dark leather beside my right hand.

"Hit that."

"How often?"

"Twice."

"Only twice?"

"In the play."

"After?"

"No."

Marek appeared beside us and reached for the block.

Nessa moved it without looking at him.

He stopped.

"I was inspecting justice."

"You can inspect it from there."

He leaned toward me.

"Corrupt."

"Apparently."

He smiled and went to find his fence.

I sat behind the desk.

The chair was solid and slightly too low.

My right foot rested flat. Left knee bent comfortably. I could keep both crutches behind the desk to my right without their tops showing above it if I angled them toward the wall. Standing would require one extra beat to reach them, but the script had enough room before the magistrate crossed to the witness rail.

Jori watched me try it once.

"Rail farther left."

Davin said, "It was farther left."

"It was crooked."

"It's a crooked fence play."

"The fence is crooked."

"Theme."

Jori moved the rail.

Not much. Maybe three inches.

That gave my right crutch a clean plant when I stood.

Teren did not comment.

We began.

Pell entered first as the Clerk carrying the petitions and a face suggesting he had already heard too much from everyone involved.

"Morning docket," he said.

My first line:

"How bad?"

Pell looked at the pages.

"Neighbors."

I looked up.

The company laughed.

Teren said, "Again."

"I did not write it."

"You enjoyed it."

"It is enjoyable."

"Again."

Second time I asked, "How bad?" like a man whose morning could still conceivably be saved.

Pell looked at the pages.

"Neighbors."

Better.

Hara entered before the laugh finished.

Her Widow carried the short fence panel under one arm.

Not a piece of paper.

The fence.

She dropped it in front of the desk.

"Evidence."

I looked at Teren.

He said, "Not you."

Right.

The Magistrate looked at the fence.

"That is a fence."

"It was."

"Has its condition changed?"

"It has moved."

Marek entered through the opposite side.

"It was moved."

Hara turned.

"By you."

"By weather."

"Weather does not own a hammer."

Marek held up both hands.

"Neither do I."

From backstage Davin said, "He does."

Teren turned his head.

Davin disappeared.

We kept going.

The dispute was simple enough to survive fifteen lines and complicated enough to occupy everybody else.

Hara's Widow claimed the fence between their yards had moved two feet into her side.

Marek's Neighbor claimed a storm had shifted the posts.

Hara claimed the posts had moved uphill.

Marek claimed water did strange things.

Pell's Clerk kept producing older descriptions of the property that contradicted both of them because the lane had once been wider, then narrower, then apparently measured by a man who wrote one number differently every time.

The Magistrate wanted one fact that stayed in place.

I understood the role.

At the first run, I did too much with that understanding.

I leaned forward through every ridiculous answer. Watched Hara. Watched Marek. Looked at Pell's papers as though I could force them into coherence through judicial attention.

Teren stopped us after five minutes.

"Greg."

"What?"

"Sit back."

I did.

"You're chasing them."

"I am hearing a case."

"The case comes to you."

I looked at the desk.

"That feels administratively optimistic."

Hara said, "He's right."

Teren pointed at her.

"Do not help him."

"I wasn't helping. The magistrate is desperate."

Marek leaned on the witness rail.

"It is his best quality."

The rail shifted.

Jori said, "Off."

Marek stepped away before Jori reached him, which saved everybody time.

Teren looked back at me.

"You don't need to catch every line. Let them bring the mess."

That was specific enough to use.

We reset.

The second run felt easier once I stopped trying to prove the Magistrate was listening.

Pell gave me the petitions.

I read one.

Hara placed the fence.

Marek blamed weather.

I let the argument happen in front of me instead of leaning into it.

The desk became useful.

It gave me somewhere to put my hands. Somewhere to look when two adults were shouting about an object shorter than my leg.

Pell's Clerk became funnier too, because I could glance at him only when the contradictions reached a level requiring professional support.

He never gave it.

At one point Hara said, "The fence moved uphill."

Marek said, "Water rises."

Pell looked at him.

I looked at Pell.

Pell looked back at his paper.

Nobody helped Marek.

The company laughed hard enough that Teren let the silence sit.

That was not my joke.

It worked partly because I did nothing.

I was beginning to understand why theatre kept assigning jobs to absence.

By midday, we had the routes.

The fence panel entered with Hara and left with nobody until the end.

The petitions stayed with Pell.

The rope appeared only in the second half, when Marek attempted to demonstrate how the storm had "pulled" the fence.

The demonstration did not help him.

The seal block stayed on its leather square unless I needed it.

Twice.

Nessa made me prove I remembered that.

"First?"

"After both start talking over Pell."

"Second?"

"Before judgment."

"Desk?"

"Never."

"Good."

She took the block and gave it to Marek.

He brightened.

"Justice."

"Take it to the table."

His face fell.

Temporary authority transferred.

Lunch happened in the aisle because the stage was still full of legal property.

Rinna had bread. Hara had two boiled eggs. Pell had something wrapped in leaves that smelled much better than my bread. Marek claimed he had already eaten and then stole half of Davin's apple.

Davin noticed after two bites were gone.

"That's mine."

Marek looked at the apple.

"Evidence unclear."

I held out my hand.

"Bring it before the court."

Davin gave me the apple.

Marek said, "You cannot be impartial. You know the victim."

I took a bite.

"Case dismissed."

Hara laughed into her egg.

Teren, from three rows away, said, "Do not improve the play."

I swallowed.

"Rehearsal never stops."

Rinna passed behind us carrying a small stack of tickets, stopped long enough to steal a piece of Hara's bread, and kept moving before Hara noticed which piece she had taken.

Hara noticed.

"That was the good side."

Rinna bit it.

"Still is."

She kept walking.

After lunch, Jori and Davin replaced the witness rail with a different one because the first had developed a wobble that was not funny enough to justify keeping.

Davin carried it away.

"I said it was crooked."

Jori said, "It was loose."

"Crooked things become loose."

"That is not carpentry."

"It is philosophy."

"Take it outside."

Davin did.

I watched the new rail go down exactly three inches farther left than the first.

No one measured from my crutch.

They measured from the desk.

The result still worked.

That was better.

The afternoon run was cleaner.

My one standing cross worked without thought. Left crutch, right crutch, right foot, up. Around the desk. Two plants to the rail. Stop. The black coat stayed clear. Nessa had been right about the length.

Hara changed one small thing during the run.

When she said the fence had moved uphill, she stopped pointing at Marek and pointed instead at the fence itself, as though the fence had participated in the crime.

Teren let her keep it.

Marek changed nothing.

Pell changed even less.

That was another thing I was learning. Experienced actors did not always decorate a scene when they found something that worked. Sometimes they protected it.

The house filled for the early performance.

Not full.

Enough.

Voices became a low uneven layer beyond the curtain. Someone coughed repeatedly in the third row. A child was told twice not to climb on a bench. Rinna moved through the front with tickets. Backstage, Nessa checked the fence panel for a loose nail that was paint, checked Marek's rope, checked Pell's petitions, then came to me.

"Block?"

"Leather."

"Twice?"

"Twice."

"Desk?"

"Never."

She nodded and moved on.

I was already seated when the curtain opened.

That was strange.

No entrance.

No moment of stepping from backstage into light.

One second I was Greg behind a desk listening to the audience settle.

Then the curtain moved and I was a magistrate who had already been at work before any of them arrived.

Pell came in.

"Morning docket."

"How bad?"

He looked at the petitions.

"Neighbors."

The laugh was better with people.

Not huge.

Warm.

Hara entered carrying the fence.

She dropped it.

"Evidence."

The audience laughed again because apparently bringing an entire fence into a magistrate's room was funny even before anyone explained it.

Good play.

We moved.

The first half went cleanly.

Marek's weather defense got a laugh.

Hara's hammer answer got a larger one.

Pell read three measurements from three years and made each sound more exhausted than the last.

I sat back.

Let them bring the mess.

When both Hara and Marek began talking over Pell, I picked up the seal block and struck the leather square once.

A hard wooden knock.

They stopped.

Correct.

"One at a time."

Written line.

We continued.

Then came the rope.

Marek tied one end around the fence panel and held the other.

"The wind catches here."

Hara said, "Wind has hands now?"

"Enough wind does."

"Then arrest the wind."

A laugh.

Marek pulled the rope.

The fence panel slid six inches across the floor.

He stopped.

Hara looked at it.

The audience understood before she spoke.

"So it can be moved."

That laugh was bigger.

Marek stared at the fence as though betrayed by physical law.

Pell lowered his petitions.

I had a line.

"Your demonstration has become hostile."

I got it out without smiling.

Small laugh.

The play rolled forward.

Then I made the mistake.

It happened because Pell began a line with the same words he used later.

"Before judgment..."

I knew the second strike came before judgment.

My hand moved.

The block hit leather.

Knock.

Pell stopped.

Not because he was supposed to.

Because I had just cut his sentence in half.

For less than a second the stage became very clear.

My hand on the block.

Pell looking at me.

Hara looking at Pell.

Marek still holding the rope.

The audience waiting.

I had ended something early.

Hara turned to me.

Her Widow said, perfectly calm, "If judgment has arrived before the clerk finishes, I would like to know whether I am required to stay for it."

The audience laughed.

Not enormous.

Enough to reopen the room.

Pell looked down at his petition.

"Apparently not."

Another laugh.

Teren would murder us both later.

Marek lifted the rope.

"Can I go too?"

Hara snapped, "You can take your weather."

Bigger.

The scene was alive again.

Pell restarted from the part I had murdered.

"Before judgment, the court has one older survey."

This time I left the block alone.

The Magistrate listened.

The survey described the property line using a pear tree that no longer existed.

Hara said it had died.

Marek said she had cut it down.

Hara said it was her tree.

Marek said that was exactly the question.

The audience liked that.

I did too.

I kept my face.

Then came the strongest beat.

Pell read the final note.

"The disputed strip includes responsibility for the drainage ditch along the north wall."

Silence.

Hara's Widow had spent the entire play fighting for two feet of land.

She looked at Pell.

Then at the fence.

Then at Marek.

"His."

The audience broke.

Marek said, "You cannot surrender it now."

Hara picked up her end of the fence.

"I have spent an hour proving your ownership."

Another wave.

Pell had to wait.

Hara did not smile.

She turned to me.

"Magistrate?"

My written line was, "You are withdrawing the claim?"

She said, "In the interest of justice."

That finished the room.

I waited.

Not because Teren had told me to.

Because I could not speak over them.

When the laughter fell enough, I said, "Remarkable how quickly justice improves drainage."

A decent laugh.

Not Hara's.

Good.

I struck the block a second time.

Correct time.

The leather square did its job.

"Claim withdrawn."

Marek objected.

Pell collected papers.

Hara carried the crooked fence out with the satisfied speed of a woman abandoning property she had spent twenty minutes demanding.

My standing cross came near the end.

I reached the crutches, stood, moved to the rail, and delivered the final exchange without catching coat, desk, stool, rope, Marek, or justice.

The curtain came down.

Backstage noise arrived immediately.

Nessa took the block from me.

"Two."

I looked at her.

"Three."

"Yes."

"Teren?"

"Later."

She went after the fence.

Pell came through behind her still holding the petitions.

"You killed me."

"I heard 'before judgment.'"

"You heard half a sentence."

"Apparently."

Hara passed us.

"Efficient court."

"I apologize."

"No."

She kept walking.

I looked at Pell.

"Was that no to the apology?"

"Probably."

Teren found me after the second short piece.

I had no role in it. I spent the time helping Nessa reset the magistrate room and then watching from the wing while Hara played a woman trying to return a horse she had never purchased.

The horse was represented by a halter and the confidence of the cast.

It worked disturbingly well.

When the audience left, the company became labor again.

Teren waited until I had carried the clerk's stool toward storage.

"Greg."

I stopped.

"Block."

"Early."

"Why?"

"Pell said 'before judgment.'"

"Which was not your cue."

"No."

"Your cue was after the survey."

"Yes."

He nodded.

That was apparently the whole note.

I said, "Hara fixed it."

"Hara continued."

Important distinction.

I accepted it.

"Again tomorrow?" I asked.

"The play?"

"Magistrate."

Teren looked toward Rinna's table.

"Maybe."

Jori had infected everyone.

"Specific."

"If we pair it again."

That was not a promise.

Good.

He moved on to tell Marek that the rope belonged in the rope box, not around the witness rail.

Marek said he was preserving the evidence.

Nobody cared.

Rinna paid me one copper.

Normal theatre money.

I put it away.

The stage was already changing before I left. Jori and Davin took the magistrate desk toward the rear. The blue-trimmed wall stayed because tomorrow's first piece apparently used the same room as a merchant's office.

Davin pointed at the window.

"Versatile."

Jori said, "It's a window."

"Exactly."

The little cream mark I had made two days earlier disappeared into the wing.

Still unrepaired.

Still irrelevant.

I stopped being offended.

Mostly.

Outside, evening had settled over Carrow with the smell of cooking oil, wet stone, and somebody burning something that had probably once been dinner.

My shoulders felt ordinary.

Right hand fine.

The one standing cross had cost less than carrying flowers all day.

The Magistrate had been the easiest physical role I had played in weeks and one of the harder ones to leave alone.

I had hit the block early.

Pell had survived.

Hara had made the room hers.

Theatre.

At home, the brown cloth was still near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

I did not inspect it.

Lyssa was sitting at the table eating bread with something spread on it that smelled sharp enough to improve the room.

She looked at the black coat folded over my arm.

"Role?"

"Magistrate."

She chewed.

"Did they know?"

"Eventually."

"How many people did you punish?"

"None."

"Poor magistrate."

"I did interrupt the clerk."

"On purpose?"

"No."

"Better."

I hung the coat over the chair so I would remember to return it tomorrow.

Lyssa looked at it.

"That yours?"

"Theatre."

"Then don't forget it."

"I was hoping to begin a private legal practice."

"In that?"

She looked at the coat again.

"No."

There was no argument available against the tone.

I sat.

She pushed the bread toward me.

I took some.

After a while she asked, "Good show?"

"Hara won two feet of land."

Lyssa waited.

"Then learned it came with a drainage ditch and gave it back."

She laughed once.

"Smart."

"Her character spent the whole play proving it belonged to her."

"Then smarter late."

I considered explaining the audience timing.

I did not.

The brown cloth stayed folded near the wall, and my cuff still had three loose threads.

Lyssa took the better piece of bread before I could reach it.

I started to object.

She had already bitten it.

I took the other piece.

It had more of the sharp spread on it anyway.

## Chapter 235 — THE COUNTERSIGN

The black coat was still on the chair when I woke.

That made it my first obligation.

Not debt.

Not magic.

Not even breakfast, although breakfast argued.

The coat belonged to the theatre, and I had brought it home because apparently being trusted with clothing meant eventually becoming responsible for remembering clothing.

Lyssa was already gone.

The brown cloth remained near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My cuff had three loose threads.

Same failure.

The room had achieved continuity without me.

I ate bread, drank water, put on my own coat, then folded the theatre coat over my left forearm.

That lasted until I picked up the crutches.

I looked at the coat.

The coat looked expensive enough to resent being dragged through Carrow.

I folded it smaller and tucked it under my right arm instead.

That made the first six steps annoying.

By the bottom of the stairs I had found a better arrangement.

Carrow was cool and damp from night rain. Water sat in the broken places between stones and made every cart wheel choose between mud and argument. A delivery boy ran past me with three covered baskets stacked high enough that he could not see anyone below shoulder level.

I moved.

He did not.

Good system.

The theatre side door was open.

I went in, returned the coat to Nessa, and discovered that my entire theatrical importance for the morning consisted of not losing it.

She checked the hem.

Not me.

The hem.

"Fine."

"I also survived."

"I saw."

"You weren't there when I walked home."

"The coat was."

She hung it.

Behind her, yesterday's magistrate room had already become a merchant's office.

The blue-trimmed window stayed.

Desk turned.

Witness rail gone.

A tall shelf had appeared where Hara had surrendered two feet of drainage responsibility to justice.

Davin was standing on a stool holding the shelf while Jori looked at its feet.

"Left."

Davin moved it left.

"Other left."

Davin looked down.

"You have created too many."

Jori pointed.

Davin moved the shelf.

I watched long enough to see it stop wobbling.

Nobody handed me anything.

That was probably a sign.

Rinna crossed the aisle with a stack of folded notices.

"Working today?"

"Apparently not here."

"Tomorrow maybe."

That was not a call.

It was also not nothing.

I nodded.

She kept moving.

I left before somebody discovered a stool that needed carrying.

Outside, I stood under the theatre awning and considered the day.

I had one copper from yesterday.

I also had a debt that did not become smaller through admiration.

Vale's office was not far.

Outgoing bundles happened most days when needed.

That did not mean there were bundles today.

It did mean I knew where the door was.

Good enough.

I went.

The front room at Antonius Vale's office had acquired two people before I arrived.

One was an older man with a red scarf who was arguing quietly about a charge.

The other was a woman holding a folded receipt between two fingers like it had personally insulted her.

Neither looked happy enough to be staff.

I waited near the wall.

The ink-fingered clerk saw me after finishing with the woman.

"Bundles aren't ready."

"All right."

He looked at me.

I looked at him.

That had been the entire plan.

He glanced toward the rear room.

"Can you read small?"

"Usually."

"That isn't an answer."

"Yes."

"Sit."

Work.

A chair appeared because another clerk pushed one away from a table with his foot.

I moved it myself.

The task was not bundles.

A stack of narrow account slips sat beside a thicker book opened flat under a stone weight. Each slip had a house mark, a name, a date, an amount, and one or more short marks along the lower edge. The book carried the same information in rows, except the rows had more space and worse handwriting.

The ink-fingered clerk put one slip beside the book.

"Returned payment acknowledgments."

I waited.

He tapped the slip.

"Runner brings this back after money is received. We match it to the account line. If name, house, date, and amount agree, you put a small cross here."

He showed me a blank margin beside the row.

"Then?"

"Then I check your cross."

"Efficient."

"It is if you stop talking."

He put another slip down.

"If anything disagrees, no cross. Turn the slip sideways."

"Which disagreement matters?"

"Any."

"Spelling?"

"If you can tell it's the same name, no."

"Date?"

"Yes."

"Amount?"

He looked at me.

"Especially amount."

"House mark?"

"Yes."

"Runner mark?"

"No."

I pointed to one of the lower marks.

"That?"

"Runner."

Another.

"Received by."

Another.

"Late."

"Does late matter?"

"Not for matching."

I nodded.

Four things.

Name.

House.

Date.

Amount.

Everything else could be interesting somewhere else.

I had learned enough recently to distrust interesting.

He gave me six slips.

"Do those."

I started.

The first matched.

Name close enough.

House mark exact.

Date exact.

Amount exact.

Cross.

Second matched.

Third had a date one day later than the book.

I turned it sideways.

Fourth had the right name and date but a different house mark.

Sideways.

Fifth matched.

Sixth had the same amount written two different ways because one number in the book had been corrected over itself.

I stared at it.

The clerk noticed.

"Problem?"

"Maybe."

He came over.

I showed him.

The slip said seven.

The book had once said one thing and now said something that could have been seven if the writer had been attacked during the upper stroke.

The clerk looked at the line above.

Then the line below.

Then the account name.

"Match."

"How?"

"Correction mark."

He pointed to a tiny hook beside the number.

I had thought it was an ink scratch.

"That means the corrected figure is accepted?"

"Here, yes."

"Everywhere?"

"No."

Good.

Of course not.

He took my sideways date slip.

"Right."

Then the house mark.

"Right."

Then my uncertain sixth.

"You should have turned this."

"You just said it matches."

"I know. You didn't."

I looked at him.

He tapped the hook.

"You didn't know the correction mark."

"So uncertainty goes sideways too."

"Yes."

That had not been in the first explanation.

Apparently it had not needed to be until I found some.

He gave me twelve more.

The work was boring in a way I liked.

Not pleasant exactly.

Contained.

The slip either agreed with the line or it did not.

When I knew, cross.

When I did not, sideways.

The difficulty was keeping my eyes from following every mark.

Some accounts had little notes in the book.

Some had lines through old figures.

Some had initials.

One had a dark dot that reminded me of the transferred collection mark from the bundle work.

I did not investigate it.

It was not one of my four things.

Progress.

After twenty slips my right leg wanted a different angle.

I moved my foot.

After thirty, my shoulders wanted me to stop leaning over the book like a man trying to enter it.

I sat back.

The clerk noticed that too.

He noticed everything that cost him time.

"Break if you need one."

"I moved."

"That isn't a break."

"I'm aware."

"Are you?"

I took a break.

I stood with both crutches and walked to the front water jug. The older man in the red scarf was gone. The insulted-receipt woman was also gone. A new man stood at the counter asking whether Antonius was in.

He was told no.

Antonius walked through the rear doorway while the clerk was saying it.

The man at the counter looked at him.

Antonius looked at the clerk.

The clerk did not change expression.

"Not for him," he said.

Antonius kept walking.

The man at the counter reconsidered several things about language.

I drank water.

When I returned, the ink-fingered clerk had added another stack.

"Encouraging."

"You came for work."

"I was hoping for the amount where work becomes decorative."

"Wrong office."

I sat.

The next batch included a slip with the right name, right house, right date, and wrong amount.

Not close.

Not a corrected number.

Wrong.

Sideways.

Another had the right amount and date but the house mark was missing one stroke.

I turned it sideways.

The clerk later checked it and put it back in the match pile.

"Same house."

"Missing stroke."

"Runner drew it badly."

"How do you know?"

He pointed to the name.

Then to the account line.

Then to a second tiny mark beside the house symbol.

"Rear court."

I had seen the tiny mark.

I had not known what it meant.

"That makes the house mark enough?"

"With the name."

"So house marks can be approximate."

"No."

I waited.

He waited.

"That one can."

"Because of the rear court mark."

"Yes."

"Which I don't know."

"Now you do."

I turned the next questionable slip sideways anyway.

He smiled.

Not kindly.

"Learning."

"Slowly."

"Safer."

That sounded like Hessa.

I refused to think about why.

By midday, I had matched enough acknowledgments that the first book closed and a second replaced it.

The second book was older.

The paper had gone soft at the corners. The ink changed color halfway down some pages. House marks were drawn with more confidence and less consistency.

The clerk put his hand on it before I started.

"Older accounts. Don't assume the same mark means the same thing."

I looked at him.

"Why would anyone design that?"

"No one designed it."

There it was.

The explanation for most systems.

He opened to a page.

"This district changed marks after the lane numbers changed."

"When?"

"Before you."

"Useful date."

"It is to me."

He showed me two similar symbols.

"This one is old east court. This one is current east court."

"They're the same."

"No."

He pointed.

One had a short line inside the angle.

I leaned closer.

"That's hostile."

"That's bookkeeping."

"Same field."

He gave me eight slips from the older set.

I went slower.

Two matched.

One had an old mark on the slip and a new mark in the book.

I turned it sideways.

One had a name I could barely read.

Sideways.

One matched everything except the amount was one copper lower.

Sideways.

The clerk checked that one twice.

Then he took it to the experienced clerk at the far table.

They spoke quietly.

The experienced clerk came over, read the slip, read the line, and turned back three pages.

He found an earlier entry.

"Partial."

The ink-fingered clerk frowned.

"Not marked."

"No."

They looked at the returned slip again.

I stayed out of it.

Eventually the experienced clerk took a pencil and made a small notation beside the account line.

"Runner collected the remainder. Earlier payment wasn't carried forward."

The ink-fingered clerk looked at me.

"Sideways was right."

"I enjoy being right through refusal."

"Don't get used to it."

The mistake was theirs.

Not dramatic.

One earlier payment had failed to carry into the current line, so the returned amount looked short when it was actually the remainder.

If I had crossed it, someone would probably have found the problem later.

Probably.

That was the thing about offices.

A great deal of civilization seemed to be one person making a mark so another person could discover whether the first person had made the correct mark.

The experienced clerk fixed the earlier line.

The slip returned to the match pile.

Nobody applauded.

I liked that too.

Lunch was bread with a piece of hard cheese I bought from a woman outside.

I ate on the low stone edge beside the office because the chair had become an enemy through repetition.

A cart rolled past carrying bundled reeds.

Two boys argued over whose turn it was to pull a smaller handcart.

A dog slept under it while they argued.

Neither boy had noticed.

I considered warning them.

The dog opened one eye.

It had made its own decisions.

I ate my cheese.

When I went back inside, Antonius was at the rear table.

He had a stack of papers to his left and nothing to his right.

The nothing was shrinking.

He looked at me once.

"Useful?"

The ink-fingered clerk answered.

"Mostly."

I said, "Cruel."

Antonius looked at the matched stack.

"Mostly is billable."

"To whom?"

"Not you."

Good answer.

I returned to the book.

The afternoon became more complicated because the acknowledgments were not all from the same district.

The clerk separated them before handing them to me.

I learned three marks.

Forgot one.

Asked again.

He told me again without pretending I had not already asked.

That was a kind of mercy.

I made one actual mistake.

A woman's surname on the slip had an extra letter.

The house, date, and amount matched.

I crossed it.

The clerk uncrossed it.

"Wrong account."

"Same house."

"Two sisters."

He turned the book slightly and showed me another line three entries down.

Same house mark.

Almost same name.

Same date.

Different amount.

The returned slip matched the lower line.

I had matched the first familiar-looking name instead of checking the full page.

"Right."

"Don't search until you find something that fits."

"I wasn't."

He looked at my cross.

"I was."

"Yes."

I moved the slip.

The mistake had not gone anywhere.

That was why he checked my crosses.

I appreciated the system more when it was protecting everyone from me.

For the rest of the stack, I read the full name first.

Not the shape of it.

The letters.

Late in the afternoon, Antonius came over.

Not to inspect me.

He wanted the book.

The clerk handed it to him.

Antonius looked at the completed pages.

"How many?"

The clerk gave him a number.

I did not count along.

Antonius looked at the sideways stack.

"Problems?"

"Three real. Two unclear. One ours."

"Resolved?"

"Four. Two waiting."

Antonius nodded.

Then he looked at me.

"You came without being sent."

"Yes."

"Why?"

I considered saying debt.

That was true.

It was also obvious.

"Rinna didn't need me."

"Today."

"Today."

He glanced at my crutches.

"Any trouble with the new tip?"

"No."

"Good."

He took the book.

That was apparently the entire medical examination.

I approved.

"Tomorrow?" I asked.

Antonius looked at the clerk.

The clerk said, "Maybe bundles. Maybe nothing."

Antonius looked back at me.

"Then maybe."

No job.

No schedule.

No promise.

Also no dismissal.

I was getting very good at employment that existed only when the room happened to contain something I could do.

Antonius recorded the day's credit before I left.

I saw the line added.

He turned the book enough for me to read the balance.

I did.

Then he closed it.

Still there.

Smaller than before.

Not small.

Outside, the light had gone yellow against the upper walls.

I had spent most of the day sitting and still felt worked.

Different work.

Eyes.

Back.

Right leg from holding one position too long.

Hands from paper, not weight.

My shoulders were better than yesterday.

My patience had suffered more.

I stopped at the market and bought onions.

The same woman from before looked at me.

"Faster today?"

"I know how many."

"Growth."

"Two."

She gave me three.

I looked at them.

She looked at me.

"That is three."

"You're improving."

"At what?"

"Counting before you stand here."

I paid for three onions.

Apparently recognition had economic consequences.

On the way home I passed the theatre.

I did not go in.

The side door was open.

Someone inside shouted for a rope.

Someone else shouted that the rope was already there.

Then someone shouted, "The other rope."

Theatre continued successfully without judicial supervision.

At home, Lyssa was there.

The brown cloth was still near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

Lyssa sat at the table with a dark sleeve turned inside out across her lap.

Different work.

She looked at the onions.

"Three?"

"I was given an opportunity."

"You bought too many."

"I was manipulated."

"By onions?"

"Their representative."

She held out a hand.

I gave her one.

She inspected it.

"Fine."

"Thank you."

"Not you."

Of course.

I put the other two down.

My cuff still had three loose threads.

Lyssa saw me look at them.

She did not.

Good.

I sat across from her.

"Vale?" she asked.

"Yes."

"Work?"

"Yes."

"What?"

"Matching returned payment slips to account lines."

She waited.

"I was wrong once."

"Only once?"

"One confirmed time."

"Better."

"That is not supportive."

"It is accurate."

She pulled a needle through the dark sleeve.

I told her about the two sisters with nearly the same name.

She laughed at exactly the part where the clerk showed me my own cross.

Not the bookkeeping.

My humiliation.

Reasonable.

"Did you get paid?"

"Credit."

She nodded.

That was enough.

No balance.

No lecture.

No plan.

I picked up one onion and rolled it beneath my palm.

Lyssa looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You look like you're testing it."

I stopped.

"I am not."

"Good."

I moved the onion to the middle of the table.

Farther from me.

She watched that.

Then went back to the sleeve.

I ate the last of the bread while she worked.

After a while she said, "Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

"Theatre?"

"Maybe."

"Vale?"

"Maybe."

She looked up.

"Busy."

"Extremely."

Neither maybe belonged to me yet.

That was all right.

The onion stayed exactly where I put it.

So did I.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 236

## THE SEAT

The dark sleeve was gone in the morning.

The brown cloth was not.

Same fold.

Same three pins.

At this point it had become the most stable object in the apartment, which was offensive because my left crutch had required repair and the cloth had apparently achieved immortality through refusing to explain itself.

Lyssa was already out.

No note.

No customer.

No Hessa.

No message from Vale.

My cuff still had three loose threads.

The world was preserving several things I had not asked it to preserve.

I ate one of yesterday's onions with bread because buying three had consequences.

This was not a good breakfast.

It was a principled breakfast.

By the time I finished, I had decided that Rinna's "Tomorrow maybe" still did not count as a call.

It did, however, count as a reason to walk past the theatre before I invented a worse use for the morning.

That distinction felt sound.

Carrow was dry after yesterday's damp, with enough sun on the upper walls to make the lower streets look colder by comparison. A man swept dust out of his doorway and into his neighbor's. The neighbor watched from a chair.

Neither spoke.

Long war.

I respected the discipline.

The theatre side door was open when I reached it.

Nobody greeted me.

Davin told me I was in the road before I had both crutches inside.

Better.

I stepped inside and nearly walked into a painted shelf.

Not a real shelf.

Yesterday it had been a real stage shelf in the merchant's office.

Today it was on its side in the passage with one leg removed.

Davin sat beside it with a wooden peg between his teeth.

He took the peg out.

"You're in the road."

"I just arrived."

"Still."

I moved.

Jori came through carrying the missing leg.

He looked at me, then at the shelf, then at Davin.

"Other side."

Davin pointed at me.

"Road."

Jori pointed the other direction.

I moved again.

No one explained what they were doing.

The shelf leg went back on.

Davin pushed.

Jori watched the joint.

The shelf did not wobble.

"Good," Jori said.

Davin looked at me.

"You witnessed excellence."

"I witnessed furniture."

"History will decide."

Rinna found me before history did.

She had a short list in one hand and a piece of chalk in the other.

"You came."

"Maybe survived."

She looked at the list.

"Can you work?"

"Probably."

"Strong answer."

She pointed toward the stage.

"Strike the office. Keep the blue window. Desk goes rear. Shelf to store. Two benches out. Then Nessa has a table."

"One table?"

"Ask Nessa."

"I was afraid of that."

Rinna handed me the chalk.

I looked at it.

"What is this for?"

"Jori."

Jori, behind me, said, "Mine."

I gave it to him.

Important contribution completed.

The merchant's office was already half gone.

The desk had been turned sideways near the rear passage. The witness rail from the Magistrate had vanished yesterday. The blue-trimmed window remained because apparently painted architecture had better employment security than actors.

Hara was rolling a painted wall section with Pell.

Marek held one end of a long canvas and was contributing mostly opinions.

"Lower."

Pell said, "You lower."

"I am lower."

"You are talking."

"Voice comes from here."

He pointed at his chest.

Hara said, "Then lower that."

I took the loose end before the argument acquired furniture.

Marek looked at me.

"Interference."

"Labor."

"Less interesting."

We rolled the canvas.

Marek actually kept tension correctly once we started, which meant the roll stayed straight.

Hara tied it.

"Store two."

I knew where store two was.

No one gave directions.

That was useful because carrying one end of a rolled canvas while using crutches required enough attention without a lecture.

Marek took the other end.

We moved through the rear passage.

"Door," I said.

He turned his end slightly before we reached it.

The roll cleared.

No flowers.

No lilies.

No education.

Just a door.

We put it on the correct rack.

Marek checked the label before letting go.

I noticed.

I did not tell him.

That seemed kinder.

Back onstage, Nessa had found her table.

Actually three tables.

One was narrow and scarred.

One was square with a repaired corner.

One was a low thing that looked designed to hurt shins.

She stood in front of them.

"Not that one."

I had not spoken.

"Which one?"

She pointed at the low table.

"Why?"

"Because Marek already hit it."

From behind the curtain, Marek said, "Once."

Nessa raised her voice.

"Twice."

"One event."

I looked at the table.

"Strong evidence."

"Take the square one."

Pell laughed.

Marek did not.

The square table needed two people because it was awkward rather than heavy.

I took the near side.

Pell took the far.

My crutches became the problem.

I could carry the table or use both crutches properly, not both at once.

Pell looked at the distance.

"Slide?"

The floor was clear.

"Slide."

We lifted enough to get the repaired corner over a seam, then pushed the table on its feet toward stage left.

Jori watched once, decided the floor would survive, and returned to the shelf.

No speech.

No special method.

Just not doing the stupid version.

The table reached its mark.

Nessa moved it four inches.

Of course she did.

"What is it for?"

"Tonight."

"Play?"

"Cards."

I looked at her.

She was already walking away.

Apparently theatre furniture had private lives too.

My first mistake arrived disguised as familiarity.

Two black benches sat near the wing.

One had been in the funeral room. The other had appeared sometime after I stopped paying attention to bench genealogy.

Rinna pointed.

"That one rear."

I took the nearer bench.

It was lighter than I expected, which should have been information.

I got it halfway to the rear passage before Nessa came around the curtain.

"Why?"

I stopped.

"Rinna."

Nessa looked past me.

Rinna was pointing at the other bench now.

Of course.

I looked down at mine.

"What is this one?"

"Cards."

"Tonight."

"Yes."

"So I have stolen the future."

"You moved a bench."

"Less dramatic."

"Put it back."

I did.

The correct bench had a shallow split under one end and a chalk X on the back rail that I had not seen because I had chosen by position instead of identification.

Jori helped me rotate it through the passage.

"X," he said.

"I know now."

"Good."

That was the entire correction.

I put the wrong bench back exactly where it had been and the correct one in the rear.

Nothing collapsed.

Nobody applauded.

This was probably how most competence improved.

The morning became that kind of work.

Not dramatic.

Not difficult enough to become a story inside the story.

Benches out.

Desk rear.

A box of rolled maps moved from under the stair because somebody needed the stair.

Two cracked practice swords returned to a rack.

A painted jug went to Nessa's table, then off Nessa's table after she discovered it leaked.

Davin repaired one bench foot while Jori worked on the shelf.

Pell carried three folded costumes upstairs.

Hara disappeared for twenty minutes and returned with nails.

Nobody asked where she got them.

Rinna crossed the room often enough that I stopped assuming each crossing involved me.

Once she pointed at a bench.

I moved it.

Once she pointed at Marek.

I did not.

That seemed outside my authority.

By midday the stage no longer looked like a merchant's office.

It did not look like anything.

Which was apparently the first step toward looking like something else.

I sat on the edge of the house aisle and drank water.

My shoulders felt used.

Right hand normal.

Right leg a little tired from repeated short stands and starts.

Residual limb comfortable.

Nothing interesting.

Good.

Hara sat two seats away and began eating bread without asking whether I wanted conversation.

Also good.

Pell came down the aisle carrying a cup.

"Done?"

Hara said, "No."

He looked at me.

"Done?"

"I do not know."

Nessa, from the stage, said, "No."

Pell sat.

"Democracy."

We ate anyway.

Marek arrived with no food.

This was suspicious.

Hara looked at him.

"No."

"I haven't asked."

"No."

"I was going to sit."

"Then sit."

He sat one row behind us.

Thirty seconds later Pell lost half his bread.

"How?"

Marek was chewing.

Pell stared at him.

"You were behind me."

"Angles."

I looked toward the stage.

Jori and Davin were still working.

Davin had the shelf upright now.

Jori pushed the top once.

It held.

Davin opened both hands.

"Furniture."

Jori said, "Shelf."

"Recognition is difficult for you."

Nessa passed them carrying the leaking jug.

Davin pointed.

"Not furniture."

"Excellent," she said.

He looked pleased anyway.

Rinna finally called a stop in the middle of the afternoon.

Not a formal stop.

She looked at the stage, looked at the list, crossed off one thing, and said, "Enough."

Everyone seemed to understand this better than I did.

I stood.

"What now?"

Hara looked at me.

"Food."

"I just ate."

"That was hours ago."

It had not been hours.

It had been enough hours for Hara.

Pell was already collecting his coat.

Marek had vanished.

"Nessa?" I asked.

Hara said, "If she comes."

That was apparently the plan.

There was no plan.

I considered going home.

Not because I needed to.

Because going home after work was what the day usually did.

Rinna came down the aisle with the list folded into her pocket.

"You coming?"

"Where?"

"Food."

Everyone had apparently joined the same conspiracy independently.

"Is this a company requirement?"

"No."

"Paid?"

"No."

"Then your recruitment method is poor."

Rinna walked past me.

"Come or don't."

Strong institution.

I went.

The cookshop was two streets over and half below street level, with six long benches, three narrow tables, and a kitchen window that produced bowls faster than I understood.

I had passed it before.

I had never gone in.

Hara entered first.

Pell behind her.

Rinna behind Pell.

I came through the door and found Marek already seated.

Of course.

"How?"

He lifted a bowl.

"Commitment."

The room smelled like onions, broth, pepper, and something fried in fat.

I approved immediately.

There was one open place on the end bench.

Not one open seat.

One place.

A bowl space.

A person space.

Hara had her coat folded on the bench beside her.

When I reached the table, she moved it to the floor without looking at me.

That was the entire invitation.

I sat.

My crutches fit against the wall behind the bench if I angled them together.

Pell shifted his feet before I asked.

Marek stole nothing during the first minute.

Excellent establishment.

We bought our own bowls.

I did not ask the price aloud.

I paid it.

The debt remained real.

So did soup.

Both could survive.

Rinna chose something with beans.

Hara had stew.

Pell had the same thing as me.

Marek had apparently chosen whatever produced the largest bowl.

Conversation began without a subject.

That was new for me.

At theatre, people talked because a thing needed doing.

At home, Lyssa and I had the entire infrastructure of living together.

At the cookshop, nobody needed anything from me except the space my elbow occupied.

Hara complained that the bread was smaller than last time.

Pell said the bowl was larger.

Hara said that was how they distracted fools.

Marek measured his bread against his thumb.

Rinna ignored all of them.

I ate.

A man at the next table laughed too loudly at something that was not ours.

Nobody looked.

Nessa arrived after we had started.

She had paint on one wrist.

Hara kicked the bench opposite.

"Here."

Nessa sat.

No greeting.

She took one piece of Marek's bread.

He stared at her.

"You said not to touch things."

"This is bread."

"It was mine."

"Was."

Pell almost lost soup through his nose.

Marek looked at me.

"Magistrate."

"No jurisdiction."

"Corruption."

"Established."

The joke survived exactly as long as it needed to.

Then Hara asked Nessa whether the painted jug was dead.

"Cracked under the handle."

"Repair?"

"No."

Marek said, "Stage only."

Nessa looked at him.

He stopped smiling.

"Too soon?"

"Wrong object."

"Important distinction."

She ate the bread.

The conversation moved again.

For several minutes the conversation belonged entirely to shoes.

Hara's left sole had started separating near the toe. She lifted the foot under the table and showed Nessa.

Nessa said, "Not tonight."

"I know."

Pell named a cobbler near the west steps.

Hara said, "Too slow."

Rinna named another near the dye lane.

Pell said, "Too expensive."

Marek said, "I can fix shoes."

Every face at the table turned toward him.

He looked offended.

"I can."

Nessa said, "With what?"

"Leather."

"Whose?"

That slowed him.

Hara lowered her foot.

"I'll go to the west steps."

Pell said, "You just said slow."

"I also enjoy owning shoes."

Decision made.

Nobody asked me.

I knew neither cobbler well enough to have an opinion, which was pleasant. A whole problem existed at the table without becoming mine.

A server came by and collected two empty bowls. She left ours because Marek was still using bread to chase the last of his broth around the bottom.

Rinna watched him for three seconds.

"You're going to wear through it."

"The bowl?"

"The bread."

"Then I have eaten completely."

Hara said, "That is not what completely means."

Marek held up the wet bread.

"Evidence."

I kept eating.

Someone mentioned a performer from another company who had apparently forgotten an entire entrance last week.

Nobody agreed on why.

Hara said drink.

Pell said panic.

Marek said artistic rebellion.

Rinna said, "He was outside."

That ended the theory.

I asked, "Why?"

Rinna ate one bean.

"Wrong door."

There were many ways to fail professionally.

Comforting.

I made one joke about hanging signs on the doors.

Nobody heard it because a tray hit the kitchen floor.

This was good for my character.

By the time bowls were empty, the light outside had changed.

Not dark yet.

Late enough that the street had begun collecting people headed home, elsewhere, or nowhere they intended to admit.

Hara left first.

Pell went with her for half a block, then turned north.

Nessa walked back toward the theatre because apparently paint had not finished with her.

Rinna disappeared into another street after saying, "Tomorrow no."

Clear.

Beautiful.

Marek stood beside me outside the cookshop.

"What now?"

"Home."

He nodded.

"Same."

We walked together for one street.

Then he turned without explanation.

I kept going.

At home, Lyssa was already eating.

Not waiting.

Good.

The dark sleeve was still gone.

The brown cloth remained.

Three pins.

Same fold.

She looked at me.

"You ate."

"Yes."

"Where?"

"With theatre."

She nodded and tore bread.

"Good?"

"Soup."

"That bad?"

"Very good soup."

She pushed the onion bowl toward me.

There were two left.

I had not escaped consequences.

I took one.

"Vale?"

"Not today."

"Magic?"

"No."

"Theatre?"

"Furniture."

She looked at my face.

"And soup."

"Mostly soup."

That satisfied her.

I ate the onion with bread because life was cruel but organized.

My cuff still had three loose threads.

I did not touch them.

The brown cloth did not move.

I took another bite of onion.

It remained a poor reward for good behavior.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 237

## THE CONDITION

Rinna had said no.

Vale had said maybe nothing.

Hessa had said nothing at all.

This produced a morning in which I was technically free and emotionally suspicious.

Lyssa was already gone when I woke. The brown cloth remained near the wall with the same fold and the same three pins. My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with the same three loose threads because I had finally discovered the repair method called leaving it alone.

I ate bread and one of the remaining onions.

The onions were winning the long war.

No note waited under the door.

I checked once.

Then I stopped checking because doing it twice would have become a habit and I already had enough of those.

Outside, Carrow had the clear cold light that made every wet stain on the stone look permanent. A cooper was rolling two empty barrels toward the lower lane. A woman had three chickens tied together by the legs and was losing an argument with all of them.

I went for breakfast that was not an onion.

This was my only plan.

I found a stall selling hot grain with chopped apple and bought a bowl. The woman gave me a wooden spoon with a crack down the handle.

I looked at it.

She said, "It holds."

"Strong standard."

"It holds."

I ate with it.

It held.

When I came home, a folded piece of paper was under the door.

Apparently timing remained a form of mockery.

I put the bowl down on the table and picked up the note.

Hessa's writing.

AFTER MIDDAY.

SAME ROOM.

EAT.

FOUR INTERMEDIATE.

I read the last line again.

Then I turned the paper over.

Nothing.

I looked toward the brown cloth.

"At least you are consistent."

The cloth declined comment.

Four intermediate.

No far.

No new mark.

No new direction written.

If Hessa had decided to invent a secret fifth distance, she had hidden it efficiently.

I did not try to design the session for her.

This was growth and also self-defense.

I spent the rest of the morning doing ordinary things badly enough to remain a person.

I washed the bowl from breakfast.

I swept half the room before discovering the broom was moving dust into the same corner the draft preferred.

I moved it somewhere else.

The draft won again.

I stopped.

I ate more bread before leaving because Hessa had written EAT in capital letters and I had no interest in sitting through the questions if I arrived hungry.

The walk to the Guild stayed ordinary.

Right hand normal.

Wrist normal.

Forearm normal.

Shoulders normal.

Right leg normal.

Residual limb comfortable.

The left crutch tip gripped the dry stone without comment.

My body had nothing new to contribute.

Good.

The same room was waiting.

Hessa had changed one thing.

The order page.

Everything else was where it had been.

Same chair.

Same marks in the floor.

Same board.

Same glass shield.

Same paper-fin target.

Same paper-fin control.

Same intermediate mark.

Same far mark, even though nothing sat there.

I looked at the empty far mark.

"Abandoned."

"No."

"Unused."

"Today."

"Better."

Hessa pointed at the chair.

I put the crutches in their usual place and sat.

My right foot found its mark.

Hips square.

Forearm where she wanted it.

Shoulder down.

She checked every part anyway.

"Food?"

"Grain. Apple. Bread."

"Water?"

"Yes."

"Work?"

"No."

"Theatre?"

"Rinna said no yesterday."

"Vale?"

"No."

"Carrying?"

"Breakfast."

She looked at my hands.

"Anything else?"

"An onion."

"Today?"

"No."

"Why did you say it?"

"I wanted credit for surviving it."

She wrote nothing.

Cruel.

"Hand?"

"Normal."

"Thumb?"

"Normal."

"Wrist?"

"Normal."

"Forearm?"

"Normal."

"Shoulder?"

"Normal."

"Right leg?"

"Normal."

"Residual limb?"

"Comfortable."

"Phantom?"

"Quiet."

She wrote the baseline.

I looked at the page beside her.

INTERMEDIATE.

INTERMEDIATE.

INTERMEDIATE.

INTERMEDIATE.

"That looks lazy."

"It is controlled."

"Same thing four times."

"Yes."

"No far?"

"No."

"No alternate order?"

"No."

"No farther far?"

"No."

"No new direction?"

"No."

"No larger target?"

"No."

"No different paper?"

"No."

"No exciting mistake?"

Hessa looked at me.

"Not planned."

"Good. I was worried."

She moved the page so I could read it properly.

"Last session established two useful things."

"Intermediate moved once."

"And did not move once."

"Far did not move twice."

"Yes."

"So intermediate won."

"No."

"I knew you'd say that."

"Then stop saying it."

I leaned back.

"The current apparatus has produced more clean observed responses at intermediate than far."

"Yes."

"Intermediate response has occurred across more than one session."

"Yes."

"Intermediate also failed cleanly."

"Yes."

"Therefore it is not reliable."

"Yes."

I pointed at the four identical lines.

"So now you want to know whether it behaves when we stop changing distance."

"Approximately."

"That is almost satisfying."

"Do not improve it."

There she was.

Hessa tapped the page.

"Today does not compare distances."

"Because far isn't tested."

"Correct."

"It does not establish range."

"Correct."

"It does not establish that intermediate always works."

"Clearly."

"It does not establish why it works when it works."

"No."

"It asks whether clean response continues to occur when we repeat the same intermediate condition in one session."

"Yes."

I considered the paper fins.

"Four?"

"Predeclared."

"Break?"

"After two external attempts."

"Extra attempt if all four fail?"

"No."

"Extra attempt if all four work?"

"No."

"Repeat one because I dislike it?"

"No."

"Good supervision remains hostile."

"Minimal draw first."

Of course.

Thirty-three successful supervised minimal draws sat behind me.

The number sounded large until Hessa gave the instruction and the actual task became small again.

I settled.

Breathed.

Drew the smallest amount I could manage.

Clean.

Contained.

No thumb spread.

No wrist catch.

No shoulder movement.

No change in the residual limb.

No phantom response.

"Return."

I returned.

Hessa watched the end of it.

"Any lingering sensation?"

"No."

"Count."

Thirty-four successful supervised minimal draws.

She wrote it.

Then shaping.

Same deliberate form as before.

No new shape.

No stronger hold.

No longer duration merely because the count had increased.

I drew under supervision and shaped exactly what she asked.

The sensation held where it should.

My shoulder stayed down.

My wrist remained quiet.

"Return."

I returned.

Hessa checked my hand, wrist, forearm, shoulder.

"Change?"

"No."

"Count."

Thirty supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Then the glass shield came between me and the target.

Hessa moved the paper fin to INTERMEDIATE.

The control remained in its established place.

The far mark stayed empty.

Attempt forty-six.

INTERMEDIATE.

AWAY.

Hessa checked target and control against their marks.

She checked the glass.

She checked the door.

The room was quiet.

"Begin when ready."

I settled.

This should have felt easier than far.

That thought arrived without permission.

I noticed it.

I did not tell the paper.

Draw.

Shape.

Away.

Nothing.

I held the effort where it belonged.

Nothing.

The target remained on its mark.

The control remained on its mark.

My mind started offering suggestions.

More effort.

Different angle.

Longer.

Every suggestion was a way to stop doing the test we had actually planned.

I let them pass.

Hessa called the stop.

I returned.

She checked the target.

Then the control.

"No observed response. Control unchanged."

"Intermediate."

"Yes."

"Again."

"Later."

She wrote.

I looked at the page.

One identical word had already produced a different feeling than I had expected.

Annoying.

Hessa checked my hand.

"Normal."

"I didn't ask yet."

"It is."

"Forearm?"

"Warm. Ordinary effort."

"Wrist?"

"Normal."

"Shoulder?"

"Normal."

"Residual limb?"

"Comfortable."

"Phantom?"

"Quiet."

Attempt forty-seven.

Same target position.

Same control.

Same glass.

Same body geometry.

Same direction.

Same stupid paper.

Hessa reset the target even though it had not moved.

She checked the mark anyway.

I watched.

"Do you expect it to move?" she asked.

That was new.

I looked at her.

"You did not ask that last time."

"I am asking now."

"Is that another condition?"

"No. It is a note."

I looked at the target.

"Less than before."

"Why?"

"The first one didn't."

She wrote.

I disliked being preserved.

"Begin when ready."

I settled.

Draw.

Shape.

Away.

For several seconds, nothing.

Then the target shifted.

Small.

Enough to be obvious because I had spent too much of my life staring at paper.

It moved away from its mark and turned slightly at the end.

The control did not move.

I returned when Hessa told me.

She crossed the room.

Measured.

Checked target.

Checked control.

Checked glass.

Then checked all three again.

"Observed target response. Small net displacement away. Minor rotation. Control unchanged."

I smiled.

Hessa looked at me.

"I am allowed to have a face."

"Yes."

"Good."

She wrote.

Attempt forty-six, no response.

Attempt forty-seven, response.

Same intermediate condition.

That was already enough to make the room feel less interested in my preferences.

We took the planned break.

I stood with both crutches and walked the room twice.

Right leg stiff from sitting.

Normal.

It loosened.

Forearm warm.

Normal.

Shoulders worked, not painful.

Residual limb comfortable.

No weakness.

No tingling.

No reason to stop.

Hessa made me drink water.

I did.

"Any change from before the external attempts?"

"Forearm warmer."

"Expected?"

"Feels like ordinary effort."

She wrote it.

I sat again.

The order page still had two identical words left.

INTERMEDIATE.

INTERMEDIATE.

"Do you expect the third one to move?" Hessa asked.

I looked at her.

"You've made this worse."

"Answer."

"Yes."

"More or less than attempt forty-seven?"

"More."

"Because forty-seven moved."

"Yes."

She wrote.

I pointed at the page.

"That is going to become embarrassing later."

"It is already data."

"Hostile profession."

Attempt forty-eight.

Target at intermediate.

Control unchanged.

Glass unchanged.

Door shut.

Same chair marks.

Same body position.

AWAY.

I began.

Draw.

Shape.

Hold.

Nothing.

I waited.

The urge to help arrived faster because I expected a response.

A little more would probably do it.

That was the thought.

Not knowledge.

Not evidence.

Not permission.

I kept the effort where it had been.

The target remained still.

The control remained still.

Hessa called the stop.

I returned.

She checked.

"No observed response. Control unchanged."

I laughed once.

Not because it was funny.

Because the paper had excellent timing.

Hessa looked at me.

"You expected response."

"Yes."

"And?"

"It did not care."

She wrote that too.

"Please don't write 'did not care.'"

"I didn't."

"Good."

She checked my hand and wrist.

No change.

Forearm warm.

Shoulder normal.

Right leg normal.

Residual limb comfortable.

One external attempt remained.

Attempt forty-nine.

Same intermediate mark.

Same target.

Same control.

Same glass.

Same AWAY direction.

Hessa reset everything.

Then she looked at me.

"Expectation?"

I considered lying for variety.

"Less."

"Why?"

"Because forty-eight didn't move."

She wrote.

"I am very predictable."

"Not the target."

That landed.

I looked at the paper.

"Rude."

"Begin when ready."

I settled.

The room became small.

Not exciting.

Not mystical.

Chair.

Glass.

Paper.

My own hand.

The sensation I could produce under supervision and still did not understand well enough to trust.

Draw.

Shape.

Away.

Nothing.

I held.

Nothing.

My forearm warmed another degree.

The target stayed where it was.

The control stayed where it was.

Hessa called the stop.

I returned.

She checked both fins.

No movement.

"No observed response. Control unchanged."

I nodded.

That one did not sting much.

Maybe expectation mattered to disappointment even when it did not matter to paper.

Hessa finished the line.

Forty-six: intermediate, no observed response, control unchanged.

Forty-seven: intermediate, small observed response away, minor rotation, control unchanged.

Forty-eight: intermediate, no observed response, control unchanged.

Forty-nine: intermediate, no observed response, control unchanged.

One clean response.

Three clean non-responses.

No control movement.

No apparatus disturbance.

Same condition repeated four times.

I read the list.

"Ugly."

"Useful."

"You need another word."

"No."

I leaned back.

"Intermediate can still respond."

"Yes."

"It did today."

"Yes."

"Under the same apparatus."

"Yes."

"Without far between attempts."

"Yes."

"So far wasn't causing the intermediate response."

Hessa did not answer.

I closed my eyes.

"Too large."

"Yes."

I tried again.

"Today shows an intermediate response can occur in a block where far is not tested between attempts."

"Yes."

"Better."

"Accurate."

"Intermediate also failed three times in the same block."

"Yes."

"So unreliable remains."

"Yes."

"More unreliable?"

"No."

I opened my eyes.

"Because we do not have a defined rate."

"Among other reasons."

"Does this weaken the distance difference?"

"Not by itself."

"Because we did not test far today."

"Correct."

"Does it strengthen intermediate existence?"

Hessa considered.

That meant the wording mattered.

"It adds another clean observed intermediate response under the current apparatus."

"Across another session."

"Yes."

"So the response has repeated across sessions and within a same-condition block."

"Observed response has occurred across sessions. Today contains one response within a repeated intermediate block."

I stared at her.

"You built that sentence to hurt me."

"I built it to be true."

Fair.

I looked at attempts forty-six through forty-nine.

One movement.

Three nothing.

"Could fatigue explain the three failures?"

"No."

"Could expectation explain them?"

"No."

"Could anything?"

"Many things could."

"Useful."

"We did not test those things."

There it was.

Hessa stacked the pages.

I looked at her hand.

"Next question?"

"Not chosen."

"Different again?"

"Yes."

"More different?"

She did not answer.

I accepted defeat.

"Restrictions?"

"Unchanged."

"Independent draw?"

"No."

"Independent shaping?"

"No."

"Paper?"

"No."

"Coins?"

"No."

"Thread?"

"No."

"Larger objects?"

"No."

"External testing?"

"No."

"Practice?"

"No."

"Barrier?"

"No."

"Theatre?"

"No."

"Loopholes?"

"No."

I nodded.

"Thirty-four, thirty, forty-nine."

"Yes."

The counts had grown again.

Nothing else had.

This no longer surprised me.

Hessa checked my body one final time.

Right hand normal.

Thumb normal.

Wrist normal.

Forearm warm from effort.

Shoulder normal.

Right leg a little stiff from sitting.

Residual limb comfortable.

Phantom quiet.

No concerning change.

At the door, I looked back at the empty far mark.

"Do we still think distance matters?"

Hessa did not look at the board.

"Under the current apparatus, the accumulated record still supports a difference associated with distance."

"Associated."

"Yes."

"Not range."

"No."

"Not reliable intermediate."

"No."

"Not generalized."

"No."

"Good."

Hessa looked at me.

I smiled.

"Still hate the word."

"Leave."

I left.

Carrow had reached late afternoon while we were arguing with one piece of paper.

The streets were busy enough that I had to wait at two crossings. A cart loaded with clay jars blocked half the lower lane. Someone above me shook a rug out a window and created weather from dust.

I bought a small piece of cheese and ate it walking slowly home.

No debt calculation.

No reward calculation.

Cheese.

At the apartment, Lyssa was still out.

The brown cloth remained.

Three pins.

Same fold.

I looked at it.

"One of us is reliable."

I regretted the sentence immediately.

I ate the rest of the cheese.

By dark, Lyssa had not returned.

That happened.

I put beans in the pot, cut one onion, and used less salt than before.

The room became warmer.

Someone downstairs laughed hard enough to cough.

A cart rattled past.

No note came under the door.

When Lyssa finally returned, she looked tired in the ordinary way, not injured. She had no bundle.

She looked at the pot.

"Beans?"

"Improved beans."

She tasted them.

"Beans."

Cruel but fair.

We ate.

She did not ask about Hessa.

I did not volunteer.

Halfway through the bowl she noticed the folded note beside the basin.

"Paper?"

"One."

She looked at me.

"Out of four?"

"Yes."

"Same distance?"

"All intermediate."

She nodded and kept eating.

That was the entire analysis.

After supper she moved the empty bowls aside and put both elbows on the table.

I did the same.

The brown cloth stayed by the wall.

Three pins.

Still nothing I understood.

I looked at Lyssa.

She looked back.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

For once, the most reliable thing in the room was not the paper.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 238

## THE TAGALONG

Lyssa was still home when I woke.

That was the first surprise.

The second was that she was not working.

The brown cloth remained near the wall with its three pins and its refusal to become my business. My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads and the good sense to stay there. The table held bread, a small knife, two empty bowls, and Lyssa's forearms.

Nothing else.

She was sitting with both elbows on the table exactly as she had been the night before.

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

"You're still here."

"Yes."

"Is something wrong?"

"No."

I waited.

She waited longer.

"Good morning," I said.

"Better."

I got up.

Right leg normal after sleep. Residual limb comfortable. Shoulders fine. Left crutch tip boring. The Guild had left me with nothing more serious than the memory of paper refusing to cooperate, which was where I preferred it.

No note sat under the door.

I did not check twice.

Lyssa tore the loaf.

"Working?"

"Nobody has claimed me."

"Theatre?"

"No."

"Vale?"

"No."

"Hessa?"

I looked at the door.

"No."

Lyssa nodded.

Then she said, "Come with me."

I stopped halfway through reaching for bread.

"Where?"

"Market."

"What kind?"

"The kind with food."

"There are several."

"The west one."

"Why?"

She ate.

I waited.

Finally she said, "Because I am going."

That was apparently the complete structure.

I took bread.

"Am I carrying something?"

"Maybe."

"Ah."

"You don't have to come."

That changed it.

Not much.

Enough.

I looked around the room as though a competing obligation might rise from the basin.

Nothing did.

"I'll come."

Lyssa nodded once and kept eating.

I had apparently passed the interview.

We left after breakfast.

She locked the door. I took the stairs first because it was easier than trying to negotiate two crutches around her on the landing. By the bottom, she had the cloth bag over one shoulder and I had nothing except my coat and the strange feeling that I had joined an errand without being assigned a function.

Outside, Carrow was bright and cold.

Yesterday's dust had settled. The lower stones still held dark seams of damp where the sun had not reached. Someone across the street was beating a rug over a rail. A child below it was trying to catch the dust in a cup.

Useful education.

Lyssa started west.

I fell beside her.

Not quite beside.

The street narrowed around a cart wheel, so she went ahead and I followed. Then it widened and we came even again.

"What are we buying?"

"Food."

"You've said."

"Oil."

"That is not food."

"Beans."

"Better."

"Bread."

"We have bread."

"Not enough."

"Salt?"

She looked at me.

"Probably."

"I used less."

"Yes."

"Successfully."

"Yes."

That was all I got.

We crossed South Lane where two women were arguing about a basket of apples. Neither seemed to own the basket. Both seemed committed to the argument.

Lyssa slowed near a fruit stall.

I expected her to stop.

She did not.

A woman behind the stall called, "Lyssa."

Lyssa lifted one hand.

"Morning."

The woman pointed at a stack of pears.

"Better today."

Lyssa changed direction immediately.

I followed.

Apparently we were buying pears.

The woman picked one up and handed it to Lyssa.

Lyssa turned it once, pressed near the stem, and put it back.

"Still hard."

"Less hard."

"That is not ripe."

"Tomorrow."

"Then sell them tomorrow."

The woman looked at me.

"She always like this?"

I considered the available loyalties.

"About pears?"

Lyssa looked at me.

I said, "I have no evidence outside pears."

The woman laughed.

Lyssa did not.

We did not buy pears.

Ten steps later, Lyssa said, "Coward."

"I protected the relationship."

"Yours."

"Exactly."

She bumped her shoulder lightly against mine.

I nearly planted the left crutch too far out because I was watching her instead of the street.

"Violence," I said.

"You survived."

"Strong standard."

She gave me a look.

I regretted borrowing the breakfast woman's line before the thought had fully formed.

Good.

Some jokes deserved to die young.
