The west market sat in a broad square with three lanes feeding into it and enough people to make every route temporary. Stalls crowded the edges. Carts claimed the middle until someone louder needed the space. Smoke from two cooking braziers moved sideways under the awnings.

I knew the square.

I did not know it the way Lyssa did.

She turned left before the bread sellers.

I would have gone right.

I followed.

A narrow stall sold dried beans, grain, salt, and things in sacks that all looked like different shades of dust until someone named them.

Lyssa greeted the man behind it with no name.

He greeted her the same way.

"What beans?"

"Dark."

"Old or new?"

"Old."

He scooped from a sack.

I looked at Lyssa.

She saw it.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Ask."

"Why old?"

The seller answered before she did.

"Cheaper."

Lyssa said, "And they cook."

He held up a scoop.

"Eventually."

"See?"

I looked at the beans.

They looked exactly like a future argument with water.

"How much eventually?"

The seller poured them into Lyssa's bag.

"Long enough."

This city had a hostile relationship with units.

Lyssa paid.

I watched because money remained money.

Not to inspect her.

Not to calculate household income.

Just because coins leaving a hand were real.

The seller added a small handful after the measure.

Lyssa noticed.

She did not comment.

Neither did he.

I wondered whether that was habit, favor, measurement correction, or an attempt to remove the worst beans from his sack.

I did not ask.

Progress.

We bought salt next.

Lyssa let me choose the amount.

This was a trap.

I knew it was a trap.

"Small."

The seller waited.

Lyssa waited.

I held two fingers apart.

He filled a paper twist.

Lyssa looked at it.

"Enough?"

"Yes."

"For how long?"

"Until I improve."

She took the salt from me.

"Then more."

The seller added another pinch.

Betrayal from both sides.

We moved on.

A boy ran between us carrying a basket and almost clipped my right crutch. I stopped. Lyssa did not grab me. She had already stepped aside because she knew I would stop.

The boy shouted an apology over his shoulder without slowing.

"Excellent," I said.

Lyssa said, "You were in his way."

"He was in mine."

"He was faster."

"Law by velocity."

"Useful law."

I could not completely disagree.

At the bread stall, Lyssa rejected two loaves and bought the third.

I asked why.

"The bottom."

She turned it over.

One was burned.

The second had a pale soft patch where it had rested too close to another loaf.

The third was ordinary bread.

I had bought all three kinds before without knowing I was making choices.

"Your standards are exhausting."

"You eat them."

"That is different."

"No."

She paid for one loaf.

I bought another.

Lyssa looked at me.

"Why?"

"Two people."

"We had one."

"Not enough, according to an authority."

She considered that.

Then nodded.

My bread survived review.

We carried one loaf each for half a street before I had to change the arrangement. A crutch, a loaf, and a crowd did not produce enough hands. I tucked mine under my coat arm.

Lyssa watched me fight with it.

"Bag."

"I have it."

"Bag."

"I am proving something."

"What?"

I moved the loaf again because it was slipping.

"Unknown."

She held the bag open.

I put the loaf in.

"Thank you."

"Useful."

There were words she could weaponize across years.

We bought lamp oil from Olin.

That had not been my idea.

Lyssa turned toward his shop and I nearly asked whether she knew him. Then I remembered that knowing where an oil merchant worked did not constitute a secret professional network.

I was learning.

Olin was behind the counter with a tin open in front of him and a rag in one hand. His boy was not there.

He saw me.

"Theatre."

Better than oil man.

"Home," I said.

He looked at Lyssa.

She put the small household tin on the counter.

"Fill."

Olin did.

No warning.

No folded message.

No brown coat.

No question about Rinna.

He filled the tin, wiped the neck, checked the stopper, named the price, and took Lyssa's coin.

Ordinary commerce had never seemed so aggressively respectable.

I almost liked him for it.

Olin looked at my crutches.

"Tip holding?"

I looked down.

"Yes."

He nodded.

That was apparently the entire subject.

Outside, Lyssa said, "You know him."

"Theatre oil."

"Of course."

"I carried one tin once and suffered a reputation."

"What reputation?"

"Oil man."

She laughed.

I pointed at her.

"Do not."

"Oil man."

"Relationship damaged."

"Carry the oil."

"No."

She handed me the tin.

I stared at it.

She kept walking.

This was how empires collapsed.

The tin was small enough to carry by its loop in my right hand for short stretches, but that hand also belonged to the right crutch. I tried the loop over my wrist.

Bad.

It swung.

I stopped.

Lyssa turned around.

I held up the tin.

"Engineering failure."

She came back, took it, and put it into the bag.

No commentary.

Worse.

"Say it."

"What?"

"Whatever your face is doing."

"My face is walking."

"Your face is very talented."

She started again.

I followed.

We left the market with beans, salt, two loaves, oil, and no pears.

I had expected the trip to feel like labor by then.

It did not.

Not exactly.

My shoulders were working because streets were streets. My right hand had the ordinary warmth of a morning on crutches. The bag stayed with Lyssa because pretending equality required identical loads was stupid and both of us knew it.

I carried one loaf again once the crowd thinned.

That was enough.

Near the edge of the square, Lyssa stopped at a stall selling cooked onions, flat cakes, and small strips of browned meat.

I looked at the onions.

"No."

She ordered two flat cakes.

"Thank you."

"One is mine."

"Still."

We ate standing near a wall because the nearest bench was occupied by a woman with three baskets and no visible intention of surrendering territory.

Lyssa tore a piece from her cake and held it out.

I took it.

Mine had more onion.

Hers had more meat.

We traded another piece without discussing the economics.

For several minutes we did not talk.

People moved around us.

A man in a green cap tried to carry six clay cups in two hands and lost one. It broke. He looked at the five surviving cups, then at the pieces, then kept walking.

A dog sat under the meat stall with the concentration of a saint.

Two girls shared a red scarf while their mother negotiated over carrots.

Nobody needed anything from me.

Lyssa finished first.

She wiped her fingers on a cloth.

"Afternoon," she said.

"What about it?"

"I have work."

There it was.

Not a problem.

Not a betrayal.

Just the edge of the morning.

"Where?"

"North."

I nodded.

"Home first?"

"No."

She shifted the bag on her shoulder and took out one loaf.

She handed it to me.

Then the salt.

Then the oil tin.

"Ah."

"Home."

"I have become transport."

"You asked what you were carrying."

"Hours ago."

"Now you know."

I looked at the collection.

The oil tin could go in the cloth bag if I had the cloth bag.

Lyssa handed me that too.

"Are you leaving me with everything?"

"Beans come with me."

"Why?"

"Because I bought them."

This was not an explanation.

It was enough.

I arranged the bag across my shoulder so it rested against my left side and did not swing into the crutch. The oil tin sat low. Bread above it. Salt tucked near the top.

Lyssa watched the arrangement.

"Good?"

"Probably."

"Strong standard."

I looked at her.

She smiled.

Fine.

That one had lived.

We started toward the split where north road left the square.

At the corner, Lyssa stopped.

I expected a work instruction.

Instead she put one hand on the front of my coat and kissed me.

Not long.

Not one of the quick polite touches people gave in doorways either.

Enough that I forgot the bag for a second and had to correct my balance when we separated.

"You attack around crutches," I said.

"You complain around everything."

"Consistency."

"Home."

"North."

She turned.

I watched her go for three steps.

Then she looked back.

"What?"

I had not said anything.

"Nothing."

"Good."

Cruel woman.

I went home.

The bag arrangement worked until the stairs.

Then it did not.

The oil tin shifted. The bread compressed against my ribs. The salt tried to migrate into a corner that did not exist.

I stopped on the first landing and reorganized.

A woman from the floor below came down carrying a bucket.

She waited.

I moved the bag.

She waited more.

"Go," I said.

"You sure?"

"Yes."

She passed.

No speech about courage.

No attempt to carry me.

Just a bucket needing the stairs.

I appreciated her immediately.

At the apartment I put the oil by the lamp, salt near the cooking things, and bread on the table.

The brown cloth remained near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

I did not speak to it.

Growth.

The damaged shirt stayed on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

Also growth.

I had the room to myself.

For the first time in several days, that did not feel like a gap waiting to be filled by a note.

I washed the morning bowls.

I checked the lamp wick because we had bought oil, not because I was conducting a study.

It needed trimming.

I trimmed it badly.

The flame leaned.

I adjusted it.

Better.

Not data.

I sat.

Then I did something dangerous.

Nothing.

No theatre.

No Vale.

No Hessa.

No errands left that belonged to me.

The debt remained.

The theatre would exist tomorrow or not.

Hessa had questions she had not chosen.

Lyssa was somewhere north doing work that did not require my witness.

I had bread.

I ate some.

At some point I slept.

Not a planned nap.

A chair, a warm room, and a full stomach negotiated without consulting me.

When I woke, the light had moved across the floor.

My neck hurt slightly from the angle.

Good.

Human.

I stood, stretched, and went downstairs for water.

The woman with the bucket was coming back up.

Empty bucket now.

She looked at me.

"Still here?"

I almost laughed.

"Apparently."

She kept going.

That was the entire relationship.

By late afternoon I had started the old beans.

Not Lyssa's new beans.

Those had gone north for reasons that remained outside my authority.

The old ones softened slowly.

I cut an onion.

One left.

Victory approached.

I considered making the food before Lyssa returned.

Then I remembered she had said work.

No promise about supper.

So I made enough for both of us and ate my portion when it was ready.

This was a skill I had acquired very slowly.

Not waiting was different from not caring.

I left the rest covered.

Lyssa returned after dark.

No bundle.

Again.

She opened the door, looked at the table, then at me.

"You ate."

"Yes."

"Good."

She set the bean bag down.

Still beans.

Apparently the north had not consumed them.

"What did the beans do?"

"Waited."

"For?"

"Tomorrow."

I nodded as if that explained an entire day.

It probably did to her.

She washed her hands.

I uncovered the pot.

"Old beans."

She looked inside.

"Soft?"

"Eventually."

She tasted one.

"Soft."

I took the victory.

She sat and ate.

I sat across from her even though I had already finished.

No report waited in my mouth.

No experiment.

No show.

No office problem.

After several bites she said, "You slept."

I stared at her.

"How do you know?"

"Face."

"What is wrong with my face?"

"Chair."

That did not answer anything.

"Did the chair tell you?"

"You have the line."

I touched my cheek.

There was, in fact, a shallow mark where my sleeve seam must have pressed into it.

Betrayed by upholstery.

"I was resting."

"You were sleeping."

"Unsupervised."

She looked at me.

I regretted that immediately.

"No magic joke," I said.

"I didn't."

"Good."

She ate another bean.

I looked at the brown cloth.

Still there.

I looked back at Lyssa.

"Good morning."

She frowned.

"What?"

"This morning. It was good."

Her face changed very little.

Enough.

"Yes."

Then she reached across the table and took the last piece of my bread from beside my hand.

I stared at the empty space.

"That was mine."

"You ate."

"Earlier."

"You survived."

"Relationship damaged again."

She ate the bread.

I stayed anyway.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 239

## THE DISTRIBUTOR

The new beans were gone when I woke.

I noticed because they had spent the previous day traveling farther than I had without becoming any more informative.

The brown cloth was still near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads, which made it the most stable project in the apartment.

Lyssa was gone.

No note under the door.

No theatre coat on the chair.

No paper from Hessa.

No Vale clerk waiting in the room with a book large enough to ruin breakfast.

I ate bread.

The last onion remained untouched.

It had survived through discipline and cowardice.

Someone knocked before I finished.

I looked at the door.

The door had not been part of my plan.

The knock came again.

I got the crutches, crossed the room, and opened it.

A boy stood on the landing with a stack of folded handbills under one arm.

Not Olin's boy.

Younger than Davin, older than a child who should have been trusted with that much paper.

He looked at me, then at my crutches.

"Greg?"

"Unfortunately."

"Rinna says if you're free, come before midday."

I waited.

He waited.

"That's all?"

He looked down at the top handbill as though more instructions might have been printed there.

"She said if you're free."

"Not dying?"

"No."

"Building on fire?"

"No."

"Someone missing?"

"No."

"Excellent message."

He frowned.

"She said you'd ask things."

That sounded like Rinna.

I took one of the handbills from the stack.

He pulled it back.

"Those aren't yours."

"Good boundary."

He left.

I closed the door.

The morning had acquired a shape.

Not an emergency.

Not a mystery.

Work, probably.

I finished breakfast, drank water, and left the onion alive.

Outside, Carrow had the washed-blue sky that made every roofline look sharper than it was. A wagon full of split wood occupied most of the lower lane. Two women were already arguing with the driver about whether most of the lane meant all of it.

Nobody needed me.

I went around.

My shoulders felt normal after yesterday's market. Right hand normal. Neck fine. Residual limb comfortable. Left crutch tip held.

The theatre side door was open when I arrived.

Inside, Rinna had turned one table into paper.

Handbills in stacks.

Handbills folded.

Handbills not folded.

A bowl of paste that nobody seemed to be using.

Three short lengths of string.

One pencil.

Marek sat on the edge of the stage folding paper badly enough that the corners never met.

Nessa was doing something with a dark green coat near the rear passage.

Jori and Davin were carrying a narrow door frame across the stage.

Teren was nowhere visible.

Rinna looked up.

"Free?"

"Until somebody improves my life."

"Good."

"I hate that word."

"Five places."

She pointed at five stacks.

"Cookshop near South Steps. Cobbler on Red Lane. Lamp-glass woman. The little tea room by the old fountain. Bread stall at West Gate."

"Handbills."

"Remarkable."

"How many?"

"Five each."

Marek held up a folded bill.

"Unless they love theatre."

Rinna said, "Five."

Marek lowered it.

I looked at the stacks.

"The boy brought these?"

"He brought some. You're taking the rest."

"Why me?"

"Because Davin and Jori have the door. Nessa has coats. Hara and Pell are with Teren. Marek has north."

Marek said, "I have the better route."

"You have the shorter route," Rinna said.

"Better."

Rinna ignored him.

"Ask before you leave them. If they say no, bring them back."

"Do I explain the plays?"

"If they ask."

"Do I promise anything?"

"No."

"Do I argue?"

"No."

"Do I perform in the shop?"

Rinna looked at me.

Marek said, "Yes."

Rinna said, "No."

I took the stacks.

Twenty-five sheets weighed almost nothing.

Twenty-five sheets plus two crutches still required somewhere to put them.

Rinna slid a narrow strap bag across the table.

"Use that."

I looked at her.

"You own a bag."

"We own many things."

"Somebody should tell Nessa."

Nessa, from the rear passage, said, "I know every bag."

Of course she did.

The strap sat high enough across my chest that the paper rested against my right side without catching the crutch. I adjusted it once.

Rinna watched.

"Works?"

"Yes."

"Bring the bag back."

There it was.

The real responsibility.

The first cookshop took five.

The woman behind the counter read the top line, looked at me, and said, "Last one brought people who ordered water."

"I apologize for the arts."

"They bought soup after."

"Then we saved the economy."

She put the handbills near the door.

I left before the claim could be examined.

The cobbler on Red Lane took three.

I had counted five onto the counter.

He pushed two back.

"Three."

"Rinna said five."

"My wall says three."

I looked at the wall.

The wall already held a wrestling notice, two room advertisements, a lost-dog sketch, a church supper announcement, and something written in letters so faded I could no longer tell what had once been urgent.

"Strong authority."

"Three."

I put two back in the bag.

The route had changed already.

Good.

Not everything became more convenient because I had done it once.

At the tea room, I made my mistake.

The owner said yes while carrying a tray.

I reached into the bag, counted five against my thumb, set them on the side table, and moved away so she could pass.

A girl sweeping near the wall looked at the stack.

"Seven."

I stopped.

"What?"

"Seven."

I counted.

Seven.

Apparently my thumb had become generous.

The owner did not stop moving.

The girl handed me two.

"Rinna will be devastated."

"Who?"

"Correct."

I put the extras away.

The girl resumed sweeping.

Useful correction.

No ceremony.

By the time I reached West Gate, the bread stall had no room.

Not no room metaphorically.

The seller had hung strings of small notices from two poles, and every available section fluttered with rooms, work, performances, sales, missing things, found things, lessons, debts, and one very aggressive drawing of a goat.

He looked at my handbills.

"No."

"Five?"

"No."

"Three?"

"No."

"One heroic handbill?"

He pointed at the goat.

"What am I supposed to cover?"

The goat did have presence.

I put the handbills back in the bag.

"Fair."

I had one stop left.

Lamp glass.

The shop was narrow, bright in the dangerous way of rooms where most surfaces could cut you, and warmer than the street. Small panes leaned in racks along one wall. Chimneys, globes, and shaped pieces sat behind the counter.

The woman behind it looked up when I entered.

Her eyes went to the bag.

"Theatre?"

"Handbills."

"How many?"

"Five unless your wall has a goat."

She looked toward the front window.

"No goat."

"Excellent."

She came around the counter and pointed at a board beside the door.

"Bottom."

I put five there.

I was straightening the stack when she said, "Wait."

I stopped.

Not fear.

Not yet anything.

Just a word.

She looked past me through the front glass, then back.

"For Rinna."

I took my hand off the handbills.

"What?"

"A man came yesterday."

Yesterday.

After Lyssa and I had bought oil from Olin, maybe before, maybe while I was sleeping in a chair.

The ordinary day remained ordinary.

This was new.

"What man?"

"Brown coat. Dark hair. Narrow face."

My attention tightened.

"Quiet voice?"

"Yes."

"Did you know him?"

"No."

"Did he give a name?"

"No."

She folded her arms.

"He asked about the theatre."

I did not ask what I wanted to ask first.

Bren.

Mason's Cut.

Cart.

Money.

Instead I heard Rinna asking a boy for exact words.

"What did he ask?"

The woman thought.

"First if I supplied the hall."

"What did you say?"

"That I sell glass."

Good answer.

"Then?"

"He asked, 'Do they take deliveries through the front or the back?'"

I repeated it.

"Do they take deliveries through the front or the back?"

"Yes."

"Exact?"

"As exact as I remember."

"Anything else?"

"He asked who opens for deliveries."

That was more.

My hands tightened slightly on the grips.

"What did you say?"

"That if he needed glass, I could sell him glass."

I almost smiled.

"Did he buy any?"

"No."

"Did he threaten you?"

"No."

"Offer money for an answer?"

"No."

"Ask when the theatre is empty?"

"No."

"Ask about Rinna's money?"

"No."

"Ask about the cart?"

"No."

"Say Bren?"

"No."

She watched me.

"Should he have?"

"No."

The answer came faster than I intended.

I corrected it.

"I don't know."

Better.

She nodded once.

"Tell Rinna."

"I will."

I looked at the board of handbills.

One corner of the top sheet had curled.

I pressed it flat.

The woman said, "Five is fine."

It took me a moment to understand.

"Right."

Theatre.

Handbills.

The job still existed.

I left.

Outside, the street had not changed because one man had asked a better question.

A porter was unloading two crates of clay lamp bases.

A woman was trying to persuade a child to wear both shoes.

Someone farther down the lane shouted about fish.

I stood against the wall for a moment and considered going directly back.

Then I looked at the bag.

Two extra from the cobbler.

Five refused by the bread stall.

The route was not finished merely because something more interesting had happened.

That was an irritatingly useful fact.

I went back to the theatre after finishing the loop I had already finished, which was to say I walked there carrying seven handbills nobody wanted.

My right palm was warm. Shoulders worked. Nothing unusual.

Marek arrived from the north at nearly the same time.

His bag was empty.

I looked at it.

"Nobody refused?"

"One place took eight."

"Rinna said five."

"They asked."

"Did you invent demand?"

"They wanted more."

I could not prove otherwise.

Inside, Jori and Davin had the narrow door frame upright.

It was not attached to anything.

Marek looked at it.

"Door."

Jori said, "Frame."

"Door soon."

"Maybe."

Marek nodded as if the distinction had been settled in his favor.

Rinna was at the paper table.

I put the bag down.

"Seven back."

She looked at me.

"Seven?"

"Red Lane took three. West Gate said no."

"Why?"

"Goat."

Rinna waited.

"Wall full."

"Better."

I slid the unused bills onto the return stack.

Then I said, "Lamp glass had a message."

Her hand stopped.

Not dramatically.

Enough.

She took the pencil.

"Tell me."

I did.

Brown coat.

Dark hair.

Narrow face.

Quiet voice.

Unknown man.

No name.

Asked whether she supplied the hall.

Asked, exact as remembered: "Do they take deliveries through the front or the back?"

Then asked who opens for deliveries.

No purchase.

No threat.

No offered money.

No question about empty hours.

No cart question.

No money question.

No Bren.

Rinna wrote the wording down.

Marek had stopped pretending not to listen.

Nessa came closer from the rear passage, still holding the green coat.

Jori did not. He was checking the frame.

Davin held it steady.

The theatre did not become a meeting because information had entered the building.

Rinna read what she had written.

"Description consistent with Olin's."

I said, "Not confirmed."

"No."

"Question is new."

"Yes."

Marek said, "Front or back."

Rinna looked at him.

"Yes."

"That's not money."

"No."

"That's doors."

"Excellent work."

Marek smiled.

Then stopped when he realized she had not meant it as praise.

I said, "It adds access."

Rinna looked at me.

I tried again.

"It adds a question about delivery access."

"Better."

Annoying.

Accurate.

Nessa set the green coat on the table edge.

"Did she answer?"

"No."

"Good."

Rinna wrote one more line.

NO USEFUL ANSWER GIVEN.

Nessa said, "We already tell deliveries where to come."

Rinna nodded.

"We keep doing that."

Marek frowned.

"Nothing changes?"

Rinna looked around the theatre.

"Something already changed. We know another question."

"That's it?"

"For now."

I understood the disappointment.

A pattern had moved.

The body wanted an action to match it.

Lock something.

Move something.

Follow someone.

Invent a guard.

Go find Mason's Cut and stare at every brown coat until the city became evidence.

Rinna capped the pencil.

Marek frowned.

"So nothing changes?"

"Not because he asked a question."

Nessa set the green coat on the table edge.

"Back latch sticks."

Rinna looked at her.

"Still?"

"Still."

"Then fix the latch."

Jori said from the stage, "After frame."

That was the right size of reaction. A stranger had asked about doors. The theatre still had one door that actually stuck.

The theatre had acquired one more fact about an unknown man and still had a bad latch.

Both could be true.

I helped with the frame.

Not because the pressure demanded labor.

Because the frame needed holding while Davin changed the lower brace.

Jori had one side. I took the other from a seated position near the stage edge, keeping the weight where my shoulders could manage it without turning the crutches into furniture.

Davin loosened two screws, shifted the brace, and said, "Now."

Jori moved.

I held.

Marek offered advice.

Nobody accepted it.

The frame settled square.

Davin checked the lower corner.

Jori checked the top.

They disagreed by less than a finger.

They adjusted it anyway.

Nessa carried the green coat past us.

"Tomorrow."

I looked at her.

"What?"

She stopped.

"You're wearing this tomorrow."

I looked at the coat.

Dark green.

Short enough not to reach my knees.

"Why?"

"Teren."

That explained nothing.

Rinna, from the table, said, "Pages."

She held up a folded set.

I released the frame when Jori told me to.

Then I crossed to her.

The top page had a role marked in the margin.

MESSENGER.

I looked at Rinna.

"That's insulting."

"You brought a message."

"Once."

"Today."

"That is how names start."

Marek said, "Message man."

I pointed at him.

"No."

Rinna handed me the pages.

"Tomorrow morning. Rehearsal first. Performance if Teren keeps it paired."

"How many lines?"

"Eight."

"Entrances?"

"Three."

"Running?"

"No."

"Good."

Rinna looked at me.

I sighed.

"Still hate it."

The piece was called The Missing Key.

I did not read farther while standing.

That was how mistakes began.

I put the pages in the now-empty handbill bag.

Rinna took the bag back out.

"Mine."

I held the pages.

Of course.

At the end of the afternoon she paid me the normal copper.

Handbills and frame work had apparently become a day.

I put the coin away.

No debt calculation followed.

The debt remained smaller than it had once been and larger than I wanted it.

Outside, Marek walked with me for half a street.

"Back door."

"Question about back or front."

"Same thing."

"No."

He thought.

"Mostly same."

"No."

"Annoying."

"Yes."

He turned north.

I went home.

The apartment was empty.

The new beans were still gone.

The brown cloth remained.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

No one had repaired my life while I was distributing paper.

I set the pages from The Missing Key on the table.

Eight lines.

Three entrances.

Messenger.

I read them once while bread warmed near the pan.

Then again because the second entrance came sooner than I expected.

No magic.

No Vale.

No answer about the man.

Lyssa returned after I had eaten half my bread.

She had no beans.

No bundle either.

She looked at the pages.

"Theatre?"

"Tomorrow."

"Today?"

"Paper."

She looked at the handbills I was not carrying.

"Successful?"

"Seven survived."

"Poor things."

I told her I had a role tomorrow.

"Messenger."

She looked at me.

I waited.

She did not say oil man.

I respected her restraint.

Then she said, "Fits."

"Relationship damaged."

She took bread.

I looked at the brown cloth.

Still there.

Then at the pages.

Lyssa took another piece of bread and leaned over the first page.

"Eight?"

"Eight."

She handed it back.

The beans stayed wherever tomorrow had put them.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 240

## THE BEARER

The pages were still on the table when I woke.

That was useful because I had put them there for exactly that reason.

The brown cloth was also still near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads.

Lyssa was gone.

No beans.

Apparently tomorrow had kept them.

I did not investigate.

I read The Missing Key again while eating bread.

Eight lines.

Three entrances.

Messenger.

The second entrance still came sooner than my body wanted it to. The first was simple. Deliver a note. Leave.

The second came after barely half a page.

The third had enough room before it that I could forget I existed.

Dangerous structure.

I read the second cue twice, folded the pages, and left before I could turn eight lines into a military campaign.

The morning was cool and dry. Carrow had already filled the lower streets with carts, baskets, shouting, and one man carrying a chair on his head because civilization was flexible.

My neck was fine.

Shoulders normal.

Right hand normal.

Residual limb comfortable.

Left crutch tip boring.

Good.

The theatre side door opened cleanly when I pushed it.

I stopped.

Closed it.

Opened it again.

No catch.

Jori, somewhere behind the stage, said, "Yes."

I looked into the room.

"Was that for me?"

"The latch."

"Fixed?"

Davin appeared in the rear passage with a small file in one hand.

"Mostly me."

Jori said, "Mostly the file."

Davin looked at the file.

"Then pay it."

I went inside.

The narrow door frame from yesterday now stood onstage with an actual door hanging in it. The door had been painted a tired brown that looked respectable from ten feet away and diseased from three.

Nessa was at the property table with three folded notes, a brass-colored key, and the short dark-green coat.

She pointed at the coat.

"On."

"Good morning."

"Coat."

"Warmer."

She looked at me until I put my pages down.

The coat fit across my shoulders better than the black Magistrate coat had. Short hem. Clear of the crutches. Deep inside pocket on the right.

I put one hand into it.

Nessa slapped the outside of the pocket.

"Not yours."

"I was checking."

"You found it."

She handed me the first folded note.

"One."

Then the second.

"Two."

Then the third.

"Three."

They were not identical.

The first was folded once.

The second twice.

The third had one corner tucked under.

I looked at her.

"Useful."

"Yes."

"Did you do that for me?"

"No. I did it for the play."

Fair.

I put them into the inside pocket in order.

Marek came through the stage door wearing a pale vest and carrying the brass-colored key.

Nessa held out her hand.

He stopped.

"I just got here."

"Key."

"I need it."

"Later."

"It is called The Missing Key."

"And this one is not missing yet."

He gave it to her.

Healthy system.

Hara sat at the edge of the stage tying the repaired shoe tighter. The front no longer opened when she flexed it.

"Comfortable?" I asked.

"No."

"Still attached?"

"Unfortunately."

Pell was reading near the first row.

Teren came in from the house aisle with his own pages.

"Places."

No speech.

No encouragement.

The profession remained efficient.

The Missing Key was worse than I had understood at home.

This was praise.

Hara played the Mistress of the house.

Marek played her younger brother, who had borrowed the key to a locked writing cabinet and then denied borrowing anything.

Pell played a family clerk who kept an inventory of objects nobody else respected.

My Messenger worked for someone who was not in the play and had apparently spent the entire day carrying increasingly unhelpful notes to the same address.

The missing key mattered because the Mistress needed a paper from the cabinet before an aunt arrived.

The aunt never appeared.

The paper might not have mattered.

Nobody knew.

The cabinet was represented by a narrow painted box on the side table.

The first note said the absent master had taken the key with him.

The second said he had sent it back yesterday.

The third said it was under the blue vase.

There was no blue vase onstage.

There had been.

Marek's character had broken it before the play began.

Excellent construction.

We started with entrances.

I waited outside the brown door frame with the first note in my pocket.

Hara and Marek argued about the cabinet.

Pell read from the inventory.

Hara said, "Then where is the key?"

I entered.

Teren said, "No."

I stopped.

"I have a message."

"You have a door."

I looked at the door.

"Yes."

"Use it."

"I did."

"You entered like the wall had failed."

I looked back at the frame.

The door was still swinging.

Apparently I had pushed harder than necessary.

Again.

Hara: "Then where is the key?"

I opened the door.

Too slowly.

Teren said, "No."

I looked at him.

"Strong range."

"You're deciding the entrance matters."

"I am entering."

"Correct."

That was not helpful.

Hara said, "He means stop presenting the entrance."

Teren looked at her.

She shrugged.

"Did I improve it?"

"Unfortunately."

Again.

This time I opened the door and came through it because I needed to be on the other side.

Better.

I crossed toward Hara.

The easiest route took me in front of Pell.

Jori said, "Back."

I backed up.

He pointed at the space behind Pell's chair.

"Through there."

The lane was wide enough if I kept the right crutch close.

I tried it.

Clear.

No chair.

No coat hem.

No foot.

"Keep," Teren said.

Nessa put a chalk nick near the door side of the route.

Not a speech.

A place.

Again.

I entered, used the lane, stopped beside Hara, and held out the first note.

"Message for this house."

Hara took it.

I waited.

She opened it.

Read.

Looked at Marek.

I waited more.

Teren said, "Why are you still there?"

"I want to know what it says."

"Greg does."

"Messenger also might."

"Messenger has work."

I looked at the door.

"Exit."

"Yes."

I left.

That was the correction.

Not mysterious.

Deliver.

Leave.

The second entrance was worse because the scene had barely recovered from the first note.

Marek insisted the absent master had the key.

Pell found a receipt showing the key had been sent.

Hara asked who had brought the receipt.

Pell said, "A messenger."

I entered.

Teren said, "No."

I stopped in the doorway.

"That is literally me."

"That is not your cue."

I looked at the page.

My cue came two lines later.

Marek had to say, "Then find the fool who carried it."

I closed the door.

From the stage Hara said, "Convenient."

"Hostile workplace."

Again.

This time I waited through "A messenger."

Waited through Hara asking which messenger.

Waited through Marek saying he did not care.

Then:

"Then find the fool who carried it."

I opened the door.

"Again?"

Small laugh from Hara.

Not audience.

Just Hara.

Teren let it stand.

I handed the second note to Pell.

Then I stayed to see him open it.

Teren pointed at the door.

Right.

I left.

Third entrance.

By then the brother had admitted he might have touched the key.

The Clerk had established that touching and borrowing were different entries in the household accounts.

Hara was considering murder.

My third note came after a longer exchange.

I almost relaxed too much.

Nessa caught me reading over Marek's shoulder from offstage.

"Ready."

"I am."

"You're not."

She tapped my pocket.

I had put the third note in the wrong inside fold after the second pass.

I checked.

First note gone.

Second note gone.

Third note had slid flat under my pages.

I fixed it.

"Thank you."

"Pocket."

There were relationships built entirely from nouns.

We ran it again.

Then again.

The door stopped being an event.

The notes stayed in order.

I left after delivering them.

My second entrance waited for the actual cue instead of the word messenger.

By the fourth pass, Teren stopped stopping me.

This felt suspicious.

At midday, Rinna came in with bread, two apples, and no new information.

I knew that because nobody asked.

She set the food down.

Marek reached for an apple.

Rinna slapped his hand with a folded handbill.

"Paid performers first."

"I am a performer."

"Not paid yet."

"Technical distinction."

Hara took the better apple.

Of course.

I sat on the stage edge to rest my right leg.

Ordinary stiffness.

Shoulders worked.

Right palm warm from the crutches.

Nothing concerning.

Pell sat one seat below me.

"Messenger."

"Clerk."

He nodded.

We had communicated fully.

Rinna looked at Teren.

"Keep it?"

"Maybe."

I looked at the pages.

"Performance?"

Teren looked at me.

"Eat."

Clear.

We ate.

Nobody said brown coat.

Nobody said lamp glass.

Nobody said front door or back door except Davin, who came through the repaired rear passage and told Jori the back door now shut properly.

Jori went to test it.

It did.

They moved on to the next problem.

By early afternoon, Teren had decided.

"The Missing Key stays."

Marek raised both hands.

"Justice."

Teren ignored him.

Rinna looked at me.

"Messenger."

"I remain."

"Today."

"Yes."

Nessa took the dark-green coat off me, brushed chalk from one sleeve, and handed it back.

Apparently that had been a reset.

The first piece was one Greg was not in.

Good.

I spent it backstage with the coat on a peg and the three message props on Nessa's table.

Marek was in the first piece too.

He came off after one scene sweating under the pale vest.

"Audience good."

"How many?"

"Audience."

"Useful."

"They laughed."

"At you?"

"Near me."

More honest than expected.

Hara came through behind him.

"Not at him."

There it was.

When The Missing Key came up, the set changed quickly.

Brown door frame.

Two chairs.

Small table.

Painted cabinet.

No blue vase.

The missing vase was represented by its absence, which was economical.

Nessa placed the three notes in my coat pocket herself.

"One. Two. Three."

"I know."

"I know you know."

This was somehow worse.

The house quieted.

Hara and Marek began.

From offstage, I could hear the play find its shape.

Marek was better with an audience.

Not because he became smarter.

Because his denial grew more confident every time the room doubted him.

Pell entered with the inventory book.

Hara asked where the key was.

My first cue.

I opened the door.

Not important.

Just a door.

Cross behind Pell.

Stop.

"Message for this house."

Hara took the note.

I left.

The audience barely noticed me.

Good.

Behind the flat, Nessa held up two fingers.

Second note.

I put my hand into the pocket and found the double fold.

The scene moved faster than rehearsal.

That was the first problem.

Pell said, "A messenger."

My body prepared.

Not cue.

Hara asked which messenger.

Marek said he did not care.

"Then find the fool who carried it."

I opened the door.

"Again?"

The audience laughed.

More than Hara had.

Not a lot.

Enough to make the next beat feel longer.

I crossed.

Handed Pell the note.

Then made my mistake.

I stayed.

One beat.

Two.

I wanted to hear the laugh settle.

I wanted to see Pell open the note.

I had spent the whole morning being told not to do exactly this.

Teren was not visible.

He did not need to be.

Hara looked at me.

Not Greg.

Messenger.

She held out one hand.

"Unless you've brought the key too?"

Written line.

My exit was supposed to happen before it.

Because I was still there, the question landed directly on me.

I said my next written line too early.

"No key."

That line belonged to the third entrance.

Pell looked at me.

Marek looked at me.

I knew immediately.

The audience did not.

Hara did.

She looked at my empty hand.

Then at Pell's note.

Then said, "Wonderful. We have acquired a messenger who spoils tomorrow."

The room laughed.

Not a giant explosion.

A good one.

She turned away from me.

That was my exit.

I took it.

Backstage, my face was hot.

Nessa held out one hand.

I stared at her.

"Third note."

Right.

I took it from my pocket and gave it to her.

"No."

She put it back into my hand.

"Keep it."

"I just used the line."

"Not the note."

Correct.

The scene continued.

I had created a problem.

Hara had continued.

Important distinction.

I stood by the door and listened.

Pell read the second note.

The absent master claimed he had sent the key yesterday.

Marek's brother said that proved nothing.

Hara asked what exactly would prove something.

The audience laughed before the next line because they had learned him.

Good.

My third cue approached.

I had already spoken "No key."

The third entrance still existed.

The note still existed.

The remaining lines still existed.

I did not invent a replacement.

Marek admitted that there had once been a blue vase.

Hara became very still.

Pell checked the inventory.

"Blue vase. Hall table."

Hara said, "Where is it?"

Marek said, "Define is."

Big laugh.

Mine came after.

I opened the door.

Third entrance.

The audience recognized me.

A smaller laugh started before I spoke.

I did not wait for it.

"Last message."

Hara looked at me.

"Does it contain the key?"

That was supposed to set up the line I had already used.

No key.

I could not say it again without making the mistake visible.

For half a second, I had nothing.

Then Pell took the note from my hand.

Not a rescue flourish.

He simply took it because the Clerk had taken the previous one.

"Perhaps we should read it."

His line was not written there.

It worked.

Hara turned to him.

The scene moved.

I left.

Pell opened the third note and read the actual message.

"The key is under the blue vase."

Silence.

Hara looked toward the empty table.

Then at Marek.

Marek looked at the empty table too.

"Define under."

The audience broke.

Hara did not hurry.

She walked to him.

Slow.

Held out one hand.

Marek reached into his vest and produced nothing.

Other pocket.

Nothing.

Then he looked toward Nessa's property table offstage as though the missing vase might be hiding there.

Hara said, "Which piece?"

That was written.

It owned the room.

Marek's answer disappeared under the laugh.

He waited.

Good.

Then tried again.

The scene finished with Pell discovering the cabinet had never been locked.

The brass-colored key had belonged to the hall cupboard.

Hara opened the painted cabinet with one finger.

Looked inside.

Looked at Marek.

Closed it.

Curtain.

The audience gave us the kind of applause a short stupid play deserved.

Warm.

Not immortal.

Enough.

Backstage, I took off the green coat.

Nessa checked the pocket before taking it.

All three notes accounted for.

"Good," she said.

I looked at her.

"Don't."

She almost smiled.

Teren found me while the others were resetting.

"You stayed."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"The laugh."

"Yours?"

"Partly."

"Then leave with it."

I nodded.

That was specific enough to hurt.

"I also used the third line."

"Yes."

"Pell continued."

"Yes."

"Hara too."

"Yes."

I waited.

Teren looked at me.

"Again tomorrow if it stays paired."

Not praise.

Not punishment.

A work condition.

"Same role?"

"Unless I change it."

"Useful."

"Don't improve it."

There he was.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I put it away.

No Vale calculation.

No threat calculation.

Money.

The back latch closed cleanly when people began leaving.

I noticed because yesterday it had not.

That was all.

Hara sat on the stage edge retightening the repaired shoe.

I stopped beside her.

"Thank you."

"For what?"

"Continuing."

She looked at me.

Then at Teren across the room.

"Did he make you say that?"

"No."

"Then you're getting strange."

"Already happened."

She nodded.

"Fair."

Marek appeared with the brass-colored key.

Nessa appeared behind him.

He put it down without being asked.

Growth.

Possibly fear.

I left before anyone found more work.

At home, Lyssa was already there.

The brown cloth was still near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt still had three loose threads.

Lyssa had a bowl in front of her and something wrapped in paper near her elbow.

Not beans.

I looked at it.

She looked at me looking.

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You were going to."

"Maybe."

She ate.

I put The Missing Key pages on the table.

"Show?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

I took bread.

"Hara was."

Lyssa nodded as if this confirmed an existing law.

"I was useful."

"Less good."

"Relationship damaged."

She ate another spoonful.

I sat across from her.

No report about lamp glass.

No analysis of doors.

No magic.

No Vale.

After a minute she pointed at the pages.

"Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

"Then don't lose them."

I looked at the title.

The Missing Key.

I looked back at her.

She had already gone back to eating.

I kept the pages where I could see them.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 241

## THE SEALER

The pages for The Missing Key were still on the table in the morning.

That did not make them a schedule.

I knew this because Teren had said, "Again tomorrow if it stays paired," which contained both tomorrow and if, and only one of those words was dangerous.

Lyssa was already gone.

The small paper-wrapped thing from last night was gone too. I noticed because it had been beside her elbow, then stopped thinking about it because objects were allowed to leave our apartment without giving testimony.

The brown cloth had not exercised that right.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads.

At least somebody respected continuity.

I ate bread, drank water, and read the Messenger pages once.

No knock came.

I waited until the bread was gone.

Still no knock.

Theatre had taught me many things, most of them about waiting in the correct place until somebody wanted me. Debt had taught me the opposite.

I folded the pages and left them on the table.

If the play stayed paired, Rinna knew where I lived.

If it did not, I had saved myself a walk.

Either way, Antonius Vale still existed.

The morning was colder than yesterday and brighter. Carrow had the hard clean light that made chipped plaster and old paint look newly accused. A cart of cabbages blocked half the lower lane. The driver was asleep on the seat while three people moved around him with the patient hatred of people who had decided waking him was somehow more trouble.

I went east.

My body had recovered normally from the theatre day. Right hand fine. Shoulders fine. Right leg fine. Residual limb comfortable. Left crutch tip dull and trustworthy.

The Vale office was already open.

The ink-fingered clerk looked up when I came in.

"Bundles?" I asked.

"Later."

"Acknowledgments?"

"Done."

"Excellent. I have successfully arrived for nothing."

He pointed to the far table.

"Seals."

I looked.

A shallow tray held folded outer wrappers, a squat wax pot near a little lamp, two brass stamps, a stack of account copies, and enough string to hang a very small criminal.

"What am I sealing?"

"Outgoing account copies."

"Why?"

He stared at me.

"Because open paper is less private than closed paper."

"Strong system."

"Sit."

I sat.

The chair was the same one I had used before, which meant I already knew which rear leg complained and how far my crutches could lean without falling into the passage.

The clerk put six packets in front of me.

"Not all of them."

"Good."

"Practice row."

"Less good."

He showed me the work.

Each packet already had the account copy inside its outer wrapper. My job was not to decide the account, correct the amount, or interpret any mark I did not know. I checked the outer name and house mark against the top line visible before the final fold. If they matched, I folded the wrapper closed along an existing crease, tied it once around with string, then waited.

The waiting mattered.

The clerk checked the face of the packet and put a short ink stroke beside the house mark.

Only then did I seal the string knot with wax and press the plain office stamp.

"Not the other stamp," he said.

I looked at the second brass stamp.

It had a deeper cut and a small edge mark.

"What does that one do?"

"Not your work."

"Efficient training."

"Good."

I looked at him.

He did not care.

The first packet went badly.

Not catastrophically.

That would have been interesting.

I used too much wax.

The drop spread under the stamp, squeezed past the knot, and made a red-brown lump with one recognizable edge and the general dignity of stepped-on fruit.

The clerk looked at it.

I looked at it.

"Private," I said.

"Ugly."

"Still private."

He took a narrow knife, lifted the wax before it fully hardened, and handed the packet back.

"Less."

"Technical language remains strong here."

"Less."

I used less.

The second seal looked like a seal.

The third looked almost competent.

On the fourth packet I got ahead of myself.

Name matched.

House mark matched.

Fold.

String.

Wax.

The clerk caught my wrist before the stamp came down.

Not hard.

Enough.

"No stroke."

I looked at the packet.

He had not checked it yet.

"Right."

"Why?"

"Because your check and your authority are different things."

He took his hand away.

I disliked how cleanly that sentence fit the office.

"I matched it."

"You did."

"And I still wait."

"Yes."

He checked the face, added the ink stroke, and pushed it back.

I stamped.

No lesson music played.

The packet joined the others.

By the end of six, the seals were all recognizable and none of them had become attached to the table.

The clerk gave me twelve more.

Apparently I had been promoted from dangerous to useful enough.

The office filled around the work.

A woman came in with two account slips and argued about a date. Not loudly. She had the controlled anger of somebody who had already explained herself somewhere else and resented having to own the story again.

The ink-fingered clerk listened, compared her slips to a ledger, then called another clerk over.

Neither asked me.

Good.

A man carrying a narrow crate wanted someone named Sel.

Nobody named Sel was there.

He left the crate anyway.

A boy came for three sealed packets and was told two.

He took two.

Another man stood near Antonius's door for almost ten minutes before being admitted and spent the whole time pretending not to look at anyone else.

Commerce.

I kept tying string.

The work had a rhythm once my hands stopped trying to improve it.

Check name.

Check house mark.

Fold.

Tie.

Wait.

Ink stroke.

Wax.

Stamp.

Set aside.

The clerk checked faster than I worked. That meant I never had to wait long enough to become philosophical.

Excellent design.

I was on the second stack when I found a problem.

The outer wrapper said MARRIS HOUSE, SOUTH WALL.

The visible top line inside said MARRIS HOUSE, SOUTH WALK.

One letter.

Different mark below it too.

I stopped.

The names matched.

The amount was none of my business.

The house did not.

I turned the packet sideways.

The clerk came over.

"What?"

I pointed.

He read it.

Then pulled the account copy free.

"Wrong wrapper."

"Do I win?"

"No."

"Do I at least avoid losing?"

"Continue."

He found the correct wrapper in the stack below.

No hidden conspiracy.

No meaningful connection.

Somebody had put one paper inside another paper.

The world survived.

I tied the correct packet.

Near midday, a boy arrived asking for me.

This one I knew.

Not his name.

His function.

Rinna's handbill boy from yesterday.

He stood in the office doorway looking uncomfortable with the amount of paper surrounding him.

The ink-fingered clerk looked at him.

"Business?"

"Theatre."

The clerk looked at me.

I closed my eyes briefly.

Of course.

The boy said, "Rinna says Teren kept the piece. If you're coming, come now."

There it was.

The if had changed.

Not completely.

Enough.

I looked at the stack in front of me.

Seven packets left in the row.

Another stack behind it.

The wax pot warm.

My hands already in the work.

"Performance?"

The boy shrugged.

"Rehearsal now. Show later."

"Messenger?"

"She said same."

The ink-fingered clerk had gone back to his ledger.

I said, "Can I leave this and come back?"

He looked at the wax.

Then at the packets.

"Today?"

"Yes."

"No."

Not cruel.

Not threatening.

The answer of a man with paper that had to leave today.

"If I go?"

"Someone else finishes."

"Credit?"

He looked at me.

I regretted asking it that way.

Not because money was shameful.

Because I had accidentally asked him to invent a rule for my feelings.

"Antonius credits work completed," he said.

There it was.

No speech.

No punishment.

No promise that my partial stack was worthless either.

The theatre boy shifted in the doorway.

I looked at him.

"Tell Rinna I'm working."

"You aren't coming?"

I looked at the seven packets.

Then at the wax.

Then at the boy.

"No."

He nodded.

No disappointment.

No argument.

He left.

The door closed.

I sat still for one breath longer than necessary.

The clerk said, "Wax."

I looked down.

The drop on my knife was cooling.

"Right."

I reheated it.

That was the choice.

Not dramatic enough for a play.

Debt credit instead of eight lines and three entrances.

I expected to feel more certain after making it.

I did not.

Good.

People who felt certain every time they chose between two useful things were probably lying or rich.

I continued.


The next packet had a house mark I recognized from an earlier acknowledgment stack.

That recognition was almost useful.

My hand started toward the string before I had checked the name.

I stopped it.

Different family.

Same house mark.

I read the line again.

The clerk saw me pause.

"Problem?"

"No."

He waited.

"I knew the mark and almost let that do the rest."

He nodded once.

Not praise.

Not correction either.

I finished the comparison properly and folded the packet.

A few minutes later the boy who had come for two sealed packets returned with one of them.

He held it by the edge.

"Cracked."

The wax had split across the center.

My seal.

I knew it because the stamp sat slightly off level.

The clerk took it, bent the wrapper gently, and the wax opened along the crack.

"Bad?"

I asked.

"Broken."

"Cause?"

He looked at the thin seal.

"Too little."

I stared at him.

"This morning you said less."

"Less than too much."

The office had found a philosophical position after all.

He scraped the old wax away, handed me the packet, and watched while I resealed it with a slightly larger drop.

"That?"

"Yes."

The boy took it.

No one recorded my journey from too much wax to not enough wax and back toward mediocrity.

Civilization would have to survive without the chart.


The woman with the date problem left with one corrected slip and the same expression she had entered with.

The crate for Sel disappeared while I was looking at wax.

Antonius came out once, saw me, saw the sealed packets, and went back into his room.

I received no medal.

By early afternoon my right hand had begun to feel the fine repetitive work differently from crutch use. Thumb and forefinger warm from string. A small wax smear dried near one nail. Shoulders fine because I had been seated. Right leg stiff enough that I stood during a natural break and walked the length of the room twice.

The clerk did not interpret this.

Neither did I.

I drank water.

Sat again.

Finished the row.

Then the stack.

The last packet had a thick fold and fought the string.

I pulled harder.

The knot shifted sideways.

The clerk looked over.

"Again."

"It is tied."

"Badly."

"It has commitment."

"Again."

I untied it.

The second knot sat flat.

Wax.

Stamp.

Done.

The clerk counted the finished packets without asking me to count with him.

Then carried them to Antonius.

I cleaned the knife and put the string ends into the scrap bowl because leaving them everywhere was apparently how offices became theatres.

Antonius came out with the account book.

He opened it to my line.

I saw the current figure before he wrote the new credit.

Still not small.

Smaller after.

He wrote.

Closed the book.

"Useful?"

I asked.

He looked at the sealed packets.

"They are leaving."

This family of answers had infected the city.

"Credit recorded?"

"Yes."

"Good."

Antonius's eyebrow moved.

I regretted nothing.

Outside, afternoon had already started bending toward evening.

I could have gone home.

Instead I went to the theatre.

Not because I thought they needed me now.

That possibility had already been answered by time.

I wanted to know.

The front doors were open and people were coming out.

So the performance had happened.

That answered one question before I entered.

Inside, the house was becoming a room again.

Audience voices thinning.

Benches visible.

Rinna at the side with coins and a small ledger.

Nessa carrying two costume pieces toward the rear passage.

Jori lifting the brown door frame with Davin.

The Missing Key had survived.

I had not been required.

For one stupid second that hurt.

Then Marek came offstage with the brass-colored key in one hand.

He saw me.

"Dead."

"What?"

"You died."

"I was at Vale."

"Debt death."

"Was the play good?"

He considered.

"Hara was."

Accurate.

"Messenger?"

Marek pointed with the key.

"Venn."

Good.

Venn existed.

The world had not waited for my body to become available.

Nessa reached Marek and held out her hand.

He put the key into it immediately.

She looked at me.

"You missed coat."

"I noticed."

"Venn fit."

I looked at the green coat over her arm.

"Better?"

"Different."

That was probably the kindest answer available.

Teren came down the house aisle.

He saw me.

"Vale?"

"Yes."

"Fine."

I waited.

He kept walking.

I turned.

"That's it?"

He stopped.

"What do you want?"

"I don't know."

"Then yes."

Fair.

"Did Venn do Messenger?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

"He left."

That landed exactly where he intended it to.

I smiled despite myself.

"Excellent."

"Tomorrow, different pair."

"So The Missing Key is done?"

"For now."

No punishment.

No reclaimed role.

No schedule.

It had run because it was useful today.

I had not been there.

Somebody else had done the work.

That was theatre too.

Rinna finished with the ledger and came over.

"The boy found you."

"Yes."

"You stayed."

"Yes."

"Vale?"

"Credit."

She nodded.

"Good."

I pointed at her.

"No."

She almost smiled.

No theatre pay waited for me because I had not done theatre work.

This also seemed offensively fair.

I helped nobody with strike.

Not because I was angry.

Because the workday I had actually chosen had already used my hands, and Jori and Davin had the frame.

I said goodbye to nobody formally.

Marek was arguing with Pell about whether a key could be considered missing if the wrong key had been found.

Hara had taken off the repaired shoe and was rubbing one heel through her stocking.

Nessa had the green coat.

Teren had another set of pages.

The theatre continued.

I left.

On the way home I bought a small hot pastry filled with mashed roots.

I paid for it.

No debt arithmetic.

I had just spent the day reducing the debt and was still allowed to be hungry.

The pastry burned my tongue.

Justice.

At home, Lyssa was there before me.

The brown cloth remained near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

The paper-wrapped item was not on the table.

The beans were still wherever beans went when they acquired plans.

Lyssa looked at the empty space in my hands.

"Theatre?"

"Yes."

"Show?"

"Yes."

She waited.

"Without me."

That got her attention.

"Why?"

"Vale had work."

"Good?"

I sat down.

"Depends who you ask."

"Who?"

"Vale credited me."

She nodded.

"Theatre replaced me."

Another nod.

Neither answer seemed to trouble her.

I took bread.

"Venn did it."

"Did what?"

"Messenger."

She had already lost interest in the administrative structure of my disappointment.

Probably healthy.

I ate.

After a while she said, "You chose."

"Yes."

"Want different?"

I thought about the green coat.

The warm wax.

Teren saying fine.

The account figure getting smaller.

Hara probably being good without me.

"No."

Lyssa nodded.

Then reached for my pastry.

I moved it.

Her hand followed.

"You ate," I said.

"So did you."

"Earlier."

She looked at me.

I had made a tactical error.

I tore the pastry in half.

She took the larger piece.

Of course.

I ate mine before the office could send anyone to seal it.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 242

## THE WAITER

Lyssa was still home when I woke, which meant I had misunderstood the sound of the door.

She was standing beside the table with her hair half tied back and one shoe on, looking through the small jar where we kept salt.

This was not promising.

"How bad?"

She tipped the jar toward me.

Enough white crystals slid down the side to insult us.

"Technical answer?"

"No."

"Low."

"Good."

"That was not good."

"I know."

The brown cloth remained near the wall with the same narrow fold and the same three pins. My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads. The beans were still absent. The paper-wrapped thing from two nights ago was still absent. Nothing had returned merely because I was awake enough to notice it.

Lyssa put the salt jar down.

"I'm going north."

"Work?"

She looked at me.

"Right. Stupid question."

"Yes."

I tore yesterday's bread in half. It resisted enough that I had to brace it against the table.

Lyssa watched.

"Buy bread."

"I had identified that."

"Salt."

"I had not identified that."

"Now you have."

She finished tying her hair.

"Anything else?"

"No."

That should have been manageable.

Bread.

Salt.

Two objects.

Neither magical.

Neither theatrical.

Neither attached to Antonius Vale.

I had recently completed work involving names, house marks, string, wax, authorization, and enough procedure to make two household purchases feel offensively easy.

Lyssa took the smaller half of the bread.

I stared.

"You assigned me an errand and taxed the starting provisions."

"You'll buy bread."

"With what strength?"

She bit into it.

"Old bread."

Then she left.

No Hessa note appeared under the door.

No theatre boy knocked.

Nobody from Vale arrived carrying a warm pot of wax and a threat about incomplete packets.

The morning remained mine.

This felt suspicious for almost five minutes.

Then I ate the other half of the bread, washed, checked my left crutch tip because it had become boring enough to deserve respect, and went west.

The air was colder than the day before, but the sky had lost the hard brightness. Clouds flattened the light over Carrow and made the painted signs look older. The stones near the drain channels were damp without being wet enough to justify caution, which was the worst kind of damp.

My shoulders felt normal after yesterday's seated work. Right hand normal. Thumb and forefinger no longer warm from string. Residual limb comfortable. Right leg fine. The left crutch tip held.

I bought bread first.

This was important because bread had witnesses.

The baker gave me a loaf with a split along the top and named the price. I paid it. He handed it across.

One task completed.

I put the loaf into a narrow cloth bag and hooked the bag high against my right side where it would not swing into the crutch.

Salt next.

I did not buy salt next.

This was Hara's fault.

I saw her outside a cobbler near the west steps with one shoe on and the other in her hand.

Technically, I could have continued walking.

Socially, she saw me.

"Greg."

I stopped.

"That sounded accusatory."

"It is."

She held up the shoe.

I recognized it.

Left shoe. The sole had separated near the toe a few days ago. She had complained about it over soup while everyone else offered competing cobblers and Marek offered a crime against leather.

The sole was attached now.

The heel apparently had declared independence.

"What happened?"

"The repair worked."

"That sounds terrible."

"It is."

She turned the shoe and pressed one thumb inside near the heel.

"I wore it yesterday."

"I saw."

"You saw my shoe?"

"I saw you take it off after the show."

"Strange."

"I was in the room."

"Still strange."

Fair.

The cobbler appeared in the doorway behind her. He was narrow, gray-haired, and had a leather apron with enough cut marks to count as writing.

"It is not the sole," he said.

Hara turned.

"I know."

"You brought it back like the sole failed."

"I brought it back because the shoe hurts."

"That is a different complaint."

"I have one foot in it. I know."

The cobbler looked at me.

I raised one hand.

"Not involved."

"Good."

Hara said, "Coward."

"I learned from professionals."

The cobbler took the shoe back from her and pressed the heel between both thumbs.

"The new stitch pulled this side tighter."

"You stitched it."

"Yes."

"So fix it."

"I am."

"Excellent."

He disappeared inside.

Hara looked down at her stockinged left foot.

Then at me.

Then at the street.

"How long?"

The cobbler called from inside, "Half an hour."

Hara closed her eyes.

I should have left.

I still needed salt.

Half an hour was not an obligation.

Then Hara said, "Sit."

I looked at the bench beside the cobbler's door.

"Was that to me?"

"You're the only person here with two crutches."

"That does not establish authority."

"Sit."

I sat.

Hara sat beside me and stretched her left foot out so the stocking did not touch the damp stone.

For several seconds neither of us spoke.

This was new.

At theatre, silence usually meant somebody had forgotten a cue or Teren was about to make the silence our fault.

Here it meant a cobbler had Hara's shoe.

A cart rolled down the west steps too fast and received three separate insults from people who had to move. A woman carrying bundled reeds stopped at the corner to argue with a man selling onions. Someone above us shook a rug from a window and immediately apologized to nobody.

Hara said, "You missed yesterday."

There it was.

Not accusation.

Fact.

"Vale had work."

"I know."

"How?"

"Rinna said."

"Of course she did."

"Venn survived."

Hara looked at me.

"Did you think he wouldn't?"

"No."

I paused.

"Not exactly."

She nodded.

No rescue.

No reassurance.

Good.

I looked toward the cobbler's open door.

"Did the play survive?"

"Yes."

"Was Marek still innocent?"

"No."

"He wasn't innocent when I was there."

"He disagreed."

"Of course."

Hara watched a man carry two baskets down the steps.

"Venn left after his lines."

I looked at her.

She did not smile.

"Everyone is becoming hostile."

"Maybe you should learn."

"I was told not to improve it."

"Then don't."

That was all the theatre talk we managed.

The cobbler remained inside.

I remembered salt.

"I have an errand."

Hara looked at my bread bag.

"You bought bread."

"One of two."

"Salt?"

I stared at her.

She smiled.

"Lyssa?"

"Yes."

"Go."

"You ordered me to sit."

"I changed my mind."

"Strong leadership."

I stood.

Hara stood too, then looked down at her stockinged foot.

"No."

I looked at her.

She sat again.

"Correct."

The salt stall was visible from the top of the next lane if I took the shorter way around the dye carts. I could have gone, bought salt, returned, and still spent most of the cobbler's half hour listening to Hara insult leather.

Instead I said, "I'll get yours if you need anything."

She looked at me.

I regretted the sentence because it sounded more useful than intended.

"Salt?"

"No."

"Bread?"

"No."

"Then I have successfully offered nothing."

"Wait."

I waited.

She dug into the small purse at her belt and held out a copper.

"Roasted peas. Stall by the red awning."

I looked down the lane.

"Why?"

"Because I'm hungry."

"Excellent reason."

"Small bag."

"How small?"

She pointed at the coin.

"This small."

I took it.

Now I had three tasks.

Dangerous.

The salt seller was where I remembered. I bought a small paper twist of coarse salt and put it carefully beside the loaf where bread could not become seasoned through structural failure.

Two tasks completed.

The roasted-pea stall had a red awning and a woman with one arm elbow-deep in a basket of dried pods.

"Small bag," I said.

She looked at the copper.

"For Hara?"

I stopped.

"That was fast."

She pointed past me.

Hara was visible at the cobbler's bench.

"Less impressive."

The woman scooped roasted peas into a folded paper cone.

"She usually buys two."

"She gave me one copper."

"Then she gets one."

"No loyalty discount?"

"No."

"Healthy business."

She handed me the cone.

I brought it back.

Hara took it and immediately ate three peas.

"You got salt."

"Yes."

"Good."

"Stop saying that."

She held out the cone.

I took one pea.

This was apparently my courier fee.

The cobbler came out ten minutes later with Hara's shoe and a strip of dark leather folded over one finger.

He handed her the shoe.

"Try."

Hara put it on.

Her face changed.

Not much.

Enough.

"Different."

"Better?"

"Different."

I looked at her.

She noticed.

"Don't."

I said nothing.

The cobbler crouched in front of her and pressed the heel while she stood.

"Walk."

Hara took six steps along the flat section beside the shops, turned, and came back.

"Less."

"Good."

She looked at me.

I pointed at the cobbler.

"His word."

"Still hate it."

"Now you understand."

The cobbler ignored us.

"Walk farther. Come back if it bites."

"Bites?"

"You know."

"I know."

Hara paid him.

Then she looked down the street.

I had bread.

I had salt.

My errands were complete.

I could go home.

Hara said, "Walk."

I stared.

"You have both shoes now."

"Yes."

"So why am I involved?"

"If it starts hurting again, I need a witness."

"To what?"

"My murder."

"Of the cobbler?"

"He'll know."

The cobbler, still in the doorway, said, "Go away."

We went away.

Hara set the pace without asking whether I could match it. She already knew how I moved from months of stages, passages, streets after work, and one cookshop where everyone had paid for their own bowl. She did not slow dramatically or take my bag. She simply did not choose the broken edge of the lane when the middle was clear.

We walked south from the west steps, then turned toward the lower market.

"Still?"

I asked after one street.

"Fine."

"That sounded disappointed."

"I paid for a complaint."

"You paid for less pain."

"Less interesting."

A child ran between us chasing a hoop.

Hara moved left.

I moved right.

The hoop survived.

Nobody learned anything.

At the lower market, Hara stopped at a vegetable stall.

I stopped because she did.

The seller was an older man with a nose like a badly carved handle and hands blackened with soil.

He looked at Hara.

"Late."

"I was buying my own foot back."

He looked down.

"Again?"

"Different problem."

"Same shoe."

Hara pointed at him.

"Don't."

He grinned and started gathering greens without asking what she wanted.

I watched.

"Regular?"

Hara looked at me.

"No. Hostage."

The seller put two bundles on the cloth.

"One."

Hara pushed one back.

"Two."

"One."

"You always take two."

"Not today."

He looked at me.

"She always difficult?"

"About vegetables?"

"Everything."

Hara stepped on my right crutch tip.

Not hard.

Enough.

I leaned away.

"Violence."

"Accuracy."

The seller laughed and took her coin.

He did not ask who I was.

Healthy.

Hara tucked the greens under one arm.

"Still fine?"

I asked.

She took two steps.

"Yes."

We kept walking.

The roasted peas did not survive to the next corner.

Hara folded the empty paper cone into a small square and put it in her pocket.

"You keep trash?"

"Until I see a rubbish barrel."

"Responsible."

"You sound surprised."

"I have seen Marek."

"Low standard."

At a cookshop near the lower lane, steam rolled through the open front and smelled like onions, beans, and something peppered enough to make my nose hurt pleasantly.

Hara stopped.

"Food."

"I have bread."

"That is an object."

"It is food."

"Later."

I could have gone home.

Again.

I did not.

Inside, the room was narrow and already half full. Hara took a table near the wall after a man lifted his coat from the second stool without being asked.

She sat.

I put my crutches against the wall where they would not trip the server.

No one moved a chair for me.

Also good.

A woman came over.

Hara said, "Bean."

The woman looked at me.

"Same."

Hara looked at me.

"You don't know what it costs."

"I have money."

"Vale ruined you."

"Rinna said that first."

"Then listen."

The bowls came with beans, chopped greens, and a piece of dark bread soaked at one edge.

I paid for mine.

Hara paid for hers.

The day remained financially complicated enough without merging lunch.

For a while we ate.

Hara was different when nobody was waiting for her to speak.

Less still than onstage, somehow.

At theatre, she could hold a room by doing almost nothing because everyone was watching. Here nobody watched. She bent over the bowl and ate like somebody who had spent the morning arguing with a shoe.

A man at the next table recognized her.

Not me.

"Hara?"

She looked up.

He frowned as if checking his own memory.

"The widow."

"The one with the ditch?"

He smiled.

Hara closed her eyes.

"Yes."

"Good show."

"Thank you."

He went back to his bowl.

I waited.

Hara looked at me.

I smiled.

"No."

"I said nothing."

"You were about to."

"I was going to ask whether justice improved drainage."

She pointed her spoon at me.

"Don't."

I ate beans.

Her face slowly betrayed her.

She laughed once.

Quiet.

Then went back to eating.

That was enough.

He did not ask her to repeat the line or perform anything for him. He went back to lunch.

Outside, we walked another two streets because Hara wanted to test the heel before going wherever she was going next.

I did not ask where.

This showed significant growth.

The shoe stayed on.

The heel did not bite.

At the crossing near South Steps, Hara stopped.

"This is me."

I looked down both streets.

"Which?"

"Not your business."

"Excellent."

She adjusted the greens under her arm.

"Salt."

I looked at my bread bag.

The paper twist was still there.

"Yes."

"Good."

I pointed at her.

She walked away before I could improve the argument.

By the time I reached home, my shoulders felt used and my right palm was warm from the longer walk. Right leg ordinarily tired. Residual limb comfortable. Nothing injured. Nothing new.

The room was empty.

Brown cloth.

Three pins.

Same fold.

Damaged shirt.

Three threads.

Same shelf.

I put the bread on the table.

Then the salt beside it.

Two tasks completed despite social interference.

I considered this an excellent record and did not write it down.

Lyssa came back late enough that I had already cut the loaf.

She carried one small apple and a wedge of cheese wrapped in cloth.

She looked at the table.

"Bread."

"Yes."

"Salt."

I put one hand over my heart.

"Both."

"Good."

I stared at her.

She bit the apple.

"You've had a difficult day."

"You have no idea."

She looked at my face, then at the bread.

"Theatre?"

"No."

"Vale?"

"No."

"Magic?"

"No."

That exhausted the usual categories.

Lyssa waited.

"Hara's shoe."

She chewed.

"What?"

"Exactly."

I told her the sole repair had tightened the heel, the cobbler had adjusted it, and Hara had made me walk until the shoe failed to murder her.

Lyssa listened to maybe half.

"Did it?"

"Did what?"

"Murder her."

"No."

"Good."

I pointed at her again.

She took another bite of apple.

I gave up.

We ate bread with the cheese and split the apple badly enough that Lyssa got more apple.

The brown cloth remained near the wall.

The shirt remained on the shelf.

No one asked anything of me for the rest of the night.

It was almost enough to make waiting feel like a job.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 243

## THE SAMPLE

Hessa's note was under the door.

This improved the morning and made it more expensive.

Not in money.

In attention.

I picked it up before I put weight on the left crutch and read it standing.

AFTER MIDDAY.
SAME ROOM.
FAR ONLY.
EAT FIRST.

I read the third line again.

FAR ONLY.

The last session had been INTERMEDIATE four times in a row. One response. Three non-responses. Clean control every time.

Apparently Hessa had chosen what came next.

Lyssa was already gone.

The brown cloth remained near the wall.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

The beans remained absent. The small paper-wrapped thing remained absent. Hara's shoe was somewhere in Carrow attached to Hara, which was the correct place for it and no longer my concern.

I put Hessa's note beside the salt jar.

Then moved it away from the salt jar because yesterday had taught me that salt was capable of acquiring obligations.

I ate bread and the last of the cheese.

EAT FIRST had become a recurring insult only because Hessa knew me.

Fair.

There was no theatre call.

No Vale runner.

No reason to invent work before midday because a note had already given the day a shape.

I washed the bowl, checked the crutch tip, and spent part of the morning doing almost nothing.

That was harder than it sounded.

I sharpened the kitchen knife a little better than last time. I stopped before improvement became damage. I sat by the window. A man across the street tried to lower a rolled rug from an upper window with a rope, discovered the knot was not attached to the rug, and had to come downstairs anyway.

Carrow provided education freely.

By late morning I was hungry again.

I ate another piece of bread.

Hessa would have been unbearable if I arrived hungry after being told twice in four lines not to.

The walk to her room felt familiar enough that I stopped thinking about each turn.

That was useful.

The room still did not feel familiar.

Hessa had the board set where it belonged. The glass shield was in place. The target and control fins were on their marks. The chair stood on the same floor lines. INTERMEDIATE and FAR remained marked where they had been.

Nothing new had appeared because I was curious.

Hessa was seated at the table reading her own notes.

She looked up.

"Eat?"

"Twice."

"Good."

"I dislike that word now."

"Sit."

That was worse.

I put my crutches where they usually went and sat.

My body baseline was ordinary. Shoulders fine. Right hand fine. Right leg fine. Residual limb comfortable. Left crutch tip still boring.

Hessa looked at me for several seconds.

"What is the question?"

I looked at FAR.

"You tell me."

"I wrote it down."

"You wrote FAR ONLY."

"Yes."

"That is a condition, not a question."

One corner of her mouth moved.

Possibly approval.

Possibly gas.

"Then answer."

I looked at the marks again.

Last time, four INTERMEDIATE attempts. Same direction. Same apparatus. One target response.

Before that, the alternating sessions had shown INTERMEDIATE responding more often than FAR under the current setup, though not reliably. FAR had responded cleanly before. It had also failed plenty.

I said, "You held intermediate fixed because alternating might have been hiding something about sequence."

"Close enough."

"Now you hold far fixed."

"Why?"

"To see whether clean FAR response still occurs when we stop alternating it with INTERMEDIATE."

"Better."

I waited.

"That is the question?"

"Yes."

It felt offensively small.

Which usually meant Hessa liked it.

"So not range."

"No."

"Not finding the farthest point."

"No."

"Not changing distance."

"No."

"Not changing direction."

"No."

"Not increasing effort."

"No."
