"Not testing whether I can make FAR behave like INTERMEDIATE."

Hessa looked at me.

"Especially not that."

"Good. I wanted the full collection."

She slid the record page toward herself.

"Same room. Same board. Same shield. Same target and control. Same body geometry. FAR. AWAY."

"Four?"

"Four."

"Break after two?"

"Yes."

The shape mirrored the last session.

That was the point.

Hessa checked the floor marks anyway.

I knew better than to be offended by this now.

Memory was not measurement.

Neither was familiarity.

She checked the glass, then the target and control. I watched her confirm what I thought I already knew.

Nothing had shifted.

"Minimal first," she said.

I placed my right forearm where she wanted it.

No shortcuts.

No independent draw because I had done this thirty-four times under supervision and could imagine the shape of the next ten.

That did not make the thirty-fifth mine.

Hessa watched.

I drew.

Clean.

Small.

Controlled.

No strange symptom. No pain. No movement outside the expected body work.

Hessa marked it.

Thirty-five supervised minimal draws.

Then deliberate shaping.

I held the form she had allowed, narrow and familiar, with no expansion and no experiment inside the experiment.

The first few seconds felt too easy.

That made me suspicious.

I corrected nothing because nothing had actually gone wrong.

The old urge was still there, to improve a success while it was happening.

I let it be what it was.

Hessa marked the completion.

Thirty-one supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Then she reset the page.

"External."

FAR.

AWAY.

Attempt fifty.

I settled into the same position.

The far target looked small enough that my eyes wanted to make it important.

I did not.

Hessa gave the start.

I worked.

Nothing moved.

Target still.

Control still.

No twitch I could defend.

Hessa waited the usual amount of time before checking.

"No observed response," she said.

"Control unchanged."

She marked both.

Attempt fifty-one.

Same condition.

Same direction.

Same everything we could reasonably keep the same.

I expected less this time.

That was information about me, not the apparatus.

I did not tell the target.

Hessa gave the start.

I worked.

Nothing.

Again.

Target still.

Control unchanged.

Two FAR attempts.

Two clean non-responses.

Hessa marked them and closed the record page.

"Break."

"I feel fine."

"Good."

I stared at her.

She stared back.

I stood.

My right leg had begun to stiffen from sitting, not enough to matter and enough to exist. I took the crutches and walked the short permitted circuit inside the room.

Hessa poured water.

For herself.

I looked at the cup.

She drank.

"This profession lacks hospitality."

"You ate twice."

"That was at home."

"Successful planning."

I sat again after the stiffness eased.

She was already looking at the page.

I tried not to.

Failed.

"Two nothing."

"Yes."

"Which matches a great deal of FAR."

"Yes."

"But not all of it."

"No."

I looked at the far mark.

"What would four nothing mean?"

"Four nothing."

"You enjoy this too much."

"No."

"You answer too quickly for someone suffering."

Hessa set the cup down.

"If all four are clean non-responses, that adds four clean non-responses at FAR under this fixed condition."

"And if one moves?"

"Then one moves."

"Profound."

"You keep asking before the record exists."

That was fair.

Annoying.

We waited the rest of the break.

Hessa rechecked my hand and forearm.

Normal warmth from work, no pain, no weakness, no tingling.

Residual limb comfortable.

Right leg better after walking.

"Continue?"

"Yes."

She reopened the page.

Attempt fifty-two.

FAR.

AWAY.

I knew exactly how little I was supposed to change.

That knowledge did not make sameness easy.

The body always wanted to help.

Shoulder slightly higher.

Breath held a little longer.

Hand tightening because the last two had done nothing.

I noticed the hand first.

I loosened it before the start.

Hessa saw.

"Ready?"

"Now."

She gave the start.

I worked.

For a moment, nothing.

Then the far paper fin moved.

Small.

Clean enough that I did not need to argue with my eyes.

The target shifted AWAY and settled.

No obvious rotation that I could see.

The control remained still.

I stopped because the attempt had ended, not because I wanted to stare at it until it became a theory.

Hessa was already checking.

She looked at the target mark.

Then the control.

Then the glass.

Then me.

"Small response," she said.

"Net away."

"Control unchanged."

I exhaled.

Hessa marked it.

I smiled.

She noticed.

"Don't."

"I said nothing."

"You were about to."

Apparently everyone in Carrow had learned this technique.

"FAR moved."

"Yes."

"Clean."

"Under the current apparatus, yes."

"Again."

"No."

"I meant the fact."

"I know."

I looked at the page.

Attempt fifty-two.

A clean FAR response.

Not the first.

Not reliable.

Still real.

"One out of three."

Hessa said nothing.

Right.

There was still another attempt.

Attempt fifty-three.

This was where my mind became unhelpful.

The target had moved once.

Now I expected it to move again.

Or I expected the expectation to ruin it.

Or I expected myself to think about expecting it, which was worse.

Hessa watched me.

"You're doing something."

"Thinking."

"Stop doing it with your face."

"That seems outside the experimental restrictions."

"Ready?"

I looked at my hand.

Loose.

Shoulder down.

Breathing normal enough.

"Yes."

Start.

I worked.

Nothing.

The target remained still.

The control remained still.

No second response arrived to make the first easier to explain.

Hessa checked.

"No observed response."

"Control unchanged."

She marked attempt fifty-three.

Then she put the pencil down.

I looked at the page.

Four FAR attempts.

One response.

Three non-responses.

I laughed once.

Hessa looked at me.

"What?"

"That's rude."

"What is?"

"Last session."

She waited.

"Four INTERMEDIATE. One response."

"Yes."

"Today. Four FAR. One response."

"Yes."

I pointed between the two pages.

"Same."

"No."

The answer came so fast I nearly laughed again.

"One out of four."

"Yes."

"One out of four."

"Yes."

"Those are extremely similar fractions."

"They are the same fraction."

"Thank you."

"They are not the same record."

I leaned back.

"Explain the insult."

Hessa pulled the older sheets closer.

"Because these four attempts do not erase the attempts before them."

"I know that."

"You are trying to make them."

"I am trying to admire symmetry."

"Admire it quietly."

She ran one finger down the earlier marks.

INTERMEDIATE responses across more than one session.

FAR responses too, but fewer in the accumulated current-apparatus record.

Clean non-responses in both.

A contaminated attempt where target and control had both moved, still counted as an attempt and not counted as clean target-specific evidence.

Different orders.

Now one fixed INTERMEDIATE block.

One fixed FAR block.

Hessa said, "If I take only the last eight attempts because they make a neat pair, I have selected the part of the record I like."

"One and one."

"Inside two groups of four."

"Exactly."

"Inside a longer record."

I sighed.

"Fractions do not get to be beautiful here."

"They can be beautiful. They cannot outrank the rest of the page."

That was probably the nicest thing Hessa had ever said about fractions.

I looked again at attempt fifty-two.

"So what changed?"

"FAR produced another clean observed response."

"Which establishes?"

"That FAR response can recur under the current apparatus."

I waited.

"Reliably?"

"No."

"Range?"

"No."

"Distance stops mattering?"

"No."

"INTERMEDIATE and FAR are equivalent?"

"No."

"One in four?"

"In this block."

"Not a rate."

"Correct."

I hated being trained this well.

"The apparent distance difference?"

"Still supported by the accumulated record."

"Even though the two fixed blocks match."

"Yes."

"Because the fixed blocks are part of the accumulated record, not replacements for it."

Hessa looked at me.

"Yes."

I had stolen her line before she could use it.

Small victory.

She wrote for a while.

I flexed my fingers.

Ordinary warmth in the forearm.

No pain.

No tingling.

No weakness.

No symptom worth promoting into plot.

"What next?" I asked.

Hessa did not answer immediately.

Good sign.

Or bad.

With Hessa those were often identical.

She looked at the records again.

"Nothing selected."

"You had five chapters."

She looked up.

"I had a life."

Right.

That landed without needing improvement.

I nodded.

She went back to the page.

I waited.

Eventually she said, "There are questions."

"There are always questions."

"Yes."

"But no next experiment."

"Not yet."

No reward.

No expanded permission.

No new object.

No direction change.

No closer or farther mark.

The room remained exactly as restrictive as when I entered.

Only the record had grown.

That should have been less satisfying than it was.

It wasn't.

I collected my crutches.

Hessa handed me no practice assignment because practice remained prohibited.

She handed me no target because targets remained hers.

She handed me no theory because the record did not owe me one.

At the door I looked back at FAR.

"One out of four."

Hessa did not look up.

"Leave."

I left.

Outside, Carrow had warmed by perhaps one degree and become windier out of spite.

I bought nothing.

This felt financially responsible until I remembered I was simply not hungry yet.

By the time I reached home, my shoulders felt used from the walk and my right forearm still carried mild warmth from the session. Right hand worked normally on the crutch. Right leg fine after moving. Residual limb comfortable. Left tip normal.

The room was empty.

Brown cloth.

Three pins.

Same fold.

Shirt.

Three threads.

Same shelf.

No beans.

No paper-wrapped return.

The universe resisted bookkeeping.

I put Hessa's note on the table and sat.

For several minutes I did nothing useful.

Then I made the mistake of drawing eight small marks on a scrap of ordinary paper.

Not magic.

Just ink.

Four marks on one side.

Four on the other.

One mark in each group darker.

I looked at them.

Same.

Hessa's voice existed in my head without permission.

Not the same record.

I turned the paper over.

Problem solved.

Lyssa came home after dark carrying nothing I could identify as my business.

She saw Hessa's note.

"Magic?"

"Yes."

She took off her outer layer and hung it.

"Good?"

I considered several answers.

"One moved."

She looked at me.

"Far?"

"Yes."

That was enough to make her pause.

"Good?"

"There it is again."

"What?"

"The word."

Lyssa sat down.

I told her the short version because she had not asked for the long one.

Four FAR attempts.

One response.

Three nothing.

Control unchanged every time.

Same as the last INTERMEDIATE block if all you looked at was one out of four.

Not the same as the whole record.

Lyssa listened while unwrapping bread she had apparently acquired somewhere without requiring my assistance.

When I finished, she tore a piece off.

"Moved once."

"Yes."

She handed me bread.

I took it.

"Still don't know."

"Correct."

She nodded.

That was the entire analysis she intended to provide.

Healthy.

I ate.

After a while I said, "Hessa says the fractions can be beautiful."

Lyssa looked at me.

"Did she?"

"Approximately."

"No."

"Emotionally."

"No."

I ate more bread.

The brown cloth remained folded.

The shirt remained damaged.

The note remained on the table.

Fifty-three external attempts now existed whether I liked their shape or not.

One far movement had made the page more interesting.

It had not made it simpler.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 244

## THE SCRAPER

There was blue paint on the boy's sleeve.

That was how I knew the theatre wanted labor before he said anything.

He stood outside our door with one hand on the frame and the expression of someone who had already climbed too many stairs for other people's employment.

"Rinna," he said.

"Of course."

"Before midday if you're free."

I looked at his sleeve.

"Blue?"

He looked too.

"Davin."

That explained less than he thought.

"What happened?"

"Wall."

"Better."

He shrugged.

"Bring clothes you don't care about."

Then he left.

I closed the door.

Lyssa had already gone. No note. No customer. No explanation.

The brown cloth remained where it had been.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf with three loose threads.

That created a problem.

I had just been told to wear clothes I did not care about, and the shirt I cared about least was also the shirt most likely to become a pile if I gave it additional work.

I put on the other one.

The Hessa note from yesterday still sat on the table. FAR ONLY. EAT FIRST.

Done.

The counts in my head were thirty-five, thirty-one, fifty-three.

They could stay there.

One clean far response out of four had not changed any rule. It had not created practice. It had not created range. It had not created a reason to spend the morning staring at paper.

Theatre had created a reason to leave.

I ate.

Outside, Carrow was gray without committing to rain. The stones still held yesterday's dust, but the air had the wet smell that meant somebody, somewhere, was about to blame the sky for something.

My body was ordinary after the Hessa session. Right forearm normal again. Shoulders fine. Right leg fine. Residual limb comfortable. Left crutch tip still boring.

I took the lower route because the upper lane had two wagons trying to occupy the same width of street.

They were discussing geometry loudly.

I let them solve it without me.

The theatre's front doors were open, but the stage was dark.

I heard scraping before I saw anybody.

Not rehearsal scraping.

Real scraping.

A dry, ugly rhythm came from the rear yard.

Shhk.

Shhk.

Pause.

Shhk.

I went around.

Two wall flats stood on trestles in the courtyard with their painted faces turned toward the light. One had been part of the blue-trimmed merchant room. The other had a pale cream surface with a brown rectangle painted where a door had once pretended to exist.

Both looked diseased.

Paint had blistered in thin scales across the lower halves. One blue strip had lifted enough that I could see another color beneath it.

Green.

Davin sat on an upturned bucket with a broad dull scraper in one hand.

Jori stood at the far end marking short chalk lines around sections of paint.

Rinna was at the rear door with a ledger under one arm.

She looked at me.

"Free?"

"Currently."

"Good."

I hated the word now.

She pointed at the flats.

"Those need to be usable tomorrow."

"As walls?"

"That is the ambition."

"What are they tomorrow?"

"Kitchen."

I looked at the blue trim.

"Blue kitchen."

"Not after Davin."

Davin raised the scraper.

"I improve."

Jori said, "He removes."

"Improvement by subtraction."

Rinna looked at me.

"You know how to scrape paint?"

"Yes."

Jori said, "No."

I looked at him.

"I know the concept."

"That's different."

Fair.

Rinna went back inside before the argument could become billable.

Jori handed me a second scraper.

It had a wooden handle polished dark by hands and a blade with one corner slightly rounder than the other.

"Loose only."

"I understand loose."

"No, you understand attacking."

"I have never attacked a wall."

Davin looked at the blue flat.

"Give him time."

Jori tapped one of his chalk lines.

"Anything outside the marks comes off if it's lifting. Inside stays unless I say."

"Why?"

"Because we can use some of the old color under the new coat."

I crouched enough to look at the edge.

The blue trim was not one layer.

Blue sat over cream.

Cream sat over something dark red.

At one chipped corner, a thin black line curved under all three.

"How old is this?"

Jori shrugged.

"Wall."

"That's not an age."

"It was here before you."

Davin said, "Older than Greg."

"Most furniture is."

"Not Marek."

"Marek is not furniture."

Jori looked toward the building.

"Debatable."

I sat on a low stool so I could work the lower half without leaning over my crutches. They rested against the courtyard wall where nobody needed the lane.

Jori showed me the angle.

Flatter than I expected.

Not digging.

Slide under what had already lifted and let the bad layer choose to leave.

My first pass produced nothing.

"Good," Jori said.

"I did nothing."

"Exactly."

Hostile trade.

I tried again.

The blade caught a loose edge.

A strip of blue paint lifted, curled, and came away in a piece as long as my thumb.

Green showed underneath.

Davin pointed.

"Wall."

"I had begun to suspect."

We worked.

The sound settled into rhythm.

Shhk.

Tap the blade against the bucket.

Shhk.

Brush flakes aside with a rag.

Jori moved between the two flats, testing edges with his thumb before scraping. Davin worked faster but stopped every few minutes to run his palm over the surface and check whether the lifted area actually ended where it looked like it ended.

I was slower than both.

That was fine.

The lower section in front of me had enough bad paint to make progress visible without requiring skill I did not have yet.

Blue came away in islands.

Cream appeared.

Then green.

At one point a little gold star emerged under the cream.

I stopped.

"Star."

Nobody answered.

I scraped around it carefully.

The whole shape appeared.

Five points.

Badly painted.

Davin looked over.

"Oh."

"What was that?"

"Star."

"Strong historical record."

Jori came around.

He looked at it.

"Old nursery piece."

"You remember?"

"No."

I looked at him.

"Then how do you know?"

He pointed to two more tiny stars hidden farther up the flat.

"Pattern."

Davin said, "Could've been a bedroom."

Jori shrugged.

"Could've."

That was all the theatre archaeology I received.

Nobody named the play.

Nobody told me who had stood in front of the stars.

The wall had been a wall more times than any of us needed to count.

Jori scraped straight through one of the stars.

I stared at him.

"You destroyed history."

"It was loose."

"Monster."

"Scrape."

I scraped.

A little later Venn came into the yard carrying a bucket of water and two rags.

I had seen him yesterday only in other people's sentences.

Venn fit the green coat.

Venn played Messenger.

Venn left.

Today he was a narrow man with dark curls pressed flat on one side of his head and a shirt that had already lost an argument with white paint.

He put the bucket down.

Jori said, "Edges."

Venn nodded.

No introduction.

Apparently we had both worked here enough to skip one.

He started wiping dust from the sections Davin had finished.

I watched for a few seconds.

"Messenger."

Venn looked at me.

"Yesterday."

"Good exit."

He stared.

Behind me, Davin made a noise that might have been a cough.

Venn said, "I walked off."

"Exactly."

He looked at Jori.

"Is he like this all day?"

Jori said, "Sometimes worse."

Excellent.

Venn went back to wiping the wall.

That settled our relationship.

By late morning, my right palm had started to warm where the scraper handle pressed into it.

Not pain.

Different work.

I changed grip and used the rounder corner on a small patch near the bottom.

The problem with simple work was that it became easier exactly when it became dangerous.

At first I had thought about every stroke.

Angle.

Pressure.

Loose edge.

Chalk.

Then my body learned enough of the motion that my mind wandered.

Not far.

Just far enough.

I found a long lifted edge and followed it.

Blue curled away.

Cream beneath.

The strip came beautifully.

Six inches.

Eight.

Ten.

Then Jori said, "Stop."

I stopped.

The blade was two inches inside his chalk line.

I looked down.

The peeled strip crossed cleanly into the section he had told me to preserve.

"Ah."

Davin leaned over from the other flat.

"Future."

I looked at him.

"What?"

"You're scraping tomorrow now."

Jori crouched beside me.

He rubbed the exposed cream with one finger.

It was solid.

Not loose.

I had simply gotten under it because I had been enjoying myself.

"That stays," he said.

"Apparently not all of it."

"No."

I waited for the lecture.

None came.

Jori stood.

"Davin."

Davin looked over.

"Blue."

"Later."

"Mostly him."

"Yes."

I frowned.

"You're not supposed to agree that quickly."

Davin grinned.

Jori handed me a piece of chalk.

"New line."

I looked at the flat.

"Where?"

"Here."

He showed me the edge of the area again.

I redrew the line around the damage I had made.

"Now?"

"Don't cross it."

"I understood that the first time."

"Evidence mixed."

Venn laughed into his rag.

I went back to work.

The mistake cost us exactly what it had cost.

A little more paint later.

No shame ceremony.

No story about learning boundaries.

I just had a chalk line I had personally made and stronger reasons to notice it.

Around midday, the gray sky finally produced rain.

Not much.

Enough to send all four of us into motion.

Jori and Davin lifted the blue flat.

Venn grabbed the rear trestle.

I took my crutches and got out of the lane.

That was my contribution.

The flat went under the rear overhang.

The second followed.

The rain lasted three minutes.

Davin stood under the overhang staring at it.

"Done."

Jori said, "Weather?"

"Yes."

"Good repair."

"Mostly me."

I ate bread against the wall.

Venn had an onion pie that smelled better.

I looked at it.

He looked at me.

"No."

"I said nothing."

"You looked."

Apparently Lyssa had spread.

Rinna came through carrying a stack of folded cloths.

"How much?"

Jori pointed at the flats.

"Enough."

Rinna inspected the blue one.

Her eyes found my pale scrape because of course they did.

"That?"

"Greg," Jori said.

I raised my bread.

"Present."

Rinna looked at the patch.

"Problem?"

"Paint."

"Tomorrow?"

"Fine."

She nodded.

Then she handed the cloths to Venn.

"Front room."

Venn took them.

No argument.

Rinna disappeared again.

I looked at Jori.

"That's it?"

"What?"

"I damaged company property."

Davin said, "You improved by subtraction."

"Do not help."

Jori picked up his scraper.

"Paint isn't company property."

I looked at the wall.

"The wall is."

"Then don't scrape through the wood."

Clear standard.

After bread, we turned the cream flat around.

The back was black.

Not painted black for a scene.

Black in the practical theatre sense, dull and uneven, with old nail holes and two patches where canvas had been repaired.

Nessa appeared in the doorway.

She had a shallow wooden box against one hip.

"Keep that side."

Jori said, "We are."

She pointed at the bottom corner.

"Not that."

I looked.

A little square of canvas had begun separating from the frame.

Davin put his scraper down.

"Glue?"

"Stitch first," Nessa said.

Davin pressed the canvas gently.

"Still good."

"Yes."

She handed him the box.

Inside were heavy thread, needles, two small wood pieces, and things I did not recognize.

Then she left.

Davin changed jobs immediately.

No protest about being the paint man.

No speech about authority.

He sat where I had been, pulled the loose canvas edge into place, and began working the repair with the kind of concentration that made conversation feel rude.

I moved to the other flat.

Jori said, "Top half."

I looked up.

Standing work.

"How long?"

"Until you're tired."

Specific enough.

I planted both crutches, leaned them against the trestle one at a time, and worked a section I could reach without twisting.

The scraper needed both hands only sometimes.

Most of the time one hand did the work while the other steadied me against the trestle or took the crutch again when I changed position.

It was slower.

Jori did not offer to make it fast.

He moved to the opposite side and kept scraping his section.

After ten minutes my right leg began to feel the standing.

After fifteen, my shoulder wanted a different angle.

I sat.

Nobody congratulated me for recognizing fatigue.

Davin had repaired the canvas corner before I noticed he was done.

He handed the box back to Nessa when she returned.

She checked the patch.

Pulled once.

"Good."

Davin looked at me.

I pointed at him.

"Don't."

He smiled.

Nessa took the box inside.

The afternoon became paint.

Old paint.

Bad paint.

Dust in my hair.

Dust on my sleeves.

Dust inside the fold of my collar.

The cream flat surrendered a patch of dark red wide enough to look like a stripe from an older room. Venn returned and wiped the cleaned sections. Jori used a small brush to clear the frame edges. Davin mixed a thin wash in a bucket for the scraped patch I had created.

I watched him.

"Blue?"

"Blue."

"Same blue?"

He looked into the bucket.

"Blue enough."

Healthy artistic standard.

He painted over my mistake.

The wet patch was visibly newer than the rest.

Jori looked at it.

"Dry darker."

Davin said, "Mostly."

"How much mostly?"

"Tomorrow."

There was no answer to that.

By late afternoon, the two flats were not beautiful.

They were usable.

Loose paint gone.

Edges cleaned.

Canvas corner repaired.

My pale mistake blue again.

The old gold stars mostly erased except one high enough that nobody had needed to touch it.

I looked at it.

Jori followed my eyes.

"Loose?"

"No."

"Then it stays."

History survived through structural integrity.

I liked that more than I should have.

Rinna came out with a coin.

One copper.

Normal theatre work.

I took it.

"No rehearsal?"

"No."

"Tomorrow?"

"Not for you yet."

Clear.

"No wall role?"

Rinna looked at the flats.

"You've had enough."

Davin said, "He could stand very still."

"Better than Marek," Venn said.

I looked at him.

"You've been here six hours and already betrayed him."

"I've been here longer than six hours."

Right.

Other people existed before I noticed them.

Annoying feature of the world.

We carried the tools in.

I carried only the bucket of rags because it could hang from two fingers while I used the crutches carefully through the rear passage. Venn took the scraper box. Jori and Davin took the flats once the paint was dry enough to move.

Nobody gave me the awkward wall to prove anything.

Good workplace.

The repaired back latch shut cleanly behind us when Jori tested the door after the last flat came through.

It shut because it had been repaired.

Then he opened it again because Davin was still outside.

Ordinary technology remained complicated.

No one mentioned brown coats.

No one mentioned deliveries.

No one mentioned who opened the door for them.

The latest fact stayed where it was.

Rinna was arguing with a paper at the front table when I left.

Not a person.

The paper appeared to be winning.

I did not volunteer.

Outside, the rain had passed and left the streets darker without making them cleaner.

Blue paint remained under two fingernails despite washing.

My right palm was warm from the scraper. Shoulders used. Right leg mildly tired from the standing section. Residual limb comfortable. No pain. No weakness. No new problem.

I bought nothing on the walk home.

This was less financially noble than it sounded. I had bread waiting.

Lyssa was there before me.

That happened often enough now that neither of us treated it as information.

She was at the table with a dark piece of cloth that was not the brown cloth.

I stopped.

She looked at me.

"Don't."

"I have learned."

"Have you?"

"No."

She went back to the dark cloth.

I put my crutches against the wall.

The brown cloth remained in its old place.

Three pins.

Same fold.

The damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three threads.

Same failure.

I washed my hands again.

Blue came off one finger and stayed under another nail.

Lyssa looked over.

"Paint."

"Wall."

"Blue."

"Mostly."

She looked at me.

I pointed.

"Davin."

That explained exactly as much to her as the boy's answer had explained to me.

"Work?" she asked.

"Yes."

"Show?"

"No."

"Role?"

"Scraper."

She nodded.

Apparently valid.

I cut bread.

Lyssa worked for another minute, then said, "You missed."

I looked at the loaf.

"No."

She pointed at my sleeve.

A small blue streak crossed the outside of my forearm.

"That is theatre."

"On your shirt."

"Still theatre."

"Your shirt."

I looked at it.

Not the damaged one.

The better one.

Of course.

I rubbed the streak with my thumb.

It spread.

Lyssa watched.

"Improved."

I stopped touching it.

"Everyone has been saying that today."

She almost smiled.

I ate bread.

After a while she moved the dark cloth farther from my painted sleeve without comment.

That was fair.

"What was the wall before?" she asked.

I thought about the green layer.

The cream.

The dark red.

The little gold star.

"Several things."

"What now?"

"Kitchen."

She accepted that.

No questions about magic.

No questions about Vale.

No questions about the theatre's strangers.

I did not offer any.

The new dark cloth remained whatever it was.

The old brown cloth remained whatever it was.

My shirt had acquired blue paint without solving the three loose threads on the other one.

This felt like progress in the most honest available sense.

Before bed, I washed my hands a third time.

The last blue mark under my nail stayed.

I left it there.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 245

## THE SPENDER

The right crutch grip had turned.

Not much.

Enough.

I noticed it because the seam that normally sat along the underside had moved against the base of my thumb sometime between yesterday's wall and this morning's bread.

I turned the crutch in my hand.

The grip did not move.

Only the wrapping had.

This was better than the crutch breaking and worse than the crutch being free.

I pressed the edge with my thumb.

No tear.

No exposed wood.

No sharp tack.

The wrap had simply shifted around the handle and tightened unevenly.

Ordinary wear.

I hated ordinary wear.

It had no villain.

Lyssa was already gone.

The dark cloth from last night was not on the table. The brown cloth was.

Three pins.

Same fold.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

My better shirt had a blue paint streak on the sleeve because I had spent yesterday improving theatre property by damaging my own.

I wore it anyway.

A blue streak was not structural.

I had learned that word.

I ate bread, drank water, and checked the grip again as though a second inspection might produce a different economy.

It did not.

The right handle felt slightly thicker on one side and slightly thinner on the other.

No pain.

No weakness.

Right palm normal after sleep.

Shoulders normal.

Right leg normal.

Residual limb comfortable.

Left tip boring.

The crutch worked.

I could ignore it.

That was how maintenance became repair.

I took both crutches and left.

The repairer's shop was open.

He was working on somebody else's walking stick when I arrived, which offended me in a small private way.

I had become accustomed to shops existing specifically at the moment I needed them.

The man behind the counter sat with the stick across his knees. A brass-colored ferrule lay beside him. He was shaving a thin curl from the end of the wood with a small knife.

He looked up.

Then at my crutches.

"Tip?"

"Grip."

He put the knife down.

"Which?"

"Right."

He pointed at the bench.

"Sit."

This relationship had not grown warmer.

It had grown shorter.

I sat.

The other customer was an older man in a dark cap who stood near the door with his hands folded over his stomach. He looked at the walking stick, not at me.

The repairer finished the shaving, fitted the ferrule, twisted it once, removed it, shaved another sliver, then fitted it again.

The older man said, "Too tight?"

"Was."

"Now?"

The repairer knocked the ferrule once against the heel of his hand.

"Now not."

Strong sales language.

He fixed it in place, handed the stick over, and made the man test it on the floor.

The man leaned on it.

Turned.

Tapped it twice.

"Good."

"Pay."

I liked this shop.

The older man paid and left.

The repairer looked at me.

"Right."

I handed him the right crutch.

He turned it over once.

Then twice.

His thumb found the shifted seam immediately.

"You twist."

"I walk."

"You twist too."

"Accusation."

"Grip."

He held the crutch horizontally and showed me where the wrap had crept.

The material around the handle was dark and smooth from my palm. One edge had shifted far enough that the overlap no longer sat evenly beneath the wood.

"Replace?" I asked.

"No."

That improved my mood.

"Why not?"

"Still good."

That improved it further.

He picked at the end of the wrap with a blunt tool.

The material lifted.

"Loose here. Tight here."

He pointed to opposite sides.

"Take it off?"

"Part."

"How much is part?"

He looked at me.

"Enough."

Apparently Jori had relatives.

He loosened the outer turns without unwrapping the entire handle, pulled the material flat, then worked it back around with even tension.

I watched for maybe thirty seconds before deciding I understood the process.

This was false.

"Could I do that next time?"

"Yes."

I waited.

He kept working.

"That sounded too easy."

"It is easy."

"Then why am I paying you?"

"Because you brought it here."

Fair.

He pressed the seam down with his thumb and reached for a small tack.

I looked at the tack.

"That was there before?"

"No."

"Why now?"

"Because it moved."

"Is the tack going into my hand?"

"No."

"Useful standard."

He set it where the underside overlap met the wood, well away from where my palm closed, then drove it flush with two light taps.

He ran his thumb over it.

Then mine.

"Feel?"

I rubbed the underside.

The tack was there if I searched for it.

Not if I gripped normally.

"No."

"Good."

He handed the crutch back.

I closed my right hand around it.

The handle felt narrower than it had this morning.

Not actually narrower.

Even.

My palm noticed the change because it had spent too many hours learning the wrong one.

"Stand."

I stood.

Both tips on the floor.

Weight through the grips.

No slip.

No edge under the thumb.

I took two steps.

Turned.

Took two back.

The repairer watched my right hand rather than my leg.

I appreciated the accuracy.

"Turn again."

"Which way?"

"Both."

I turned left.

Then right.

The wrap stayed where it belonged.

"Fine," he said.

"I enjoy how moved you are."

"Other one."

I looked at the left grip.

"What about it?"

"Give."

I handed him the left crutch.

He checked the wrap.

Pressed the seam.

Twisted the handle in both directions.

Then handed it back.

"Fine."

"Should we do that one too?"

"No."

"For balance?"

"No."

"Matching?"

"No."

He looked almost offended.

"If good, leave."

That was a cruel policy for a man who took money for repairs.

I looked at the right grip.

"How long will it last?"

He shrugged.

"Until it doesn't."

Hessa and this man should never meet.

Or perhaps they had and the universe had wisely kept it from me.

I paid him.

The amount was ordinary.

The loss felt personal.

Not because it was large.

Because it had been money before I woke and was now grip.

Necessary grip.

Still grip.

I put the remaining coins away.

I had bought no new ability. The crutch did not become faster. I did not become taller. I had paid to make one irritating seam return to where it used to be.

Maintenance had terrible marketing.

The repairer had already picked up another piece of work from behind the counter.

No farewell.

I left.

Outside, the right grip felt too new for perhaps half a street.

Then my hand stopped noticing.

That was what I had paid for.

Carrow was brighter than yesterday.

The rain had cleaned nothing important.

Mud remained in the joints between stones. Wagon wheels had redistributed it. The air smelled of damp wood, cooking fat, and something sharp from a dyer's lane I did not take.

I had no theatre call.

Rinna had said not for me yet.

No Hessa note.

No reason to invent one.

Vale existed.

The debt also existed.

I had just spent money maintaining the thing that allowed me to move around the city and earn more money.

This was probably defensible.

Antonius would still prefer the money.

I stood at the next corner longer than necessary.

Home was one direction.

Vale's office was another.

I had not been promised work.

That mattered.

I went to Vale anyway.

Not because the repair had ruined me.

It had not.

Because debt did not become less real on days when nobody summoned me.

The office door was open.

The ink-fingered clerk was at the front desk with three stacks of paper arranged around him.

He looked up when I entered.

Then looked past me.

"No bundle."

"I did not bring one."

"No, ours."

"Ah."

He went back to the papers.

I waited.

He looked up again.

"What?"

"Work?"

"No."

That had been fast.

"None?"

"None for you."

Important distinction.

I looked at the three stacks.

"Those appear extremely work-shaped."

"They are."

"And yet."

"And yet."

I was beginning to suspect every profession in Carrow shared one teacher.

"What are those?"

"Accounts."

"I have handled accounts."

"These need decisions."

"I can make decisions."

"That is the problem."

I leaned on the repaired right grip.

It did not move.

"What is suitable?"

"Nothing today."

No apology.

No promise about tomorrow.

No attempt to create work because I had arrived with debt.

Correct.

Still annoying.

"What about outgoing packets?"

"Done."

"Returned acknowledgments?"

"Done."

"Sealing?"

"Tomorrow, maybe. Not today."

There it was.

Maybe.

The most expensive word in Carrow.

I looked toward the rear.

Antonius was not visible.

"Is he here?"

"Yes."

"Does he have work?"

The clerk looked at me.

"For you?"

"That is usually the relevant category."

"No."

I nodded.

Then stayed where I was.

The clerk finished a line.

Dipped his pen.

Wrote another.

Eventually he said, "Standing there doesn't create it."

"I was testing."

"Result?"

"Negative."

"Good."

I hated that word.

I turned to leave.

"Greg."

I looked back.

The clerk pointed at my right crutch.

"New?"

"Adjusted."

"Tip?"

"Grip."

He nodded.

That was all.

Apparently the office kept its interest in my equipment at the level of whether I could continue being useful.

Consistent.

Outside again, I had accomplished something important.

I had confirmed I could not earn debt credit today.

This felt like paying for information.

I had not paid for the information.

Small victory.

My stomach interrupted before I could develop a philosophy around unemployment.

I walked toward the cookshop district.

Not the theatre cookshop.

Another one.

I had eaten there once before, far enough back that I could not remember what I had ordered and the woman behind the counter did not remember me at all.

Healthy relationship.

The board listed stew, beans, and a meat pie that cost more than I wanted it to.

I looked at the pie.

Then at the stew.

Then at my money.

The grip repair had already happened.

The debt still existed.

Hunger also existed.

Three systems.

Only one could be solved with stew.

I bought stew.

The woman put it in a heavy bowl and pointed to an open place near the wall.

I sat.

A laborer across from me had sawdust on both sleeves and one red mark across his forehead where something had rested there.

He ate without speaking.

I respected him.

For almost five minutes, nothing happened.

Nobody asked me to perform.

Nobody asked me to test magic.

Nobody asked me to compare account lines.

Nobody asked whether the theatre used its front or back door.

I ate stew.

It was too salty.

I finished it anyway.

The laborer left before me.

A woman took his place, put a basket under the table, and began eating beans.

Carrow replaced people quickly when seats were useful.

I paid attention to the right grip when I stood.

Still flat.

Still even.

The tack did not touch my hand.

The repair was becoming invisible.

Good repair.

I left the cookshop with less money and more body.

That was the entire exchange.

On the way home I passed the lane toward the theatre.

I did not take it.

No call.

No maybe transformed by proximity.

Not for me yet had been clear.

I kept walking.

A handcart blocked half the street near a cooper's yard while two men rolled a barrel toward it.

I waited because there was nowhere sensible to go.

One of the men said, "Other side."

The other said, "I know."

"You are on this side."

"I know where I am."

They moved the barrel.

I passed.

Carrow remained capable of operating without me.

At home, the room was still empty.

The brown cloth remained in the same place.

Three pins.

Same fold.

The dark cloth from last night was still absent.

That established only absence.

My damaged shirt remained on the shelf.

Three loose threads.

My better shirt still had blue paint on the sleeve.

I took it off.

Looked at the paint.

Considered rubbing it.

Did not.

Growth through laziness.

I changed into the damaged shirt carefully enough not to pull the cuff.

Three threads remained three.

I set the better shirt on the chair.

Then I counted my money.

Not the debt.

My money.

Repair.

Stew.

What remained.

The one copper from yesterday had not become a debt payment.

It had joined the rest and then helped pay for ordinary life.

This was not failure.

It was also not progress against Antonius.

Both could be true without producing a speech.

I put the coins away.

The right crutch leaned against the wall.

I looked at the grip.

For a moment I could see the new tack because I knew exactly where it was.

Then I stopped looking.

Lyssa came back near dusk.

No dark cloth.

No bundle.

She carried two pieces of fried dough wrapped in a clean cloth.

This was immediately more relevant.

She put them on the table.

Looked at me.

Then at the shirt I was wearing.

"That one?"

"Temporary."

"Why?"

I pointed at the chair.

Blue streak.

She looked at it.

Then at me.

"You didn't rub it."

"I have matured."

"No."

"Correct."

She sat.

I reached for the cloth.

Lyssa slapped my hand once.

Not hard.

"Mine."

I withdrew.

"Hostile household."

She unwrapped one piece of fried dough.

Then the other.

Then pushed the cloth toward the middle.

Shared, apparently.

I took one.

She noticed the right crutch when I reached for it to shift the chair.

"Grip."

"Yes."

"Bad?"

"Moved."

"Fixed?"

"Adjusted."

"Paid?"

"Yes."

She ate.

"Vale?"

I looked at her.

"How did you get there?"

"You paid."

Fair.

"No work."

She nodded.

No sympathy ceremony.

No question about the balance.

"Tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

She made a face.

"Exactly."

We ate the fried dough.

It had sugar on one side and too much oil on the other.

Excellent.

After a while I said, "The repairer says I twist the right grip."

Lyssa looked at my hands.

"When you turn."

I stopped chewing.

"You knew?"

"I see you walk."

"Why did nobody tell me?"

"Problem?"

"No."

"Then why?"

I looked at the repaired grip.

This household had standards.

I did not like them.

"You could have mentioned it."

"You could have asked."

"I did not know to ask."

"Then not useful."

I considered arguing.

She was already eating again.

I let it go.

Not because she was right.

Because I wanted the last piece of fried dough and conflict had become strategically unsound.

She reached for it first.

Of course.

I looked at her.

She broke it in half.

Gave me the smaller half.

"Generous."

"Eat."

I ate.

The room darkened.

The brown cloth remained folded.

The damaged shirt remained damaged on my body.

The better shirt remained blue on the chair.

The dark cloth remained elsewhere.

My debt remained smaller than it had been and larger than I wanted.

No work had appeared because I needed it.

The right grip held because somebody who knew grips had fixed what was actually wrong and left the other one alone.

By bedtime I had stopped noticing it completely.

That was the most useful thing I bought all day.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 246

## THE MINDER

We were out of onions.

Not nearly out.

Not one onion left that could be cut carefully enough to become two onions.

Out.

I discovered this by opening the basket, which was an advanced household technique nobody had previously explained to me.

Lyssa was already gone.

The brown cloth remained near the wall with its three pins and the patience of stone. The dark cloth from two nights ago remained absent. My damaged shirt still had three loose threads, so I wore the better shirt with the blue paint streak and accepted that respectable clothing was now a historical category.

The right crutch grip felt normal.

I noticed that only because yesterday I had spent money making it normal.

Then I stopped noticing.

Worth every copper.

No theatre boy came up the stairs. No Hessa note waited under the door. Vale had said tomorrow, maybe, which meant nothing had happened yet and might continue doing so all day.

The onion basket, however, was definitely empty.

I ate bread, drank water, and went to correct the one problem in Carrow that had supplied evidence.

The market was already loud by the time I reached it.

A fish seller near the lower lane had arranged six silver fish on a board and was arguing with a woman who claimed one of them had looked larger yesterday. A boy carried a stack of shallow baskets taller than his head. Two dogs had found something under a cart that neither dog appeared willing to surrender.

Nobody needed me.

Good city.

The onion seller saw me before I reached her stall.

She pointed at a pile with the knife in her hand.

"Three."

"I haven't spoken."

"Three."

"I was going to say two."

"You always say two."

That was not true enough to be comfortable.

"I have said two twice."

"And bought three."

"Once."

She looked at me.

I looked at the onions.

Memory was becoming expensive.

"Two," I said.

She put three on the cloth.

"This is coercion."

"This is onions."

The third one was smaller.

That weakened my case.

I reached for my coins.

A handcart struck the edge of the next stall hard enough to make a stack of empty baskets lean.

The onion seller turned before the baskets fell.

A man behind the cart swore.

One of the sacks on the cart had split along the side.

Onions had not poured into the street. That would have been dramatic. Instead six or seven had worked through the gap and lodged between the sack and the cart rail, while one had escaped completely and rolled under a cabbage table.

The seller put her knife down.

"That mine?"

The cart man looked at the sack.

"Probably."

"Probably?"

"Mark's on the other side."

"Turn it."

"It's tied."

"Then untie it."

The cabbage seller leaned over his own table.

"Your onion's under me."

"Keep it."

"I don't want it."

"Then stop announcing ownership."

He kicked the escaped onion gently back into the lane.

It rolled three feet and stopped against my left crutch tip.

I looked down.

The city had made a decision.

The onion seller came around the front of her stall.

"Sit."

I looked at the stool beside the baskets.

"Why?"

"Watch."

"What?"

She pointed at the stall.

I looked at the stall.

Then at her.

"All of it?"

"Onions."

"Sell?"

"No."

That was clear.

"How long?"

She was already moving toward the cart.

"Until I'm back."

This was less clear.

I sat.

My three onions remained on the cloth beside the coin I had not yet given her.

The seller reached the cart, shoved one sack enough to expose the painted mark on its side, and began arguing with the cart man about whether a split sack was the same as a short sack.

Apparently I had been appointed to government.

I set my crutches along the inside of the stall where nobody would catch a foot on them. The right grip stayed flat when I turned it to fit under the table.

I did not think about that again.

For perhaps thirty seconds, I minded onions successfully.

Then an old woman with a narrow basket came up.

"Three small."

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

I looked toward the onion seller.

The seller was pointing at the torn seam while the cart man pointed at a wheel, which did not appear relevant.

"She's over there," I said.

"I can see."

"Good."

The woman reached toward the small onions.

I put one hand over the pile.

She stopped.

"What are you doing?"

"She said not to sell."

"I'm not buying from you."

That seemed technically worse.

"Then you should probably wait."

The woman stared at me.

"I take three every fourth day."

"Congratulations."

"Move your hand."

"I don't know what you pay."

"She does."

"She is over there."

"I can see."

We had reached a stable system.

The old woman put her basket on the stall.

"I'll wait."

"Excellent."

"It isn't."

A second customer arrived before either of us improved.

He was a boy perhaps twelve or thirteen carrying a square basket lined with cloth.

"Six for the cookshop."

I looked at him.

"Which cookshop?"

He named one near the upper market that I knew only because its steam made one corner smell like onions most mornings.

Appropriate.

"She said they'd be ready."

"Are they?"

He looked at the onions.

"Those are onions."

"Strong evidence."

The old woman said, "He won't let you have them."

The boy looked at me.

"Why?"

"Instruction."

"What instruction?"

"Do not sell."

"I'm not buying. Cookshop pays later."

I closed my eyes briefly.

Of course.

"That is somehow less helpful."

He put his basket beside the old woman's.

"I can wait."

The old woman said, "We are forming a society."

"Membership appears involuntary."

She sniffed.

A man walking past slowed long enough to look at me sitting behind the stall.

"New?"

"No."

He kept walking.

Correct interaction.

Across the lane, the onion seller pulled the split sack open enough to inspect the contents. She pressed one onion near the torn seam, cut a soft place with her thumbnail, and held it up toward the cart man.

He shook his head.

She held up another.

He shook his head again, though with less confidence.

The cabbage seller had abandoned his own work enough to watch.

I understood none of the pricing problem.

That did not stop me from having opinions.

I kept them.

A young woman approached the stall with a copper already between two fingers.

"Two large."

The old woman said, "No."

The young woman looked at her.

Then at me.

"Why no?"

I pointed at the seller.

"Temporary government."

The old woman said, "Bad government."

The young woman looked across the lane.

"She knows what I pay."

"So does everyone except me."

"Then take the coin."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I don't know if it is the right coin."

"It is."

"According to you."

She stared at me.

The boy from the cookshop said, "He's very serious."

"I am guarding onions."

"From customers."

"Apparently."

The young woman sighed and joined the waiting society.

This was becoming visible.

The onion seller finally looked toward us.

She stopped.

Three waiting customers.

Me behind the stall.

My hand still resting protectively on produce.

Her face changed.

Not alarm.

Something worse.

Understanding.

She came back across the lane.

"Why are they here?"

I looked at the old woman.

The old woman looked delighted.

"You said don't sell."

The seller looked at me.

"Yes."

"I have sold nothing."

"I can see."

"Then I have performed perfectly."

The old woman said, "He defended them."

The seller turned to her.

"From who?"

"Me."

The cookshop boy laughed.

I did not.

The seller pointed at the old woman.

"Three small. Same."

The old woman immediately took three onions, dropped a coin onto the cloth, and left.

No counting.

No discussion.

The seller pointed at the cookshop boy.

"Six are under the green cloth."

He reached beneath the rear corner of the stall and pulled out a small tied bag I had not known existed.

"Tomorrow morning too?" he asked.

"Maybe eight."

He nodded and left without paying anything.

The seller looked at the young woman.

"Two large."

The copper in the woman's hand disappeared into the seller's palm.

Two onions went into her bag.

She left.

The entire backlog vanished in less than a minute.

I looked at the seller.

"You have hidden systems."

"I have customers."

"I was told to watch onions."

"Yes."

"I watched them."

"You stopped people buying them."

"You said don't sell."

"I meant you don't sell."

I pointed at the lane where the old woman had disappeared.

"She sold to herself."

"She knows what she takes."

"The boy paid nothing."

"Cookshop pays later."

"The other woman told me the coin was right."

"It was."

I sat back.

"This operation relies dangerously on information."

She took my three onions from the cloth and put them into a small bag.

"Most do."

That was rude enough to sound like Hessa.

I ignored it.

"What was I supposed to do?"

"Don't choose. Don't make change. Don't promise. Don't let strangers walk off with things."

I stared at her.

"Those are much better instructions."

"I thought you had sense."

"Evidence mixed."

She looked at me.

I regretted letting Jori spread.

The cart man called from across the lane.

The seller turned.

He had opened the split sack completely now. The top layer looked fine. The onions around the tear did not. Two had crushed sides. One had a wet dark patch.

She looked back at me.

"Stay."

I lifted both hands.

"New instructions first."

Her eyes narrowed.

Then she pointed at the regular piles.

"If they know what they take and what they owe, let them. Coin there."

She tapped a shallow wooden dish.

"Cookshop bag is gone. No credit for anyone else. No prices from you. No promises. Nobody touches the torn-sack pile when I bring it over."

I nodded.

"Strangers?"

"Wait for me."

"People who claim not to be strangers?"

"Use your head."

"That clause destroys the rest."

She was already walking away.

The second administration began.

This time nobody came.

Naturally.

I sat behind onions under improved law and watched the market continue refusing to care.

The cabbage seller sold half a cabbage to a woman who had brought her own knife.

The fish seller finally ended the argument about yesterday's larger fish by telling the customer to buy yesterday's fish.

A child passed with a broken wooden wheel under one arm.

The cart man and onion seller moved the split sack onto the ground between them.

She sorted quickly.

Firm onions to one side.

Bruised ones to another.

Soft ones into a shallow basket.

The cart man objected to the size of the bad pile.

She put one thumb through a damaged side and held it up.

He stopped objecting to that onion.

A few minutes later she came back carrying the shallow basket.

"Move."

I moved my crutches and shifted the stool.

She set the damaged onions behind the stall, separate from everything else.

"These don't sell with those."

"Because damaged."

"Because cheaper."

I looked at the basket.

"Some are still good."

"Yes."

"Then why cheaper?"

She looked at me.

"Do you want to buy them full price?"

"No."

"Learning."

Cruel.

She went back for another load.

A woman with a green scarf came to the stall.

She picked up four medium onions, checked two, replaced one, chose another, and placed a coin in the dish.

I watched.

She looked at me.

"She here?"

I pointed.

The woman followed my finger.

"Fine."

She started to leave.

Every instinct from the first administration told me to stop her.

The new instructions did not.

"Four?" I asked.

She looked back.

"Four."

"Coin there?"

"Yes."

"Then apparently we are citizens."

She left.

I checked the dish.

There was a coin.

I did not know whether it was enough.

That was not my job.

Progress through ignorance.

The seller returned with a second small basket.

She looked at the coin dish.

Then at the missing onions.

"Green scarf?"

"Yes."

"Four?"

"Yes."

She nodded.

I felt an unreasonable amount of satisfaction.

Then she said, "She usually takes five."

I looked toward the street.

The woman was gone.

"She said four."

"Then four."

No investigation.

No accusation.

No need to recover the lost fifth onion that had never left.

Healthy commerce.

The seller crouched beside the damaged pile and began sorting again.

I stayed on the stool because leaving would have required announcing that I had finished a job nobody had formally given me.

After a minute she handed me an onion.

"Soft?"

I pressed it.

Firm on one side.

Softer near the neck.

"Part."

"Cut."

"I don't have a knife."

She held hers out handle first.

I took it.

This had become employment without wages.

I cut through the soft section.

The center beneath it was clean.

She took the onion back.

"That pile."

She pointed to a basket.

"What is that pile?"

"Today."

"Compared to?"

"Tomorrow."

I looked at the other basket.

"Those survive?"

"Probably."

"Strong system."

"Cut."

So I cut.

Not many.

Six or seven onions from the split edge.

Soft section off.

Clean remainder into the today basket.

Anything wet through or smelling wrong went into a shallow reject bowl.

The seller checked the first two pieces I chose.

On the third she moved one from today to reject.

"Why?"

She turned it.

The base had gone translucent.

"Ah."

"Smell."

I did.

Wrong.

Not rotten enough to announce itself from a distance.

Wrong enough.

The next time I checked the base.

Nobody congratulated me.

The cart man and seller finished whatever argument governed the sack. He retied the good ones, shifted another sack down, and left with fewer coins than he probably wanted.

The seller watched the cart go.

"Short?"

I asked.

"No."

"Split?"

"Yes."

"Who pays for bad?"

"Some me. Some him."

"How much?"

She looked at me.

"Not you."

Good boundary.

I cut another onion.

By the time the damaged basket was sorted, my hands smelled sharp enough to make my eyes notice. My right palm was fine. The repaired grip had stayed out of the way completely. Shoulders normal. Right leg slightly stiff from sitting, which resolved when I stood and moved around the stall once.

Residual limb comfortable.

No new problem.

The seller reclaimed her stool.

I took that as dismissal.

"Am I paid?"

"No."

"I performed public service."

"You blocked three customers."

"Then recovered."

"You cut six onions."

"Seven."

She looked at the reject bowl.

"Six."

One had apparently ceased to qualify as work.

I accepted the correction.

She held up my bag.

"Three."

I reached for it.

She did not let go.

"Pay."

I stared at her.

"I guarded your property."

"Badly."

"I sorted damage."

"Slowly."

"I preserved order."

"You made a line."

"Lines are order."

"Pay."

I paid.

No discount.

Healthy business.

She gave me the bag.

Then added one small onion from the damaged-today basket.

I looked at it.

"Free?"

"Use tonight."

"That is free."

"That is dying."

"Still onion."

"Today."

I put it in the bag.

Four onions.

I had come for two.

The market had structure.

I left before she could improve my household further.

I bought a small flat cake from a nearby stall because cutting onions had made me hungry and because yesterday's stew had already established that food continued costing money after other things cost money.

No debt arithmetic.

No moral hearing.

Flat cake.

I ate it standing near a wall while two women argued about ribbon color at a stall across the lane. One preferred yellow. The other preferred not yellow. The seller appeared personally harmed by both positions.

Then I went home.

The bag hung from my wrist for the clearer stretches and moved to the crook of my elbow when I needed more hand around the right grip. Four onions were not heavy. They were simply shaped badly for a man using two crutches.

At the apartment, Lyssa was still out.

Brown cloth.

Three pins.

Same fold.

The better shirt still had blue on the sleeve.

The damaged shirt remained damaged.

I put the dying onion apart from the other three because apparently I had become responsible for onion chronology.

Then I washed my hands.

The smell remained.

I washed again.

Still onion.

There were worse reputations.

Lyssa returned after dark with no bundle I recognized and dust along one side of her skirt.

She saw the onions immediately.

"Three?"

I looked at the table.

Three good onions.

One dying onion.

"Complicated."

She put down a small packet of something green and leafy that I did not ask about because I had suffered enough commerce for one day.

"We needed two," she said.

"I know."

"Why four?"

"One is temporary."

Lyssa looked at me.

I pointed at the softer onion.

"Today."

She touched it.

"Fine."

"Apparently."

She took the knife.

"Where did you learn onions?"

"Employment."

She stopped.

"Vale?"

"No."

"Theatre?"

"No."

"Market?"

"Unfortunately."

She cut the softer onion.

The clean part went into the pot.

The soft neck went aside.

She looked at me.

"Good onion."

"I was told it was dying."

"Everything is."

I stared at her.

"Do not become Hessa."

She smiled.

Just enough.

I told her about the stall while she cooked.

Not every customer.

Only the first three because the shape improved with suffering.

When I reached the part where the seller returned to find them waiting, Lyssa stopped cutting greens.

"You stopped them?"

"I followed instructions."

"What instructions?"

"Don't sell."

Lyssa looked at me for a long moment.

"She meant you."

"Yes. This became clear later."

She laughed.

Not politely.

Enough that she had to put the knife down.

I waited with the dignity available to a former public official.

When she recovered, she said, "You made them wait."

"Three people."

"Why?"

"Because I did not know their arrangements."

"Then why were you in charge?"

"I was not."

That made her laugh again.

This household had become unsafe.

I sat.

The right grip disappeared under my hand when I leaned on the crutch.

I did not think about yesterday's repair until that moment.

Then it disappeared again.

Lyssa put onions and greens into the pot.

"Did she pay you?"

"No."

"Good."

I looked at her.

"Why good?"

"You would go back."

"I might go back anyway."

"For onions."

"Potentially administration."

"No."

She stirred the pot.

I considered my options.

"She gave me the fourth onion."

Lyssa glanced at it already cooking.

"Then she paid you."

"That was dying."

"Still onion."

I hated when two professionals agreed independently.

We ate.

The onion was fine.

Not excellent.

Not symbolic.

Fine.

The brown cloth stayed folded near the wall.

No note arrived.

No work appeared because I had checked yesterday.

No theatre boy climbed the stairs.

No Hessa question entered the room.

I had spent part of the day preventing a market from functioning and part of it cutting soft onions under supervision.

Carrow had survived both.

Before bed, Lyssa asked, "Two next time?"

I looked at the three good onions still in the basket.

"We have three."

"Good."

There it was.

I pointed at her.

She turned away before I could improve anything.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 247

## THE UNCLE

The theatre wanted me before I wanted breakfast.

This was poor scheduling.

The boy knocked while I was cutting bread and said, "Rinna."

He had learned enough not to wait for me to ask who.

"What did I do?"

"Nothing."

"Then why is she sending you?"

He looked at the piece of paper in his hand.

"Teren wants you before midday."

"Role?"

He read.

"Uncle."

I looked at him.

"Whose?"

He checked the paper again.

"It just says Uncle."

"Strong family."

He held the page out.

I took it.

The note said:

IF FREE.
BEFORE MIDDAY.
READ FIRST.
UNCLE.

No play title.

No line count.

No warning about clothes, paint, flowers, keys, magistrates, landlords, or other professions I had acquired without training.

The boy was already turning away.

"Wait."

He stopped.

"Performance?"

He shrugged.

Of course.

"Rehearsal?"

Another shrug.

"Are you paid by the answer?"

"No."

"Excellent system."

He left.

Lyssa had gone earlier. The brown cloth remained folded near the wall with its three pins. My damaged shirt still had three loose threads. The better one still had blue paint on the sleeve.

I wore the blue one.

The market onions from yesterday sat in the basket.

Three good.

No dying administrator among them.

The right crutch grip disappeared under my hand when I stood.

Fine.

I ate bread, drank water, and read the theatre note one more time in case UNCLE had become more specific.

It had not.

Outside, Carrow had already started without me.

A baker's assistant carried a flour sack against one shoulder. Two women stood beneath an upstairs window arguing with somebody they could not see. A dog slept in a doorway until the shopkeeper opened it, then moved exactly far enough to remain inconvenient.

I went west.

The theatre front doors were open.

No audience.

No handbills.

No one waiting with a threat report.

Inside, the stage held a table, four chairs, a narrow sideboard, and a covered serving dish large enough to make me suspicious immediately.

That was probably the problem.

Nessa stood at the property table with three wooden plates and a cloth napkin over one shoulder.

Venn was onstage carrying a pot.

Marek sat in one of the chairs eating something from his hand.

Teren saw me from the aisle.

"Good."

I hated that word everywhere now.

"Uncle?"

He held out pages.

"Read."

The play was called The Guest Pot.

That was not better.

I found UNCLE in the margin.

Eleven lines.

One entrance.

One exit.

Mostly seated.

Promising.

Hara played the woman of the house. Marek was her younger brother. Venn played the household cook.

The plot was simple enough to become dangerous.

Hara's character had promised her visiting uncle a large supper.

There was no supper.

The cook had been told the wrong day.

The brother had spent the food money on something described only as "necessary business," which meant the play did not respect him enough to specify.

The covered serving dish onstage was empty.

Everyone except my character knew this.

I looked at the dish.

Teren said, "Stop."

I looked at him.

"I haven't started."

"Exactly."

He pointed at the dish.

"You read ahead."

"That is generally encouraged."

"Not with your face."

Hara, sitting at the stage edge, said, "He does have one."

"Temporary," I said.

Teren handed me the pages.

"Again. From the entrance."

"We have not done the first one."

"Then this will be educational."

I went to the side door.

Nessa caught my sleeve.

"Chair."

She pointed to the near side of the table.

The chair had been angled slightly away from the table, leaving a clearer lane for my crutches and enough space to sit without moving the furniture during the scene.

Jori was near the wing with a short board and chalk.

I looked at him.

He looked at the chair.

"Fine."

That was the entire design review.

I went out.

Teren called, "Begin."

Hara and Marek were already arguing.

"He's early," Marek said.

"He is on time."

"That is early for family."

"Only yours."

Venn came in carrying the pot.

Hara looked at him.

"Well?"

Venn looked into the pot.

Then at her.

"No."

Marek said, "That is not a useful answer."

Venn put the pot on the sideboard.

"It is the most useful answer I have."

Small laugh from Hara.

Not audience.

Still deserved.

Then my knock.

Hara called, "Come in."

I entered.

I had eleven lines.

I also had a covered serving dish sitting three feet from my future chair and the knowledge that it contained nothing.

I looked at it.

Teren said, "Stop."

I closed my eyes.

"I need eyes less."

"You need the uncle to have your eyes."

"He can have different ones?"

"He hasn't read the play."

I looked at the dish again.

"Enviable."

Teren stepped onto the stage.

"You are coming for supper."

"Yes."

"You expect supper."

"Yes."

"Then why are you checking whether the supper is a lie before you sit?"

Because I knew.

That was the problem.

"I am protecting myself."

"The uncle isn't."

"Poor man."

"Again."

We reset.

This time I entered and looked at Hara first.

Then Marek.

Then the chair.

The serving dish remained at the edge of my vision where it belonged.

Hara crossed to greet me.

"Uncle. You made excellent time."

"My stomach kept the appointment."

That got a small laugh from Marek, who had heard the line before.

Hara said, "Sit. Supper is nearly ready."

I sat.

The chair angle worked.

My crutches settled to the side without touching the table.

I looked at Hara.

Not the dish.

Teren said nothing.

Progress through deprivation.

The scene continued.

Marek's brother tried to explain why no bread had arrived.

Venn's cook came in and out with increasingly useless objects.

A spoon.

An empty bowl.

A folded cloth.

At one point he returned with a cabbage leaf.

My line was, "Is that the first course?"

Venn looked at the leaf.

"No. It is evidence."

I laughed.

Teren stopped us.

"Greg."

"That is funny."

"Your character is hungry."

"He can still appreciate craft."

"Not before his line."

I looked at Venn.

He was not smiling.

Professional betrayal.

Again.

Venn entered with the cabbage leaf.

I looked at it.

Waited.

"Is that the first course?"

"No. It is evidence."

This time I let Hara react first.

She took the leaf from him.

"Evidence of what?"

"That there was once a cabbage."

Better.

Marek's character said he could explain.

Hara said, "You have been explaining since breakfast. It has not produced lunch."

That was the strongest line so far.

Mine were fine.

I began to understand the job.

Hungry.

Expectant.

Increasingly suspicious, but only after the room gave me reasons.

Not suspicious because I had pages.

Teren ran the covered-dish section.

Hara finally told Venn to bring supper.

Venn went to the sideboard.

He lifted the covered serving dish with both hands.

Brought it to the table.

Set it in front of me.

The lid stayed on.

My line was, "That smells subtle."

Marek laughed again.

Teren ignored him.

Hara said, "Family recipe."

Then the next written beat belonged to Venn.

He was supposed to stop Hara before she lifted the lid and admit there was nothing inside.

Instead, during the first run, his sleeve caught the edge of the napkin.

The napkin pulled.

One wooden plate slid off the table.

It hit the stage floor.

Did not break because it was wood.

Still loud.

Everyone looked down.

Teren said nothing.

Venn picked up the plate.

Put it back.

Then said his line.

"There is one problem."

Hara looked at the plate.

"Only one?"

That was not written.

Marek laughed.

I did too.

Teren let the scene continue.

Venn said, "The dish is empty."

Hara looked at him.

Then at Marek.

Marek's character immediately stood.

"I should check the street."

Hara caught his sleeve.

"No."

Audience or no audience, that was the shape of the play.

The brother tried to leave.

The house would not let him.

The uncle remained hungry.

The cook remained honest enough to make everyone else uncomfortable.

At the end, Hara's character lifted the empty dish lid anyway.

She looked inside.

Then at me.

"My apologies."

My last line was, "I have had lighter suppers."

Small.

Good.

Marek's character offered to fetch food.

Hara said, "With what money?"

He sat again.

End.

Teren looked at us.

"Again."

No speech.

We went again.

The second run went cleaner.

I did not look at the dish early.

Venn did not catch the napkin.

Marek remembered that he was trying to conceal the missing money rather than advertise guilt through posture.

Hara improved without announcing it.

On her first line after my entrance, she stopped moving before she said my stomach had kept the appointment.

The stillness gave the line more room.

Teren did not comment.

He did not need to.

After the run, he changed two crosses.

One was mine.

He moved my entrance line half a step wider around the table so my right crutch did not come close to Marek's chair when Marek stood too early.

Jori shifted the chair by less than the width of his hand.

Then he chalked the rear leg.

No accommodation speech.

The scene simply had geometry.

Rinna appeared during the third run.

She stood at the back with her ledger.

I saw her.

The uncle did not.

I kept looking at Hara.

Teren noticed.

"Good."

There it was again.

After the scene, Rinna said, "Paired."

I looked at her.

"Today?"

"Yes."

"Performance?"

"After the short piece."

"Pay?"

She looked at me.

"Yes."

"Healthy theatre."

Marek said, "He asks now."

"I have learned the dangerous connection between labor and food."

Rinna left.

The performance changed the room.

Not dramatically.

Chairs became audience chairs.

The stage floor got swept.

Nessa checked plates.

Venn checked the pot.

Hara disappeared into costume and came back wearing a brown house dress with pale cuffs.

Marek acquired a vest that made him look more trustworthy than the play allowed.

My Uncle got a dark coat with a short hem and no important pockets.

Nessa said, "Sit."

I sat.

She adjusted one sleeve.

"Fine."

"Strong character work."

"Coat."

"Right."

The first short piece went on without me.

I stayed backstage with Venn.

He had flour on one sleeve that did not belong to any prop I had seen.

I pointed at it.

"Real?"

He looked down.

"Breakfast."

"Excellent continuity."

He wiped at it.

Made it worse.

I respected him more.

From the stage, Hara delivered a line in the short piece and the audience laughed.

Venn listened.

Not with panic.

Just enough to know where the play was.

I asked, quietly, "How long have you been here?"

He looked at me.

"Theatre?"

"Yes."

"Before you."

"That is not a length."

"It is enough."

I considered arguing.

The short piece onstage reached a laugh.

Venn looked back toward the sound.

Conversation concluded.

The short piece ended.

Applause.

Movement.

Reset.

Then The Guest Pot.

I waited by the side door.

The audience settled.

Hara and Marek began.

The first laugh came on "That is early for family."

Bigger than rehearsal.

Venn's first "No" got another.

Good room.

My knock.

"Come in."

I entered.

Hara.

Marek.

Chair.

Not dish.

"Uncle. You made excellent time."

"My stomach kept the appointment."

Laugh.

Small.

Mine.

I sat.

The chair worked.

The crutches stayed clear.

I listened.

That was the job.

For three minutes I did it correctly.

Marek lied about bread.

Venn brought the spoon.

The bowl.

The cabbage leaf.

"Is that the first course?"

"No. It is evidence."

I waited for Hara.

She took the leaf.

"Evidence of what?"

"That there was once a cabbage."

Good laugh.

Hara: "You have been explaining since breakfast. It has not produced lunch."

Bigger.

Hers.

Correct.

Then the covered dish.

I knew the beat.

The audience did not.

Venn brought it.

Set it down.

I smelled nothing.

My line:

"That smells subtle."

A laugh began.

And I looked at the lid.

Not long.

Not dramatically.

Just early.

The audience saw it.

I knew because the laugh shifted.

A few people laughed again before Hara spoke.

They were ahead.

Only a little.

Enough.

Hara said, "Family recipe."

The second laugh was smaller than rehearsal had promised.

My stomach dropped.

Not Greg's stomach.

The uncle's remained hungry.

I kept the scene.

Venn stepped in.

"There is one problem."

Hara looked at him.

This time the napkin stayed where it belonged.

"The dish is empty."

The audience laughed.

Still.

Less surprise than it should have had.

Marek stood too quickly.

"I should check the street."

Hara caught his sleeve.

"No."

That got them back.

Good.

The scene moved.

Hara questioned him.

He folded.

Venn stood beside the sideboard with the expression of a man who had reported a fire and declined to apologize for heat.

The audience liked him.

Near the end, Hara lifted the lid.

Empty.

She looked inside longer than she had in rehearsal.

Then looked at me.

"My apologies."

My last line.

"I have had lighter suppers."

Laugh.

Fine.

Then Marek offered to fetch food.

Hara asked, "With what money?"

He sat.

The audience hit hard.

That was hers.

The play ended.

Backstage, I came off knowing exactly what I had done.

Teren waited until the applause had started before speaking.

"You looked."

"Yes."

"Before the cook."

"Yes."

He nodded.

That was almost worse than a lecture.

"I knew it was empty."

"The uncle didn't."

"I know."

"Good."

I stared at him.

He walked away.

Of course.

I had looked at a prop too early in a comedy.

That was enough failure for one profession.

After curtain, work resumed.

Nessa stacked plates.

Venn carried the empty dish back to the property table.

Marek tried to steal the cabbage leaf.

Nessa said his name.

He put it down.

Hara came off with the strongest applause still following her.

I said, "You recovered the lid."

She looked at me.

"It was still empty."

"Audience knew."

"Some did."

"Because me."

"Some."

She removed one cuff pin from her costume.

"Then don't look tomorrow."

I stopped.

"Tomorrow?"

Hara shrugged.

"I don't schedule you."

Right.

Rinna appeared with coins.

One copper for me.

Normal performance day.

I took it.

"Tomorrow?"

"Not paired yet."

There it was.

Maybe.

Not a promise.

"Uncle?"

"Maybe."

"Same piece?"

"Maybe."

"Healthy certainty."

Rinna had already moved to Venn.

She paid him too.

Good.

The company existed in multiple directions.

I helped Jori move my chair back after the audience cleared.

The chalk mark on the rear leg came up with the cloth.

The floor kept a faint white line.

Jori rubbed it once with his boot.

Mostly gone.

The covered dish returned to the sideboard.

Empty.

Still not a mystery.

Outside, evening had cooled the street.

My right palm was ordinary.

Shoulders used but fine.

Right leg mildly tired from standing between scenes.

Residual limb comfortable.

Left tip normal.

Both grips normal.

No new problem.

I bought a small piece of cheese on the way home.

I spent ordinary money on cheese.

The debt remained elsewhere.

At the apartment, Lyssa was already there.

No customer.

No new dark cloth.

The brown cloth remained folded with three pins.

My damaged shirt remained at three loose threads.

She saw the coat pages before the cheese.

"Theatre?"

"Uncle."

She looked at me.

"Whose?"

"Exactly."

I put the cheese on the table.

She cut bread.

I told her the play was about an empty supper.

She looked at the cheese.

"Did you eat?"

"Yes."

"Good."

I waited.

"That is the wrong response."

"You ate."

"I performed hunger."

"Hard."

"Thank you."

She ate cheese.

After a while I said, "I looked at the empty dish too early."

Lyssa looked at me.

"You knew."

"Yes."

"Then don't."

I stared.

"Teren made me work much harder for that answer."

"Smart."

"Hostile."

She ate cheese.

I laughed.

The brown cloth remained where it was.

The blue streak remained on my better shirt.

The onions remained onions.

No Hessa note arrived.

No Vale work appeared.

No brown coat came to the door.

Tomorrow The Guest Pot might exist for me.

Or not.

Rinna had said maybe.

I had become old enough in Carrow to know what maybe was worth.

I ate the cheese before it became evidence.
------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 248

## THE NAME-TAKER

I met Vale's runner halfway down the stairs.

He was coming up fast enough that we nearly invented a new use for my crutches.

He stopped two steps below me, put one hand on the rail, and said, "Antonius."

I looked past him toward the bottom landing.

"Is he down there?"

"No."

"Good. That would have been strange."

The runner had already decided not to participate.

"Work," he said. "If you want it. Now."
